The Ravel book

What Ravel is for

Ravel lets a program use an LLM in Claude Code or Codex as one step of its own work. This chapter opens with one such step, a code review. It follows that review from the request to the result. Then it explains which parts of the review apply to every use of Ravel.

You do not run anything in this chapter. Each code block is a fragment, a part of the review or of a real run, and its label says what it omits. Chapter 3, “Run the first session”, gives the first session that you run. It names the directory, each file and each command.

Follow one code review from request to result

An orchestrator is a program that runs LLM work as steps of its own work. Suppose that an orchestrator wants an LLM to review the last commit of a project. The commit changes two files, src/parse.rs and tests/parse.rs. The orchestrator knows these files, because it chose the work. It also knows what it will do after the review.

First, the orchestrator writes a prompt. The prompt is the text that tells the LLM what to do. This prompt also tells the LLM how to report the outcome of the review.

Fragment: the whole prompt file review-prompt.txt of the review example. This block omits the code of the orchestrator that writes the file. Ravel keeps the text unchanged in request.originalPrompt and does not interpret it.

Review the changes to src/parse.rs and tests/parse.rs in the last commit.
Check that the code is correct and that the tests cover each change.

When the review is complete, end this session with one command:
- If you found no problem, run: ravel end-session approved
- If you found a problem, write your findings to review-notes.md.
  Then run: ravel end-session changes-needed

The prompt has four parts. Each part has a different purpose:

Part of the promptPurpose
The work: review two named filesTells the LLM what to do
The two permitted results, approved and changes-neededGives the only values that the orchestrator expects
The condition for each resultTells the LLM which value to send
The command ravel end-session RESULTTells the LLM how to end the session and send the value

Then the orchestrator runs Ravel as a child process. --kind review names the sort of work. The prompt file is the prompt.

Fragment: the request of the review example. This block omits the code of the orchestrator that runs the command.

ravel start-session --kind review --prompt-file review-prompt.txt

Do not type this command now. It needs an installed Ravel, the file review-prompt.txt in the current directory and a configuration script with a rule for the kind review. Chapter 3 installs Ravel. Then it gives you the files and the command of a simpler first session.

Ravel gives the kind and the prompt to the owner’s configuration. The owner is the person who controls Ravel’s configuration for an account. The configuration chooses a harness for each kind. A harness is the program in which an LLM works on a prompt, such as Claude Code. In this example, the configuration chooses Claude Code for the kind review.

What happens next? The table follows the review in order. In this run, the LLM finds a problem.

OrderWho actsWhat happens in the review
1The orchestratorRuns ravel start-session with the kind review and the prompt file
2The owner’s configurationChooses Claude Code and its arguments for the kind review
3RavelLaunches Claude Code with the prompt and supervises it
4The LLM in Claude CodeReviews the two files, finds a problem and writes review-notes.md
5The LLM in Claude CodeRuns ravel end-session changes-needed
6RavelStops Claude Code and the other processes that remain in its process group, the set of processes that Ravel can signal together. Then Ravel completes cleanup.
7RavelWrites changes-needed on standard output. ravel start-session exits with status 0.

The sequence connects the review request to the orchestrator’s next action.

Figure: The orchestrator acts on the result only after Ravel completes cleanup.

Open the review sequence at full size.

Here is a real run of this request, with the checkout build on macOS. Its configuration script was the short script in “The configuration script chooses a program”, below, at the default path of a private home directory. That script selects a fake agent in place of Claude Code. Thus, the routing notice names the provider local and the model fake.

The fake agent wrote review-notes.md and sent changes-needed, as the LLM did in step 5. The table omits the environment of the run and two cleanup reports on standard error.

Output of the runValueWho reads it
Standard outputchanges-needed, with no newlineThe orchestrator, as the result
Standard errorravel: starting provider local, model fake, effort low for kind "review"The person or the log that watches the session
Exit status0The orchestrator, to know that the session succeeded

The orchestrator now acts on the result. Its own code decides what each value means:

Outcome of ravel start-sessionWhat the orchestrator does next
Status 0 and the result approvedMarks the review as complete and moves to its next task
Status 0 and the result changes-neededReads review-notes.md, then requests a session of the kind fix with the notes in its prompt
Status 0 and another valueTreats the review as failed, because its prompt permitted only two values
A status other than 0Reads standard error, then decides whether to request the review again. It does not use standard output as a result.

Ravel does not know what approved and changes-needed mean. These two values are the orchestrator’s convention, which its prompt states. Ravel returns the value that the LLM sent, unchanged, after it stops Claude Code and completes cleanup. The orchestrator gives the value its meaning.

What the review shows about every session

The review had three participants with different responsibilities. Every use of Ravel has the same three. The table connects each general role to its part in the review.

ParticipantIn the reviewIn general
CallerThe orchestrator wrote the prompt and acted on changes-neededSupplies the kind and the prompt. The prompt gives the permitted results and the end-session instruction. The caller interprets the result.
OwnerConfigured Claude Code for the kind reviewWrites the configuration that chooses a program for each request
RavelLaunched Claude Code, stopped it and returned changes-neededRuns the chosen program, supervises it and returns the result after cleanup

The caller is the person or program that runs ravel start-session. The orchestrator is one caller, and you are the caller when you type the command yourself. The owner and the caller can be the same person, but their responsibilities stay separate. One run of ravel start-session, from the request to the result, is a session.

Why use a harness, and not a direct call to a model? A harness such as Claude Code or Codex can run under the subscription of its user. Thus, your program can use that subscription for LLM work instead of paid API access. The cost can be much lower for work that the subscription covers. The Claude Code documentation and Codex documentation explain their access options and link to their plans and terms. This paragraph agrees with those pages as they were on 18 October 2026.

Why does the prompt tell the LLM to run ravel end-session? An interactive harness waits for more input when the LLM completes its task. It does not exit by itself. Thus, the orchestrator cannot wait for the harness to exit. ravel end-session RESULT gives the LLM a way to say that the work is complete.

The caller chooses the permitted values and the condition for each value. Ravel stops the harness and, after cleanup, returns the value to the caller.

From a request to a result

This section follows the same review through Ravel in more detail. It names each actor in the order in which that actor acts. Each subsection below explains one row of this table, with a real example.

OrderWho actsWhat happens
1The callerRuns ravel start-session with a kind and a prompt
2The configuration scriptSelects a program and its arguments, or refuses the request
3RavelWrites the text routing notice, which tells the caller what the configuration script chose. With --json, the notice follows the supervision attempt.
4RavelLaunches the selected program and supervises it
5The selected programSends a result with ravel end-session
6RavelStops the program, completes cleanup and returns the result to the caller

The caller asks for a session

The caller runs ravel start-session with a prompt and a kind. The prompt is the text that tells the LLM what to do. A kind is a short token that names the sort of work that the caller wants. The caller gives the prompt with --prompt TEXT or, as in the review, with --prompt-file PATH.

In the review example, the caller uses review as the kind. The caller and the owner’s configuration agree on that token. Ravel checks only that the kind is not empty. It has no built-in review category and gives the kind no meaning.

The caller can also use the kind to extend or modify the prompt. For example, if the harness supports skills, the caller can add a line to the prompt for the kind review. That line tells the LLM which skill to load. A skill is a set of instructions for a particular task. This use of the kind is a caller convention.

Ravel keeps the caller’s prompt unchanged in request.originalPrompt. With skill delivery enabled, request.prompt holds discovery instructions followed by the caller’s text in a separate block. The script passes that prepared prompt to the harness. Ravel adds no task meaning or instruction that ends the session. Chapter 6 explains skill delivery.

The configuration script chooses a program

The owner’s configuration has two parts. The configuration script is a file of Rhai code that selects a program for each request. The settings are values in a JSON file, such as limits and the harnesses that the script can run. The table gives the default path of each part:

Part of the configurationDefault pathWhat it holds
Configuration script$HOME/.config/ravel/config.rhaiRhai code that selects a program or refuses the request. --config PATH names another script for one session.
Settings$HOME/.config/ravel/settings.jsonThe deadline, limits, environment grants, harnesses, budgets and skill sources. The script cannot change them.

Ravel gives the request to the configuration script. Then the script selects a program and its arguments, or it refuses to launch a program.

What is Rhai? Rhai is a small computer language for programs that are written in Rust. A Rust program can contain the Rhai engine and run Rhai code in its own process. Ravel does this. Thus, the ravel executable already contains everything that runs your configuration script, and you install no separate language runtime.

Rhai’s feature list names these characteristics. Each one is important for a configuration script:

  • A familiar syntax. Rhai looks like a mix of JavaScript and Rust. It has variables, functions, if, loops, arrays and maps.
  • Dynamic types. You do not declare a type. Each value has its type while the script runs.
  • A sandbox. Rhai code changes things outside the script only through functions that the host program gives it. In Ravel, the configuration script reads files and runs harnesses only through ravel:: functions. The book calls these functions host capabilities.
  • Protection against runaway scripts. Rhai can stop a script that runs too long, calls functions too deeply or makes data that is too large. Ravel sets these limits, and one deadline covers each evaluation.
  • Modules. A configuration script can import other Rhai files.

Here is a short configuration script for the review example. The book’s examples must run without a real harness. Thus, this script selects fake-agent, a small program that stands in for Claude Code. It refuses every kind except review.

Fragment: a short configuration script for the review example. This block omits the program fake-agent and the command that runs the script. You read this script here, and you save nothing. Chapter 3 runs a script of this sort with a fake agent.

export const SCHEMA_VERSION = 4;
export const VERSION = "chapter-1";

fn select(request, context) {
    if request.kind == "review" {
        return #{
            status: "selected",
            program: "fake-agent",
            args: ["You are a careful code reviewer. " + request.prompt],
            provider: "local",
            model: "fake",
            effort: "low",
            reason: "Reviews go to the fake agent.",
        };
    }
    #{
        status: "refused",
        code: "unknown_kind",
        message: "This configuration script has no rule for the kind " + request.kind + ".",
        remedy: "Use the kind review.",
    }
}

Read the configuration script from the top. The table says what each part does:

Part of the scriptWhat it does
export const SCHEMA_VERSION = 4;Names the version of the interface between Ravel and the script that this file uses
export const VERSION = "chapter-1";Names this revision of the owner’s script
fn select(request, context)Defines the function that Ravel calls. request holds the kind, the prompt and other data of the request. context holds optional selection context, which is data for the choice.
if request.kind == "review"Applies the first rule only to the kind review
#{ ... }Makes a map, which is a set of named fields
returnEnds the function with the map that follows it
"You are a careful code reviewer. " + request.promptPuts an instruction before the caller’s prompt
The last mapIs the value of the function when no return ran. This map is a refusal.

How does Ravel run the script? The Ravel CLI, the ravel command-line program, does not run it in its own process. It launches a second copy of its own executable as a child process. That child is the Rhai worker. The worker evaluates the configuration script and sends the selection or the refusal to its parent.

Why a separate process? The parent stays in control while the script runs:

  • The deadline. Each evaluation has a deadline. When it expires, the parent ends evaluation and can signal the worker process. Rhai cannot interrupt some of its own functions, such as sleep. A kernel stall can delay the worker’s exit.
  • Requests from the script. The worker only computes. When the script reads a file or runs a harness, the worker asks the parent. The parent does the work and sends the data back.
  • Isolation of a failure. If the script exhausts the memory of the worker or overflows its stack, only the worker stops. The parent continues, reports the failure to the caller and launches no program.

The worker does not run at the same time as the selected program. Ravel waits until the worker exits and collects its exit status. Only then does Ravel launch the program that the script selected.

Do you want to learn more Rhai? Rhai has its own documentation. Chapter 4 teaches the Rhai that a configuration script needs, so you do not need these pages for this book:

  • The Rhai Book is the manual. On 27 October 2026, it documented Rhai 1.27.0, the version that Ravel contains.
  • The language reference gives the syntax in a short form.
  • The online playground runs Rhai code in your web browser. It has no ravel:: functions and none of Ravel’s limits. Thus, a script that calls a host capability does not run there.

The selection and the routing notice

What does the configuration script give back to Ravel? It gives back a selection or a refusal. A selection is the map that select returns when it chooses a program. Its status field is "selected". A refusal is a map with status: "refused".

After a refusal, Ravel launches no final managed process and returns no result to the caller. Evaluation harness runs can occur before the refusal.

The selection of the review example has these fields:

FieldValue in the exampleWho uses it, and how
programfake-agentRavel finds this program and runs it
argsOne string: the instruction, then the caller’s promptRavel gives these arguments to the program, without a shell
providerlocalRavel writes it in the routing notice
modelfakeRavel writes it in the routing notice
effortlowRavel writes it in the routing notice
reasonReviews go to the fake agent.Ravel writes it in the JSON form of the routing notice

program and args are the command that Ravel runs. The other fields are labels. A label describes the choice of the configuration script, but it does not configure the harness. For example, a script that selects Claude Code puts the model option of Claude Code in args. The model label only tells the caller which model the script chose. Ravel does not check that the labels agree with the arguments.

Why does the caller need these labels? The caller usually wants to know what the configuration script chose, but the caller does not read the owner’s script. Ravel tells the caller in the routing notice:

  • Who writes it: the Ravel CLI, the ravel command-line program.
  • When: in text form, after selection and before the launch. In JSON form, after the supervision attempt.
  • Where: one line on the caller’s standard error. Standard output carries only the result.
  • Who reads it: the caller, or the person who watches the terminal.
  • Why: this line is the way in which Ravel tells the caller which harness, model and effort the configuration script chose.

Fragment: the routing notice of the review example, as Ravel writes it on standard error. This block omits the other lines on standard error.

ravel: starting provider local, model fake, effort low for kind "review"

The selection separates the command that Ravel launches from the labels that the caller reads.

Figure: The review selection reaches the program and the caller by separate paths.

Open the selection and routing notice at full size.

With --json, Ravel writes a JSON routing notice in place of that line. The JSON document gives more detail, such as the reason and the program file that Ravel found. Ravel writes this document after the supervision attempt, not before the launch.

Fragment: the selection member of the JSON routing notice of the same request, run again with --json. This block omits the other members of the document.

{"selection":{"effort":"low","model":"fake","provider":"local","reason":"Reviews go to the fake agent."}}

Chapter 5, “Read notices separately from results”, teaches both forms of the routing notice.

An LLM can help the configuration script choose

You may want an LLM to choose the best harness, model, effort and prompt additions for a kind and a prompt. You can do this with a harness run in your configuration script. A harness run is one run of a program from the harnesses entry of your settings. The script starts it with the host capability ravel::run_harness. The harness uses the owner’s sign-in, and Ravel holds no model credential.

The configuration script reads the answer of the harness as data. Then it builds its selection from that answer. A harness run does not complete the session. Its result goes back to the script, not to the caller. Ravel supervises each harness run within the owner’s evaluation budget, which limits the work of one evaluation of the configuration script. Chapter 4 gives the recipes for Claude Code and Codex.

Ravel runs the program, and the program sends a result

During the session, Ravel supervises the program that the configuration script selected. This program is the managed process of the session. The harness runs the LLM session that works on the prompt. That LLM session is not the Ravel session. Ravel supervises the harness process, not the conversation of the LLM in that process.

The contract with the selected program is loose. The configuration script selects a program, Ravel runs it, and a program in the session sends one value with end-session. Ravel needs nothing more from the program. The selected program does not have to use an LLM or understand a prompt.

The table separates what Ravel needs from the harness use that this book teaches. The runnable examples use ordinary programs as fake agents.

Part of a sessionWhat Ravel needsWhat this book expects from a harness
Selected commandThe configuration script selects a program and its argumentsThe program runs an LLM session
PromptThe caller supplies --prompt or --prompt-file. Ravel keeps that text unchanged in request.originalPrompt. The script decides whether its program receives request.prompt, which also holds discovery instructions when skill delivery is enabled.The script passes the prompt to the harness
ResultA program in the session sends one value with end-sessionThe LLM follows the caller’s instructions and tells the harness to run that command

The caller’s prompt tells the LLM to run ravel end-session RESULT when the task is complete. RESULT is a placeholder for the text to send. As in the review, the prompt gives the permitted values and tells the LLM when to use each value.

A process group is a set of processes that the operating system can signal together. Ravel puts the selected program in a new group. Its children stay in that group unless they leave it.

In the review, the LLM found a problem. It ran ravel end-session changes-needed, as the prompt told it. The program that Ravel launched, usually a harness that runs an LLM, runs that command. Ravel then stops that program and the rest of its process group. After cleanup, changes-needed becomes the result of ravel start-session.

Ravel writes changes-needed unchanged on the caller’s standard output. The orchestrator interprets changes-needed as a request to read the notes and to request a fix session. Ravel gives changes-needed no meaning and starts no next task. The book calls this text an opaque result, because Ravel does not interpret it.

How end-session finds its session

How does ravel end-session know which session to end? It takes no session ID and no target argument. It finds the session through an environment variable, RAVEL_EXIT_FILE.

Before the launch, Ravel makes a result channel for the session. The result channel is a private temporary directory, with a path for one result file in it. Ravel puts that path in the RAVEL_EXIT_FILE environment variable of the program that it launches. The book calls this path the session-result context.

How does the path reach end-session? When a process starts a child, the child gets a copy of the environment of its parent, unless the parent changes it. A harness runs each command that the LLM requests in a child process, for example a shell. That shell starts ravel end-session. If each parent leaves RAVEL_EXIT_FILE unchanged, each child inherits the same path. Thus, ravel end-session finds the path in its own environment and writes the result to that file.

ProcessWhich process starts itIts RAVEL_EXIT_FILE
ravel start-sessionThe callerNone, unless the caller itself runs in a session
The harness, which is the managed processRavelA new path that Ravel makes for this session
A shell for a command of the LLMThe harnessThe same path, inherited from the harness
ravel end-session changes-neededThat shellThe same path, inherited from the shell. The command writes the result there.

Each child inherits its parent’s path unless its parent changes it. Ravel sets a new path for the inner harness.

Figure: The nested run gives each session’s children its own result path.

Open the inherited result paths at full size.

Here is a real run. A fake agent printed its own RAVEL_EXIT_FILE. Then it was the caller of a second, inner session. The inner fake agent printed its value, and the outer fake agent printed its value again.

Fragment: standard error of a real nested session on macOS with TMPDIR=/tmp. This block omits the configuration script, the two fake agents and the cleanup reports. The random parts of each path change in each run. macOS gives /tmp the real path /private/tmp.

ravel: starting provider local, model fake, effort none for kind "outer"
outer-agent: RAVEL_EXIT_FILE=/private/tmp/ravel-session-mlcN86/signal-c7d5f2eed4198eb768996f7b4868bbcc
ravel: starting provider local, model fake, effort none for kind "inner"
inner-agent: RAVEL_EXIT_FILE=/private/tmp/ravel-session-QpztGn/signal-b643cbee529e5887c7d2a3d0496210c9
outer-agent: the inner session returned inner-done
outer-agent: RAVEL_EXIT_FILE=/private/tmp/ravel-session-mlcN86/signal-c7d5f2eed4198eb768996f7b4868bbcc

Ravel made a directory under TMPDIR for each session. The inner session got a new path, but only the inner fake agent received it. The outer fake agent kept its own path. Thus, each end-session goes to the session that encloses it. A nested session gives only its own child a new path.

The Rhai worker never receives RAVEL_EXIT_FILE. The Rhai worker is the second copy of the ravel executable that evaluates the configuration script. A harness run that the script starts gets a path of its own, so its result goes back to the script. What if end-session finds no path? Then it stops with status 1 and writes a line on standard error.

Fragment: standard error of ravel end-session changes-needed in a shell outside any session. This block omits the command and its status.

ravel: end-session: no enclosing Ravel session (RAVEL_EXIT_FILE is unset or empty)

Ravel stops the program and returns the result

When the program sends the result, it requests the end of the session. This request does not prove that cleanup succeeded. A process group is an operating-system unit that contains the managed process and the descendants that remain in its group. Ravel stops the harness and each other process that remains in that group. Then it completes the cleanup of the group.

To reap a child process, its parent collects the exit status of that child. Before Ravel returns a successful result, it confirms that the managed process ended and that Ravel reaped it. Ravel also confirms that the process group is gone and that it removed the temporary files of the session. Chapter 3, “A complete session, end to end”, explains these mechanics at the point where a session needs them.

The table follows the action of the LLM to the result that the caller receives. The caller receives a successful result only after Ravel stops the managed process and its process group and completes the cleanup.

Step after the LLM completes its taskWho acts and what happens
SendThe program that Ravel launched, usually a harness that runs an LLM, runs ravel end-session RESULT. This command writes the result to the path in RAVEL_EXIT_FILE and requests the end of the session.
StopRavel stops that program and all other processes that still run in its process group.
Confirm cleanupRavel confirms that it reaped the managed process, that the group is gone and that it removed the temporary files of the session.
ReturnRavel writes the result unchanged on standard output. It becomes the result of ravel start-session, and the caller decides its next step.

The figure shows Ravel between an orchestrator that requests work and a harness that runs an LLM session. The owner chooses the harness. Ravel supervises the harness until cleanup lets the result return. Other callers and selected programs use the same boundary.

The C4 system-context view shows Ravel as one system. These shapes show responsibilities, not security boundaries.

Open the system-context figure at full size.

After a successful session, Ravel writes the result unchanged to standard output. Ravel does not interpret the result and adds no newline. Ravel redirects the standard output of the managed process to standard error. Thus, the caller can keep the output of the managed process and the diagnostics of Ravel separate from the result stream.

Check whether start-session succeeded before you interpret that stream. A failure during the write of the final result can leave only the start of the result on standard output. Thus, the output alone does not prove success.

Some outcomes return no result to the caller:

  • A refusal means that Ravel launches no final managed process. Evaluation harness runs can occur before the configuration script refuses.
  • If the managed process exits and no program sent a result, the session has no result and fails. This is also true when the exit status is zero.
  • Cancellation of the session suppresses the result, even if a program sent the result before the cancellation.
  • A supervision failure or a cleanup failure also prevents success.

But a session with a sent result can succeed after cleanup, even if the managed process then exits with a nonzero status. The result is the value that the caller’s instructions to end the session asked for. The exit status tells how the process ended. The work is what the program changed. None of these three outputs can replace another.

The table keeps the three outputs separate. Thus, you do not confuse an exit status or a changed file with the result that the caller requested.

What the session producesWhat it tells the callerWhat it does not prove
Opaque resultThe value sent with end-session, returned after successful supervision and cleanup That the requested work is correct
Managed process’s exit statusHow that process endedThat it sent a result or that the session succeeded
Work, such as changed filesWhat the selected program changed, such as review-notes.mdThat it sent a result or that the cleanup completed

In short, the caller asks with a kind and a prompt. The prompt gives the permitted results and the end-session instruction. The owner’s Rhai configuration script selects a program, and the routing notice tells the caller what it chose. Ravel launches the program and gives it a path in RAVEL_EXIT_FILE. The program sends a result to that path with ravel end-session. Ravel stops the program, completes cleanup and then returns the result on standard output.

Decide whether Ravel fits

Ravel fits when your program needs to use an LLM session in an interactive harness as a step of its own work. Your program can let the owner’s configuration choose the harness. In each harness that the configuration script selects, the LLM uses the same command, end-session, to send the result. Ravel supervises the session and returns its explicit result after cleanup. Your program still owns the task and the decisions after the session ends.

Use this table to find which part of your need Ravel meets. The other parts need a caller, a service or a selected program.

If you need…Ravel’s partWhat stays outside Ravel
Different owners to choose different harnesses for the same LLM taskEvaluate the configuration script of each owner and run the command that it selectsThe caller’s task meaning and the owner’s decisions in the configuration
An interactive harness to finish as a step of your programAccept the result that end-session sends, stop the group members that remain and return the result after successful cleanupThe caller’s instructions to end the session, the interpretation of the result and all further work
Task queues, schedules, retries or durable historyRun a session when a caller invokes RavelA caller or service that stores and schedules work
Model access, credentials, usage measurement or rules for model selectionSupervise evaluation harness runs and the command that the configuration script selectsThe harness’s model integration, sign-in and usage reports, and the owner’s selection rules
A host that continues to do other work after a launchThe Ravel CLI supervises one session and then exitsA separate host that continues to run after each launch, which Ravel does not provide

The Ravel CLI, the ravel command-line program, is a launcher CLI. One invocation supervises one managed launch, reports its result or failure, and then exits. A persistent runner is a host that continues to run after a launch is complete. Ravel does not provide one. A program that exits after one session does not give what such a host needs.

Do you need a record of past sessions? Ravel keeps none. It has no persistent session store and no history lookup. Its result channel, the private temporary directory that receives the result, exists for one session only. If a caller needs a permanent record, that caller saves it.

The configuration script has no direct filesystem, network or process access. It asks Ravel to read files or run a harness from the settings through host capabilities, the ravel:: functions that Ravel performs for the script. That harness can use files and network services within its own permissions.

Grove, a task-tree driver, is one illustrative caller. It is an orchestrator that runs LLM sessions in harnesses as steps of the work on its task tree. It supplies its own task kinds and interprets its own result values. These conventions belong to Grove. Another caller can use different kinds and results without a Ravel adapter.

This chapter writes INT, TERM, HUP and KILL without the SIG prefix. Other signal names keep that prefix. Code and diagnostic text keep their literal names.

The supervision guarantee has limits. This table shows the consequence of each limit for the caller.

ConditionLimit of the guarantee
A descendant leaves the process group of the managed processGroup cleanup does not reach it. The session can succeed while that descendant continues to run.
An evaluation harness run starts descendants that leave its process groupCleanup of that run does not reach those descendants. The Rhai worker, the separate process that evaluates the configuration script, itself starts no program.
The Ravel CLI stops abruptly, for example because of KILLThe managed process or temporary files can remain. The Ravel CLI cannot catch KILL.
Other code runs as the same user as RavelThat code can tamper with Ravel’s files, RAVEL_EXIT_FILE included. The separation of sessions is not a security check against that code.

Chapter 2 explains why the separation of these roles is useful within these limits.

Choose a route through the book

The book assumes everyday use of shell, Git and JSON, the ability to read Rust, and general operating-system knowledge. It teaches the domain of Ravel and the job-control details that Ravel needs. This chapter showed a short Rhai configuration script. Chapter 4 teaches the Rhai syntax and host capabilities that configuration scripts need.

Choose the route that serves your task. The table gives the chapter order and the chapters for further detail.

Your taskRead in this orderFurther detail
Call Ravel from your own toolChapters 1–3, then chapter 5, “Run sessions”Chapter 6, “Reference”, for lookup
Write a configuration scriptChapters 1–3, then chapter 4, “Write a configuration script”Chapter 6, “Reference”, for lookup
Change RavelChapters 1–3, then chapter 7, “The parts of Ravel”, 8, “Follow a session through the code”, and 10, “Develop Ravel”Chapter 9, “Jobs, signals and the terminal”, and chapter 11, “Deliver Ravel”
Reuse Ravel from another programRavel supports no separate component. Invoke the Ravel CLI as a child process. Chapters 2, 3 and 5 teach that interface.Chapters 7 and 9 explain the private runner and its limits

Why can another program not use Ravel’s parts as a library? Ravel’s runner, keyed-launch, is the Rust code that launches and supervises the managed process. It is a private package of the Ravel CLI. The runner can change process-wide signal state, and its return does not restore that state. Thus, a program that needs supervised harness sessions invokes the Ravel CLI as a child process. The Ravel CLI supervises one session and then exits.

The learning route is to read the chapters in order. The first practical session, in chapter 3, uses a small configuration script and a fake agent. Its directory is ~/ravel-first-session, and the chapter gives each file and command.

A fake agent is an ordinary program that sends a result with end-session. It takes the place of a harness that runs an LLM session. Thus, you can learn the protocol without a model service or a personal agent account. The installation and the configuration of real agents are outside the scope of the book.

See what we tested and what remains unchecked

Which platforms did the project check, and what does that evidence show? This section answers that question. It also says which checks of the book remain open.

Recorded measurements checked how Ravel starts a session and completes its cleanup on two platforms. One platform is aarch64 macOS with Rust 1.99. The other platform is native aarch64 GNU/Linux with Rust 1.85.0, which was the declared minimum at that time. Each measurement applies only to the platform, kernel, C library and compiler that it records.

The project built the x86_64 GNU/Linux target, linted it at Rust 1.85.0 and ran it only under QEMU user-mode emulation. This emulation translates x86-64 programs to run on the kernel of the host. Thus, no x86-64 kernel takes part. No x86-64 kernel is qualified.

These facts are limits of the evidence. The three targets do not have the same evidence. Chapter 11 explains the limits of delivery and of the platforms in detail.

The table shows why a built or emulated target does not have the same evidence as a measured native target.

TargetRecorded executionCompilerLimit
aarch64 macOSNative measurementsRust 1.99Only the recorded platform and compiler
aarch64 GNU/LinuxNative measurementsRust 1.85.0, the earlier declared minimumOnly the recorded kernel, C library and compiler
x86_64 GNU/LinuxBuilt, linted and run under QEMU user-mode emulationRust 1.85.0Uses the kernel of the host. No x86-64 kernel is qualified.

The reader checkpoints of the book use the owner as a labelled stand-in for a developer new to Ravel. These reads can show the owner’s findings, for example errors in the book. They cannot show whether a new developer can use the book.

You can build and examine the local book. GitHub shows the Markdown of a pushed branch, and nobody checked that view of this copy. The book of Ravel 1.1.0 is on GitHub Pages. The book of a later version reaches GitHub Pages only with its release. Thus, that copy stays unchecked until then.

Who does what in a Ravel session

Remember the code review of chapter 1. An orchestrator asked for a review of two files. Its prompt permitted two results, approved and changes-needed. The owner’s configuration chose Claude Code for the kind review. Ravel launched Claude Code, and the LLM sent changes-needed with ravel end-session.

Ravel stopped Claude Code, completed cleanup and returned changes-needed. Then the orchestrator read the notes and requested a fix session.

Three different participants made three different decisions in that review:

Decision in the reviewWho made itThe general rule
Review two files, and permit approved or changes-neededThe orchestrator, which is the callerThe caller chooses the work, the prompt and the permitted results
Use Claude Code for the kind reviewThe owner, through the configuration scriptThe owner chooses the program for each request
changes-needed is the result, after Claude Code stopped and cleanup completedRavelRavel decides whether the session, one run of ravel start-session, completed successfully

If you know who makes each decision, you can change a rule in the configuration script, and the caller does not change. You can also interpret the outcome of a session. You do not confuse a response from the configuration script with a completed session. This chapter names each participant and each term that the book uses for it.

You do not run anything in this chapter. Each code block is a fragment of the review, and its label says what it omits. Chapter 3 gives the first session that you run.

Caller and owner

The caller is the person or program that invokes Ravel and interprets the result that Ravel returns. An orchestrator is one such caller. An orchestrator is a program that runs LLM sessions in harnesses as steps of its own work. A harness is an agent program, such as Claude Code or Codex, in which an LLM works on a prompt. The caller supplies the prompt, with the instructions to end the session. The caller also decides the next step after the session.

A session is one ravel start-session invocation. It lasts from the request until Ravel reports the result or another outcome. A Ravel session is not a POSIX session that setsid creates. It is also not a login session or the LLM session in a harness. Ravel supervises the harness process, not the conversation of the LLM.

The caller supplies a kind. A kind is an open token that names the sort of session that the caller wants. Ravel checks that the token is not empty and gives it no meaning. The configuration script of the owner interprets it. For example, a caller can request review. But Ravel has no registry of task kinds and does not know what a review includes.

The owner is the person who controls the configuration of Ravel for the account. The configuration has two parts:

PartDefault pathWhat it does
Configuration script$HOME/.config/ravel/config.rhaiRhai code that selects a program and its arguments, or refuses the request
Settings$HOME/.config/ravel/settings.jsonValues that the script cannot change, such as the deadline, limits, environment grants, harnesses and budgets

The caller and the owner can be the same person, but their roles stay different. The choice of the work does not set the rules that select a command. The table shows which decisions stay separate, even when one person has both roles.

RoleDecidesLeaves to the other role
CallerThe requested work and the meaning of the resultWhich command the configuration script selects
OwnerThe configuration script that selects a command or refuses a request, and the settingsThe caller’s task and next step

What is Rhai? Rhai is a small computer language for programs that are written in Rust. Ravel contains the Rhai engine, so your configuration script runs in Ravel’s own binary, and you install no separate language runtime. Rhai looks like a mix of JavaScript and Rust, and you do not declare types. A Rhai script changes nothing outside itself, except through functions that Ravel gives it. Ravel also limits how long a script runs and how much data it makes.

Chapter 1 shows a short configuration script and links to the Rhai Book, Rhai’s own manual. Chapter 4 teaches the Rhai that a configuration script needs.

By default, Ravel uses the account’s configuration script, $HOME/.config/ravel/config.rhai. --config PATH names a different script. Ravel resolves that path relative to the caller’s working directory. Ravel does not search the caller’s repository for a configuration script.

What does the configuration script give back? When it chooses a program, it returns a selection. A selection is the map that the script’s select function returns with status: "selected". It names a program and its arguments. It also has the labels provider, model, effort and reason, which describe the choice.

The provider is the owner’s label for the model’s origin. Ravel reports it unchanged and never connects to it. The labels do not configure the program. The configuration script puts the actual options, such as a model option, in the arguments. Ravel runs that command without shell expansion or word splitting. A shell runs only if the script selects one.

Then why does a selection have labels? They tell the caller what the configuration script chose. After selection and before the launch, the Ravel CLI writes the text routing notice as one line on the caller’s standard error. The caller, or the person who watches the terminal, reads the choice there. Standard output stays free for the result. For example, a script that returns provider: "local", model: "fake" and effort: "low" for the kind review gives the routing notice below.

Fragment: the routing notice of that selection. This block omits the other lines on standard error.

ravel: starting provider local, model fake, effort low for kind "review"

With --json, a JSON routing notice replaces that line, after the supervision attempt. Chapter 5 teaches both forms.

The system-context figure in chapter 1 shows these roles around Ravel. An orchestrator can choose the work, and the owner chooses the harness. Ravel supervises that harness as the managed process, the program that the configuration script selected for the session.

Evaluation and supervision

The Ravel CLI is the ravel command-line program, written in Rust. During start-session, it keeps the caller’s environment, terminal and standard output. It evaluates the request and supervises the selected program. Its runner is the Rust library code that launches and supervises the managed process. The runner runs in the process of the Ravel CLI. It is not a separate service.

The Rhai worker is a separate process that evaluates the configuration script for the Ravel CLI. The Ravel CLI launches a second copy of its own executable, in an internal role, as a child process. Here, “worker” means a process, not a thread. You install no separate language runtime.

Why does the Ravel CLI use a separate process? The parent stays in control while the script runs:

ReasonWhat the separate process permits
The deadlineWhen the deadline expires, the parent ends evaluation and can signal the worker process. Rhai cannot interrupt some of its own functions, such as sleep. A kernel stall can delay the worker’s exit.
Host requestsThe worker only computes. The parent performs each read or harness run that the script asks for and sends the data back.
Isolation of a failureIf the script exhausts the memory of the worker or overflows its stack, only the worker stops. The parent reports the failure and launches nothing.

The worker does not run at the same time as the managed process. The Ravel CLI waits until the worker exits, reaps it and removes its private directory. Only then does it launch the selected program.

The worker has an empty environment, and the configuration script has no direct filesystem, network or process access. A host capability is an operation that the script asks the Ravel CLI to perform. These operations include measured file reads and harness runs. The Ravel CLI controls each host call, the script’s request for an operation, and returns its data to the script.

Ravel gives the worker a request with a size limit. The worker gets no session-result context, the RAVEL_EXIT_FILE path with which a program sends the result of a session. Thus, the configuration script cannot end a session. The Ravel CLI checks the worker’s response. Only the Ravel CLI supervises the managed process. Evaluation and all its host calls end before Ravel launches that process.

Do you want an LLM to help the configuration script choose the harness, model, effort or prompt additions? The script can ask one through a harness run.

A harness run is one execution of a program from the harnesses entry of the settings. The configuration script requests it with ravel::run_harness during evaluation. An LLM call is a harness run that asks a model a question. The harness uses the owner’s sign-in, and Ravel holds no model credential. The script reads the answer as data and decides what it means.

An evaluation budget limits work during one evaluation of the configuration script. For example, the owner sets the number of harness runs in the settings. The script cannot raise that number or catch its exhaustion. One deadline covers evaluation. Compare purposes and completion rules:

ExecutionPurposeHow it ends
Rhai workerEvaluate the configuration scriptReturn a selection or refusal, then exit
Evaluation harness runReturn data to the configuration script, such as an LLM answerNatural exit or its own end-session result, followed by cleanup
Final managed processPerform the work of the caller’s sessionSend the session result with end-session, followed by cleanup

Each harness run has its own result channel. Its result reaches only the configuration script, and cannot complete the outer session. The final managed process receives a different channel after evaluation ends.

A refusal is a decision not to launch the final managed process. The configuration script makes it, or Ravel makes it when it cannot accept the file, behaviour or data of the script. Ravel does not try a different route. A failure of the worker, a failure of supervision and a selection timeout are different outcomes. A caller needs to tell a refusal from a failure during evaluation or supervision. Evaluation harness runs can occur before the script refuses.

A valid selection alone is not sufficient to start the launch. Ravel also confirms the cleanup conditions in this table before it launches the selected program.

Condition before launchWhat the Ravel CLI confirms
Host-call completionEvery host call ended. Each harness run completed its required cleanup.
Worker exit and reapThe worker ended, and the Ravel CLI collected its exit status
Removal of the private directoryThe Ravel CLI removed the worker’s private directory

The managed process and its result

The managed process is the direct child that the configuration script selects and that the Ravel CLI supervises. A process group is a set of processes that can receive a signal as one unit. Cleanup applies to that group, with all descendants that stay in it.

A harness is the agent program, such as Claude Code or Codex, in which an LLM session works on the prompt. The harness gives the prompt to the LLM and runs the commands that the LLM requests, for example shell commands. The configuration script selects the harness command. Here, “harness” means the agent program, not a test harness. Ravel supervises it as any other selected program.

The configuration script decides whether to pass the prompt to that program. Ravel does not require an LLM or a program that understands prompts. An ordinary program can take the place of the harness and send a value with end-session, as the examples do.

An interactive harness waits for more input when the LLM completes its task. Thus, the caller’s instructions to end the session tell the LLM to run ravel end-session with a result. Follow that command to its result. The program that Ravel launched, usually a harness that runs an LLM, runs ravel end-session. Ravel then stops that program and the rest of its process group. After cleanup, the sent value becomes the result of ravel start-session.

The opaque result is text that a program in the session sends as one UTF-8 argument with ravel end-session. In the prompt’s instructions to end the session, the caller gives the permitted values and tells when to use each value. In the review, the orchestrator permitted approved and changes-needed, and it gave a next action for each value. Ravel gives this text no meaning. After successful supervision and cleanup, Ravel returns the text unchanged. Then the caller decides its next step.

Fragment: the command that the review prompt asks the LLM to run when it finds a problem. This block omits the prompt and the harness.

ravel end-session changes-needed

The LLM runs this command in a shell that the harness starts. That shell inherits the path of the session’s result file. In your own shell outside a session, the same command fails with status 1, because it finds no such path.

The result is separate from the exit status of the managed process. It is also separate from the files or other work that the program produced, such as review-notes.md. A program can send an empty result. If no program sends a result, the session has no result, even when the managed process exits with status zero.

The result channel is the private temporary directory and completion file through which end-session sends that value. Ravel makes it before the launch and puts the path of the file in the RAVEL_EXIT_FILE environment variable of the managed process.

Ravel keeps the first complete result that it receives. That first end-session requests shutdown. A second end-session can still run before the shutdown stops its process. For example, another process in the group can send a result before Ravel stops it. Ravel keeps the first result, even if the second command succeeds.

This chapter writes INT, TERM, HUP and KILL without the SIG prefix. Other signal names keep that prefix. Code and diagnostic text keep their literal names.

The table shows the short interval in which another process can still send a result. These waits end early if the managed process exits.

Point after Ravel detects the resultWhat Ravel does
First 2 secondsGives the managed process time to exit
After 2 seconds, if the managed process still runsSends TERM
Up to 5 seconds after TERMWaits for the managed process to exit
After that wait, if the managed process still runsSends KILL
After the managed process exitsStops any group members that remain, then reaps the managed process

The result channel carries a request to end the session. That request does not prove that cleanup is complete.

Before the Ravel CLI returns success, it confirms the reap, the absence of the process group and the removal of the session’s temporary files. Cancellation suppresses the result, even if Ravel received it before the cancellation.

The result channel uses the completion channel of the runner. That completion channel only reports that a file appeared. Ravel’s code for the result channel checks and reads the contents. The appearance of the file is not a successful session. The selection from the Rhai worker is also not a successful session.

The figure shows why a response from the configuration script cannot complete a session. The response reaches the Ravel CLI on a path that is separate from the opaque result of the managed process. A harness that runs an LLM session has the managed-process role. An ordinary program can take its place.

This C4 container view shows the processes and the temporary store of Ravel. These are not operating-system containers. The result channel holds temporary files, not a database. The outline around them groups the software of Ravel, not a sandbox.

Steps 1 and 2 show the order: Ravel completes every host call and worker cleanup before the final managed process starts. The participants do not all run at the same time. The figure omits evaluation harness runs and their result channels. Those runs return data to the configuration script through host replies, as the table above shows.

Open the process-and-authority figure at full size.

The table shows that the Ravel CLI supervises the session and decides its final outcome. The caller and the owner keep their separate decisions.

ParticipantDecides or doesWhat Ravel lets it do
Caller, such as an orchestratorSupplies the task and the instructions to end the session. Interprets the result.Start the session and decide the next step
OwnerWrites the configuration script and the settingsConfigure which commands the requests select
Rhai workerEvaluates the configuration script and sends its responseReceive bounded request data and ask for host capabilities, with no environment or session-result context
Ravel CLI, with its runnerChecks the request, launches and supervises the program, and decides the outcomeSupervise processes, control the terminal and write the final result
Managed process, such as a harnessRuns the LLM session and the commands that it requests, which include end-session. Another selected program can take its place.Use its own session-result context, which its children inherit

Three different kinds of information

The word “context” can mean three different inputs. The book names each one. Thus, you do not confuse request data with permission to end a session.

The session-result context is the inherited RAVEL_EXIT_FILE path. It identifies the result channel of the enclosing session. How does it reach end-session? Ravel puts the path in the environment of the program that it launches. A child process gets a copy of the environment of its parent, unless the parent changes it. If each parent leaves the variable unchanged, the shell and the ravel end-session that it starts have the same path.

end-session reads that path from its own environment. It reads no configuration and accepts no target argument. It fails with status 1 if the path is missing, empty or stale, that is, from a session that already ended. Ravel has no persistent session lookup that it can use in place of that path.

In a nested launch, the outer managed process invokes a new start-session as a caller. Ravel gives a new path to the inner managed process. This path replaces the outer path only in the environment of that child. The outer managed process keeps its own session-result context. Thus, with normal inheritance, each end-session goes to its enclosing session, not to a session that an ID selects. Chapter 1 shows both paths from a real nested run.

The table shows why an end-session with the inner path ends the inner session. The outer process keeps its own path for the result.

Process that runs end-sessionInherited session-result contextThe result goes to
Outer managed process and its ordinary childrenPath of the outer sessionResult channel of the outer session
Inner managed process and its ordinary childrenNew path of the inner sessionResult channel of the inner session

The selection context is JSON data that the select function of the configuration script receives as context. It comes from the caller’s context document, which --context PATH names, or from the optional load_context function of the script. It can describe the work and the sources that inform a choice. The script reads files through ravel::read_text and ravel::read_json. For each successful read, Ravel records the canonical name, the byte count and the SHA-256 digest of the bytes that it read.

The configuration script cannot read a file directly. A harness program can read files, but Ravel does not measure those reads for the script. Selection context is data for selection. It gives no permission to send a session result, and it does not prove that the data is trustworthy.

The working directory of the caller is the cwd of the request. It tells the configuration script where the caller works. It is not one of the two forms of context above. The selected program starts in the working directory of the caller. An owner uses the cwd of the request when the location of the caller is important. The owner does not assume that the current directory of the worker is the same.

The Ravel CLI starts the worker in an empty private directory. The configuration script cannot change that directory or use it to read files. Relative measured reads use request.cwd. Relative imports use the directory of the file that imports them. After the worker ends and Ravel reaps it, Ravel removes its private directory.

The table identifies the directory for each path operation or program execution.

OperationDirectory used
Relative --config pathCaller’s working directory
Relative import in the configuration scriptDirectory of the real file that imports the module
Relative measured readCaller’s request.cwd
Evaluation harness run or final managed processCaller’s working directory

Use this table to keep selection data and the working directory separate from the inherited path for the result.

InformationFormPurpose
Session-result contextInherited RAVEL_EXIT_FILE pathDirects end-session to the result channel of the enclosing session
Selection contextJSON dataInforms the selection of the configuration script. Gives no permission to send a result.
Caller’s working directorycwd of the requestNames where the caller works. The selected program starts there.

Trust limits

The owner controls the configuration script and the settings. The separate worker computes with restricted inputs, while the Ravel CLI performs its host capabilities. A measured read can reach any regular file that the user can read. A configured harness can access files and the network within its permissions. These capabilities do not make arbitrary owner code safe.

Ravel does not restrict the filesystem access of the managed process or of an evaluation harness run. Each receives the environment of the caller, less the launch controls of an enclosing session. The harness sandbox is the only boundary of either process. The configuration script selects that sandbox through the arguments of the harness, such as codex --sandbox workspace-write. A harness with its sandbox off has no boundary at all. Ravel 2.0.0 refuses --confine and --runtime-read, which Ravel 1.1 accepts.

The inherited session-result context keeps nested sessions separate while each process uses the path that it inherited. It is not a security check against code that runs as the same user. That code can tamper with files or launch unrelated processes. Also, descendants that leave the process group of the managed process are outside its cleanup. If KILL stops the Ravel CLI, the managed process can continue to run without a supervisor. The Ravel CLI cannot catch KILL.

These limits are important when you decide what a caller can trust. A successful Ravel result confirms the supervision and cleanup that this chapter describes. It does not confirm that Ravel contained arbitrary same-user code. It also does not confirm that the work for the task of the caller is correct.

Chapter 3, “A complete session, end to end”, uses these roles in one session with a small configuration script and a fake agent. You make its directory, ~/ravel-first-session, and save its three files in it. The fake agent makes the selected command, the sent result and the cleanup visible. It needs no agent account and no model service.

A complete session, end to end

In this chapter, you run one session, one run of ravel start-session, on your own computer. The session uses a small configuration script, the Rhai file that chooses the program, and a fake agent. Then you follow the request through selection, launch, the sent result and cleanup.

You act as both owner and caller. The owner controls the configuration of Ravel, and the caller requests the session and interprets its result. You write the rule in the configuration script, supply the prompt and choose the result that you expect. The fake is an ordinary shell program. It needs no model service or agent account.

The fake is the managed process, the program that the configuration script selects. Its process group holds it and the descendants that stay in that group. Ravel can signal all members together. To reap its child, Ravel collects the child’s exit status from the operating system.

The example returns finished. In chapter 1, the review prompt permitted approved and changes-needed, and the orchestrator had an action for each value. Here, the prompt permits one value, and your action as the caller is to check the exit status and read finished. That word is your convention, not a Ravel command to continue or stop other work. Ravel returns this word only after it reaps the managed process, the whole process group is gone and cleanup succeeds. start-session exits with status 0 only if its final write to standard output also succeeds.

Install Ravel and check its version

Use a macOS or GNU/Linux host with Homebrew. The Homebrew formula linkuistics/taps/ravel@2 installs release archives for aarch64 macOS, aarch64 GNU/Linux and x86_64 GNU/Linux.

Ravel 2.0 first uses a separate, keg-only formula while the unversioned formula stays at 1.1.0. Keg-only means that Homebrew keeps its executable outside the shared command directory. This shell uses the versioned prefix so that the examples run with Rhai.

Fragment: installation and version check. This block omits Homebrew setup and the installation output.

HOMEBREW_NO_INSTALL_CLEANUP=1 brew install --skip-link linkuistics/taps/ravel@2
export PATH="$(brew --prefix ravel@2)/bin:$PATH"
ravel --version

Before you use the examples of this book, compare the version output with this version line:

ravel 2.0.0

The release procedure writes the version of the release into that line. If the line has a placeholder, this copy of the book is not from a release. If the two versions are different, open the book for your installed version from the release list on GitHub Pages. The release procedure keeps each release’s book in its own directory there.

Ravel 1.1.0 was the first release that carried the book. A copy of the book from a checkout between releases has the placeholder, and its text can describe changes that no release has yet. Chapter 11 explains the version line and the address of each release’s book.

The directory that brew --prefix ravel@2 prints contains the Ravel CLI, bin/ravel. That executable also contains the Rhai engine, which runs the configuration script. Ravel launches a second copy of its own binary as the separate Rhai worker, the process that evaluates the configuration script. You install no separate worker or language runtime.

The platform evidence in chapter 1 separates the measured aarch64 macOS and aarch64 GNU/Linux targets from x86_64 GNU/Linux. For x86_64 GNU/Linux, the only execution evidence comes from emulation. Chapter 11, “Deliver Ravel”, gives the installation and target details.

Run the first session

The example of this chapter is the first session. Its directory is ~/ravel-first-session, in your home directory. It holds a configuration script that selects one fake agent for every request. The fake prints the prompt that it receives and sends the result finished.

When you finish the steps, the directory holds these paths:

~/ravel-first-session/
├── config.rhai        you save it
├── bin/               you make it
│   └── fake-agent     you save it
├── home/              the command makes it
│   └── .config/ravel/settings.json  you save it
└── tmp/               the command makes it

Each path has one purpose in the session:

PathWho makes itPurpose
config.rhaiYouThe configuration script. The option --config config.rhai selects it.
bin/fake-agentYouThe fake agent, a shell script that the configuration script selects. It stands where a harness stands.
home/.config/ravel/settings.jsonYouSettings for this example. An empty source array disables skill delivery, so the fake receives only your prompt.
tmp/The commandA private temporary directory. Ravel makes the result channel of the session here.

Do these steps in the shell where ravel --version succeeded:

  1. Make the example directory and its bin subdirectory, then cd into the example directory. The fragment after these steps gives the two commands.
  2. Save the three files of the complete example at their labelled paths. Each label is a path in ravel-first-session.
  3. Type the command of the complete example without its first $ , which stands for the prompt of your shell.
  4. Run echo "$?" immediately after the command to see its exit status. Compare the output and the status with the example.

Fragment: make the example directory and cd into it. This block omits the three files, which you save next. The book checker makes its own directory for each example and does not run this block.

mkdir -p ~/ravel-first-session/bin ~/ravel-first-session/home/.config/ravel
cd ~/ravel-first-session

The ~ stands for your home directory, so these commands work from any current directory.

Complete example: pass a prompt through a small configuration script to a fake agent. The fake prints the prompt and sends finished.

home/.config/ravel/settings.json

{"skills":{"schemaVersion":1,"sources":[]}}

config.rhai

export const SCHEMA_VERSION = 4;
export const VERSION = "first-session";

fn select(request, context) {
    #{
        status: "selected",
        program: "fake-agent",
        args: [request.prompt],
        provider: "local",
        model: "fake",
        effort: "none",
        reason: "Learn the session protocol without a model service.",
    }
}

bin/fake-agent

#!/bin/sh
set -eu
printf 'fake-agent received: %s\n' "$1"
ravel end-session -- "finished"

Command

$ mkdir -p home tmp && chmod +x bin/fake-agent && env -i HOME="$PWD/home" TMPDIR="$PWD/tmp" PATH="$PWD/bin:$PATH" ravel start-session --kind demo --config config.rhai --prompt 'Print this prompt, then call ravel end-session -- "finished".'

Standard output, without a final newline

finished

Standard error includes

ravel: starting provider local, model fake, effort none for kind "demo"
fake-agent received: Print this prompt, then call ravel end-session -- "finished".

Exit status

0

In your terminal, the lines of standard output and standard error appear together. The book shows each stream in its own block. Ravel writes the lines of the second block before the result.

What did the command do? It is one line with three commands, joined by &&. Each command runs only if the command before it succeeded. These are its parts:

Part of the commandWhat it does in ravel-first-session
mkdir -p home tmpMakes home and tmp. With -p, mkdir gives no error when they exist already.
chmod +x bin/fake-agentMakes the fake executable, so that Ravel can run it as a program
env -iRuns the rest of the line with an empty environment. Your own variables, such as a RAVEL_EXIT_FILE of an enclosing session, do not enter the session.
HOME="$PWD/home" and TMPDIR="$PWD/tmp"Give Ravel the private directories. Your shell expands $PWD before env runs, so each value is an absolute path.
PATH="$PWD/bin:$PATH"Puts bin first on the search path. Your shell expands $PATH before env -i clears the environment, so your installed ravel stays on the path.
ravel start-sessionAsks Ravel for one session
--kind demoGives the caller’s kind. This configuration script does not examine it.
--config config.rhaiSelects the configuration script of the example, relative to the current directory
--prompt '…'Gives the caller’s prompt. The script passes it unchanged to the fake.

The private home and the explicit --config keep your usual settings and configuration script out of this session. The settings also disable the installation’s default skill directory. An empty home alone does not do that. With skill delivery enabled, Ravel adds discovery instructions before the caller’s prompt. Chapter 6 explains that prepared prompt.

Without --config, Ravel looks for the default script home/.config/ravel/config.rhai. That file does not exist, so Ravel refuses with config_missing and exit status 3.

Ravel writes the result unchanged to standard output. Here, the output is the eight bytes finished. Ravel does not interpret the result and adds no newline. Thus, in an interactive shell, the next prompt starts on the same line, unless the shell itself marks or supplies the missing newline.

The standard-error block shows the two stable lines. It does not promise that the stream contains only those lines. Ravel can also report cleanup operations with details, such as process IDs, that are different in each run.

On macOS, an attempt to stop a group whose members already exited can report Operation not permitted. Ravel writes that report to standard error. It allows success only after it confirms the reap, the terminal recovery and the absence of the group. A report alone does not prove success or failure. Check the exit status of start-session.

The fake’s printf writes to its own standard output, but the line appears on the caller’s standard error. Ravel uses the caller’s standard output only for the opaque result. Before the launch, Ravel redirects the standard output of the managed process to the caller’s standard error. The fake does not interpret the prompt. Its author wrote the script to run end-session, which is the action that the prompt requests. Ravel does not understand that instruction and does not add it.

The configuration script selects the fake for every request. demo is the caller’s kind, the token that names the sort of work. This script does not need to examine it.

What did the configuration script choose? Look at the first line on standard error. This line is the routing notice. The Ravel CLI writes it on your standard error after the script selects a program and before the launch. It tells you, the caller, which provider, model and effort the script chose, and for which kind. The values come from the labels of the selection.

The configuration script returns a selection, the map with status: "selected". Ravel reads these fields from it:

FieldValue in this exampleEffect
status"selected"Tells Ravel that the map is a selection, not a refusal
programfake-agentNames the program to execute
args[request.prompt]Passes the unchanged prompt as one argument
providerlocalDescribes the provider in the routing notice
modelfakeDescribes the model in the routing notice
effortnoneDescribes the effort in the routing notice
reason"Learn the session protocol without a model service."Explains the choice in the JSON form of the routing notice

The provider, model and effort labels do not select a model service. A real configuration script puts the options of its harness in args. The labels only report the choice.

This file is Rhai, the small language that Ravel contains. SCHEMA_VERSION identifies the interface between Ravel and the script, and VERSION names this revision of the owner’s script. fn defines a function, and #{ ... } constructs a map. The final expression returns that map. Ravel checks its fields during evaluation.

The function accepts a request and selection context. This example needs only request.prompt, so it leaves the second argument unused. Chapter 4 teaches the syntax and the other fields.

Check the request and evaluate the configuration script

The Ravel CLI first reads the settings and the command-line inputs and checks the configuration script. --config config.rhai names a file relative to the caller’s working directory. Ravel resolves its real path and requires a readable regular file. It does not search the caller’s repository for a different configuration script. Settings still come from $HOME/.config/ravel/settings.json, even when --config is present.

The example’s settings disable skill delivery. The other settings use their defaults.

The Ravel CLI launches a second copy of its own binary, the Rhai worker, in the worker’s empty private directory. At the deadline, the Ravel CLI ends evaluation and can signal the separate worker. A kernel stall can delay the worker’s exit. A memory or stack failure in the script ends only the worker. The Ravel CLI checks the identity of the worker before it sends the evaluation request. The worker receives an empty environment and no session-result context, the RAVEL_EXIT_FILE path with which a program sends the session result.

The cwd of the request keeps the caller’s working directory. The configuration script has no direct filesystem access. Ravel reads config.rhai and sends its source to the worker, which evaluates it and calls select.

The configuration script has no load_context function, and the caller supplied no selection context, the optional JSON data that informs the choice. In this example, the selection therefore depends only on the request. The second argument to select is (), Rhai’s value for no value.

A configuration script that needs files or model advice asks Ravel through host capabilities, the ravel:: functions that the Ravel CLI performs for the script. Ravel measures each successful file read and supervises each evaluation harness run. This script needs neither operation.

The worker sends its response through a private socket. The Ravel CLI checks the metadata of the script and the selected command. Anything that the worker prints is diagnostic text, not a selection.

The worker and the managed process have different roles in this example:

PropertyRhai workerManaged process
PurposeEvaluate the configuration scriptExecute the selected command
Working directoryStarts in a private directory. The script cannot read files directly or change that directory.Starts in the caller’s working directory
Session-result contextNoneReceives its own RAVEL_EXIT_FILE path
Response to RavelSelection or refusal through a private socketOpaque result through the result channel

After the response, the Ravel CLI confirms that the worker ended and that the Ravel CLI reaped it. It also removes the private directory of the worker. Only then does it resolve the selected executable and allow the managed launch. Any host call also ends before that launch. If the worker cleanup fails, Ravel does not launch, even after a valid response.

Here, Ravel searches the caller’s PATH for fake-agent. The first directory in that PATH is the bin directory of the example. The selected program starts in the caller’s working directory. Ravel executes the selected program and argument list directly. It does not use shell expansion, and it does not split the prompt into words. The fake still runs in a shell, because its first line, #!/bin/sh, tells the operating system to run the file with /bin/sh.

Launch the managed job

After selection, Ravel makes a new result channel. This channel is a private temporary directory and a path where a program can send a result. Ravel puts that path in the RAVEL_EXIT_FILE environment variable of the managed process. This path is the session-result context of the managed process. Here, the managed process is the fake agent.

How does end-session find that path later? Each child process gets a copy of the environment of its parent, unless the parent changes it. The fake starts ravel end-session as its child, so that command inherits the same RAVEL_EXIT_FILE. In a real run on macOS with TMPDIR=/tmp, the path was /private/tmp/ravel-session-mlcN86/signal-c7d5f2eed4198eb768996f7b4868bbcc. The random parts change in each run. The PATH of the example also lets the fake find the same installed ravel for end-session.

The runner in Ravel launches the managed process in a process group of its own. A process group is a set of processes. A program can send one signal to all processes in the group. A child inherits the group of its parent unless it moves to another group. Thus, with one signal to the group, Ravel can stop the fake and each descendant that stays in that group.

The managed process and the members of its group form the job that Ravel supervises. Here, “job” has its job-control meaning: a process group that a program controls as one unit. Ravel stays the parent of the managed process while it supervises the process. start-session does not replace itself with the selected program.

The new process group does not make a new Ravel session or a new login. These three terms name different units:

UnitWhat it meansIn this example
Ravel sessionOne start-session invocationCovers selection, the managed job and cleanup
Process groupProcesses that can receive a signal as one groupThe managed process leads a new group
POSIX sessionAn operating-system unit that job control usesThe managed process stays in the caller’s POSIX session

This chapter writes INT, TERM, HUP and KILL without the SIG prefix. Other signal names keep that prefix. Code and diagnostic text keep their literal names.

If you start Ravel from an interactive terminal, one more group is important: the foreground process group of the terminal. The controlling terminal sends keyboard input to that group. It also sends the signals that the terminal makes, such as INT from Ctrl-C, to that group.

If Ravel’s own process group is the foreground group, Ravel saves the terminal modes. Then it makes the group of the managed process the foreground group. Ravel does these two steps before the managed process executes the selected program. This book says that Ravel lends the terminal. The selected program can then read from the terminal as a foreground job. If a different job has the foreground, Ravel launches the program and does not lend the terminal.
