If Ravel lent the terminal, it attempts recovery under the holder rules of chapter 9. It restores the saved modes only after its own group holds the foreground.

The modes include settings such as whether the terminal shows typed characters. This recovery is important when a program changes the modes. The fake does not read input or change terminal modes. The automatic example check runs without a terminal. Thus, its transcript shows the choice of command and the delivery of the result, not a change of the foreground group.

Chapter 9, “Jobs, signals and the terminal”, gives the terminal rules. These rules include, for each case, the process group from which Ravel recovers the terminal. Chapter 9 also gives signal masks and failure guarantees.

Send the result and finish the job

The fake prints its argument and calls ravel end-session -- "finished". That command reads the inherited RAVEL_EXIT_FILE path. It reads no configuration and needs no session ID. It writes the complete value into a temporary file. Then it sends the value into the result channel, but it does not replace a result that is already there. Ravel keeps the first complete result that it receives.

Follow that command to its result. When end-session sends the result, it asks the Ravel CLI to end the session. Ravel then stops the fake and the rest of its process group. After cleanup, finished becomes the result of ravel start-session.

The request does not mean that the job already ended. Also, end-session does not wait for the cleanup of the enclosing session. In this fake, the script has no more commands, so it exits.

A managed process can continue to run after it sends the result. The runner then uses these stages. Chapter 9 gives the exact times.

StageWhen it startsRunner action
Grace periodThe runner detects the sent resultAllows time for the managed process to exit
TERMThe managed process still runs after the grace periodSends TERM to the process group, then allows a second grace period
KILLThe managed process still runs after the second grace periodSends KILL to the process group

The exit of the managed process does not complete the cleanup. Before it reaps the direct child, Ravel stops the members of the process group that still run. To reap a finished child, the parent collects its exit status. This action also releases the last record of that child in the operating system.

The managed process leads its group. Thus, the ID of the group is the PID of the managed process. The operating system does not use that PID again until Ravel reaps the process. Thus, Ravel sends the last group signals before the reap, so that they cannot go to an unrelated group that took the same ID.

Then, if the runner lent the terminal, it recovers the terminal. It also confirms that the process group is gone. The Ravel CLI checks and reads the sent result. It removes the temporary directory of the result channel. Then, before it writes to the caller’s standard output, it checks the outcomes of cancellation and cleanup. The appearance of the result file does not remove any of these steps.

Success means that the result reaches the caller only after Ravel reaps the managed process, the whole process group is gone and cleanup succeeds. start-session exits with status 0 only if the final write also succeeds. If the write fails, the caller can receive only the start of the result, and the invocation fails. Thus, bytes on standard output alone do not prove success.

That guarantee is for the process group of the managed process. It does not include these three cases:

  • a descendant that leaves the group
  • a descendant of an evaluation harness run that leaves its group
  • a KILL that stops the Ravel CLI (the Ravel CLI cannot catch KILL)

The trust limits in chapter 2 also explain why code that runs as the same user can change files or launch unrelated processes. A successful session shows that Ravel delivered a sent result after it confirmed the cleanup. It does not show that the work that the prompt requests is correct.

The sequence below shows two orders that Ravel guarantees. Worker cleanup comes before the managed launch, and job cleanup comes before the delivery of the result. The figure follows the successful path of this chapter.

In this UML sequence, an X ends the pictured lifetime after confirmed reap or removal, not at the instant a process exits. The runner shares the Ravel CLI’s lifeline because it runs inside that process.

Figure 3.1 — The sent result requests completion. The final write of the result requires successful cleanup and no cancellation. Open the successful-session sequence at full size.

In words, the Ravel CLI checks the request, the identity of the worker and the response of the configuration script. Then it confirms the reap of the worker and the removal of the private directory of the worker. It resolves the executable, makes the result channel and launches the managed process. end-session sends the value into that channel. It does not return the value to the caller.

The Ravel CLI stops the group of the managed process before the reap. It recovers a lent terminal and confirms that the group is gone. It reads the value and removes the temporary directory of the channel. Then it decides the outcome. That decision uses any cancellation or cleanup problem. Then it writes the value unchanged to standard output and flushes it.

The text above explains each stage. The figure does not show times or individual system calls.

Recognise the other outcomes

Refusal, cancellation, natural exit without a result and failure are normal outcomes. Each one is different from the successful example and from the others. A caller needs to recognise each one.

  • A refusal occurs before the final managed launch. Evaluation harness runs can occur before the refusal. It occurs when the configuration script declines the request, or when Ravel cannot accept the file, behaviour or data of the script. Ravel does not try a different route and returns no result to the caller.
  • Cancellation by an INT, TERM or HUP signal can occur during selection or supervision. The session then returns no result to the caller, even if a program already sent a value.
  • A natural exit without a sent result also returns no result to the caller, even when the managed process exits with status 0. An empty sent result is different. A program sent an empty value, and the session can succeed after cleanup.
  • A failure of the worker, of supervision or of cleanup prevents success. A failure of the final write of the result also makes the invocation fail after cleanup.

Also, a managed process can send a result and later exit with a nonzero status. That exit does not make the result invalid. Ravel checks the sent result, cancellation and cleanup separately from the exit status of the program. Chapter 5 teaches the outcomes and their exit codes with examples.

The state machine below shows why a session with a sent value can end without a successful result. It separates the phase in which work stops from the outcome that the caller receives after the cleanup attempts.

Figure 3.2 — The sent result, cleanup and the final write are separate conditions of success. Cancellation returns no result to the caller, even after a program sends a value. Open the outcome state machine at full size.

In words, a refusal stops selection before the launch. Cancellation and failure can stop selection or supervision. A natural exit without a sent result is part of supervision, even if the managed process exits with status 0. On each path, Ravel attempts the necessary cleanup.

Then Ravel decides the final outcome, and cancellation has priority. If no cancellation occurred, the outcome is the original refusal or failure. A missing result is one of these failures. Ravel reports any further cleanup problems next to that outcome. A cleanup failure prevents success, even when everything else succeeds.

Only a valid sent value with no cancellation, a confirmed reap, confirmed absence of the group and confirmed cleanup reaches the final write and flush. An empty sent result is a value. A later nonzero exit of the managed process does not, by itself, make the value invalid.

The machine shows the cleanup operations above as a group. It does not mean that a failed cleanup completed. Its cancellation conditions apply until Ravel decides the final outcome, and also at that decision. A signal that arrives after that decision can still stop the Ravel CLI while it writes the result. A failed write can leave only the start of the result on standard output. Chapter 8 gives the full outcome decision.

To write rules in the configuration script beyond this single fake, continue with chapter 4, “Write a configuration script”. To invoke Ravel from your own tool, continue with chapter 5, “Run sessions”. Chapter 8, “Follow a session through the code”, follows this same sequence into the implementation.

Write a configuration script

In chapter 1, an orchestrator asked Ravel for a code review with the kind review. A short Rhai script chose the program for that kind, and Ravel ran it. You write that script. This chapter teaches you to write and check a configuration script, and to let an LLM help it choose.

First, the words that this chapter uses on every page:

WordWhat it names
SessionOne run of ravel start-session, from the request to the result
CallerThe person or program that runs that command, for example an orchestrator. The caller gives a prompt and a kind.
KindA short token from the caller that names the sort of work, such as review
OwnerThe person who controls the configuration of Ravel for the account. In this chapter, the owner is you.
Configuration scriptA file of Rhai code that you write. Ravel gives the request to the script. The script then selects a program and its arguments, or it refuses the request.
SettingsValues in the JSON file $HOME/.config/ravel/settings.json, such as limits and the harnesses that the script can run. The script cannot change them.
ConfigurationThe configuration script and the settings together

Ravel uses configuration names in its file paths, options and diagnostic codes, for example config.rhai, --config-env and config_refused.

Know the Rhai language

What is Rhai? Rhai is a small computer language for programs in the Rust language. The Rhai engine is part of the ravel executable. Thus, you install no separate language runtime for your configuration script. Chapter 1 introduces Rhai with a short script. These characteristics of Rhai are important for a configuration script:

  • A familiar syntax. Rhai looks like a mix of JavaScript and Rust. It has variables, functions, if, loops, arrays and maps.
  • Dynamic types. You do not declare a type. Each value has its type while the script runs.
  • A sandbox. Rhai code changes things outside the script only through functions that the host program gives it. In Ravel, these are the ravel:: functions.
  • Protection against runaway scripts. Ravel sets limits on the operations, the call depth and the data size of a script. One deadline covers each evaluation.
  • Modules. A configuration script can import other Rhai files.

Each example explains the Rhai that it uses. Rhai also has its own documentation:

  • The Rhai Book is the manual. On 27 October 2026, it documented Rhai 1.27.0, the version that Ravel contains.
  • The language reference gives the syntax in a short form.
  • The online playground runs Rhai code in your web browser. It has no ravel:: functions and none of Ravel’s limits. Thus, a script that calls a ravel:: function does not run there.

The sections of this chapter go from a fixed route to an LLM that helps the configuration script choose. Read them in order, or go to the task that you need:

SectionWhy you may want it
Define a configuration script and read the requestMake Ravel find your script, and read what the caller asked for
Select a command or refuseChoose the program and its arguments, or refuse with a message that the caller can act on
Validate a configuration script with fake agents Test each route and each refusal before a real harness runs
Put each kind and its route in one tableSee each kind and its route in one place
Import other Rhai filesKeep routes or helper functions in separate files
Let a repository choose an arrangementLet a repository ask for one of the arrangements that you offer
Supply selection contextLet facts about the task change the choice
Check JSON with the data helpersReject JSON from a file or a model when it has the wrong shape
Set bounds and environment grants in the settingsGive the script more time, or let it read an environment variable
Run a harness during evaluationAsk another program a question before you choose
Ask a model through Claude CodeLet an LLM in Claude Code help with the choice
Ask a model through CodexLet an LLM in Codex help with the choice
Select Claude Code or Codex for the caller’s workRun an interactive LLM session as the program of the session
Copy a configuration script and its filesMove a script and its files to another place

Follow the evaluation of a configuration script

What happens between the caller’s command and the start of a program? Start with the review of chapter 1. The orchestrator ran the command below.

Fragment: the request of the review example in chapter 1. This block omits the code of the orchestrator that runs the command and the prompt file.

ravel start-session --kind review --prompt-file review-prompt.txt

The configuration script of that chapter had one rule. For the kind review, it selected fake-agent and put You are a careful code reviewer. before the caller’s prompt. The table follows that request from the command to the start of fake-agent. In the table, the Rhai worker is a second copy of the ravel executable that evaluates the script. This section explains it after the table.

OrderWho actsWhat happens in the review
1The orchestratorRuns ravel start-session with the kind review and the prompt file
2The Ravel CLIReads the settings, finds the configuration script and starts the Rhai worker
3The Rhai workerRuns the top-level statements of the script one time. Here, they are only the two export const lines.
4The Rhai workerCalls select(request, context). request.kind is review, so select returns a selection of fake-agent.
5The Ravel CLIChecks the map that select returned, reaps the worker and checks the selection again before launch
6The Ravel CLIWrites ravel: starting provider local, model fake, effort low for kind "review" on the orchestrator’s standard error, then starts fake-agent

A successful selection follows this order. The values depend on the request and the script. A script can also define load_context(request), which the worker calls between steps 3 and 4. The section “Supply selection context” teaches it. With --json, the routing notice is a JSON document, and it follows the supervision attempt.

To reap a child process, its parent collects the exit status of that child. Ravel reaps the worker before it starts the selected program. Thus, the Rhai worker no longer runs when the selected program starts.

A selection is the map that select returns when it chooses a program. In the review, it held fake-agent, one argument and the labels local, fake and low for the provider, model and effort. A refusal is a map that says why Ravel starts no final managed process. The routing notice is the line in step 6. It tells the caller which provider, model and effort the configuration script chose. Chapter 1, “The selection and the routing notice”, shows both with real values.

What is the Rhai worker? The Ravel CLI, the ravel command-line program, does not run your script in its own process. It starts a second copy of its own executable as a child process. That child is the Rhai worker. The worker evaluates the configuration script and sends the selection or the refusal to its parent.

The worker has an empty environment. It has no direct access to files, the network or other programs.

Why a separate process? The parent stays in control while the script runs:

ReasonWhat the separate process permits
The deadlineWhen the deadline expires, the parent ends evaluation and can signal the worker process. Rhai cannot interrupt some of its own functions, such as sleep. A kernel stall can delay the worker’s exit.
Requests from the scriptThe worker only computes. When the script reads a file or runs a harness, the worker asks the parent. The parent does the work and sends the data back.
Isolation of a failureIf the script exhausts the memory of the worker or overflows its stack, only the worker stops. The parent continues, reports the failure to the caller and starts no program.

The worker does not run at the same time as the selected program. Ravel waits until the worker exits and collects its exit status. Only then does Ravel start the program that the script selected.

How does the script read a file or start a program, if the worker cannot? It asks the Ravel CLI. A host capability is an operation that the configuration script asks Ravel to perform. Each host capability is a function in the ravel namespace, such as ravel::read_text.

When the script calls a host capability, the worker sends the request to the Ravel CLI over a private socket and waits. The Ravel CLI does the work within the limits of your settings and sends the answer back. For the script, the call looks like an ordinary function call. The data helpers, such as ravel::json_parse, are different. They run inside the worker and send no request.

The script can call host capabilities at the top level of the module, in load_context and in select. A measured read is a read of a file through ravel::read_text or ravel::read_json. Ravel reads the file for the script and records its canonical path, its byte count and its SHA-256 digest. The JSON routing notice lists each measured read and each harness run of the evaluation.

A harness is the program in which an LLM works on a prompt, such as Claude Code or Codex. Your configuration script can use a harness in two different ways:

Use of the harnessWhen it runsWhere its answer goes
A harness runDuring evaluation, when the script calls ravel::run_harnessBack to the script, as data for its choice
The managed processAfter evaluation, when the script selects the harnessTo the caller, as the result of the session

The managed process is the program that Ravel starts and supervises for the session. Ravel starts it only after the evaluation ends and Ravel reaps the worker.

Run the examples of this chapter

Every example includes settings.json. Its skills entry has an empty source array, so the fake receives only the caller’s prompt. The command block copies this file into the example’s private home before Ravel runs. Other settings in an example serve that example’s task.

Every complete example in this chapter uses fake programs. A fake agent is a small shell script that stands where a harness stands. Most fake agents here run ravel end-session with the arguments that the configuration script gave them. Thus, the result of an example shows you what the script chose. The fake harnesses for an LLM call print a fixed answer. No example needs a model account or a real harness.

Each example has a name and a directory of its own. Put these directories in one workspace directory, ~/ravel-chapter-4, in your home directory. Why a new directory for each example? Several examples use the same file names, such as config.rhai and bin/fake-agent, with different contents. Some commands also write files, such as .ravel-choice, that change the result of the next command.

The first example is fixed-route. Its configuration script selects one fake agent for every request. The fake sends the caller’s prompt back as the result. When you finish the steps, the directory holds these paths:

~/ravel-chapter-4/
└── fixed-route/
    ├── settings.json      you save it
    ├── config.rhai        you save it
    ├── bin/               you make it
    │   └── fake-agent     you save it
    ├── home/              the first command line makes it
    └── tmp/               the first command line makes it

Each path has one purpose in the example:

Path in fixed-routeWho makes itPurpose
settings.jsonYouDisables skill delivery with an empty source array. The setup copies it into the private home.
config.rhaiYouThe configuration script. The option --config config.rhai selects it.
bin/fake-agentYouThe fake agent that the configuration script selects. It sends its first argument as the result.
home/The first command lineA private home directory. The setup copies the example’s settings here and disables skill delivery.
tmp/The first command lineA private temporary directory. Ravel makes the result channel of the session here.

Do these steps in a shell where ravel --version succeeds:

  1. Make the example directory and its bin subdirectory, then cd into the example directory. The fragment after these steps gives the two commands.
  2. Save the three files of the complete example at their labelled paths. Each label is a path in fixed-route.
  3. Type each command line of the example without its first $ , which stands for the prompt of your shell.
  4. Run echo "$?" immediately after the final line to see its exit status. Compare the output and the status with the example.

Fragment: make the directory of fixed-route and cd into it. This block omits the three files, which you save next. The book checker makes its own directory for each example and does not run this block.

mkdir -p ~/ravel-chapter-4/fixed-route/bin
cd ~/ravel-chapter-4/fixed-route

The ~ stands for your home directory, so these commands work from any current directory.

Complete example: pass the caller’s prompt to a fake agent.

settings.json

{"skills":{"schemaVersion":1,"sources":[]}}

config.rhai

export const SCHEMA_VERSION = 4;
export const VERSION = "fixed-route-1";

fn select(request, context) {
    #{
        status: "selected",
        program: "fake-agent",
        args: [request.prompt],
        provider: "example",
        model: "fake",
        effort: "low",
        reason: "one fixed route",
    }
}

bin/fake-agent

#!/bin/sh
ravel end-session -- "$1"

Command

$ mkdir -p home/.config/ravel tmp && chmod +x bin/*
$ cp settings.json home/.config/ravel/settings.json
$ env -i HOME="$PWD/home" TMPDIR="$PWD/tmp" PATH="$PWD/bin:$PATH" ravel start-session --config config.rhai --kind question --prompt "Explain this change."

Standard output, without a final newline

Explain this change.

Exit status

0

In your terminal, standard error also shows the routing notice and can show reports of cleanup operations. The book makes no claim about standard error in this example.

The command block prepares the directories, copies the settings and runs the session. These are its parts:

Part of the commandWhat it does in fixed-route
mkdir -p home/.config/ravel tmpMakes the private configuration directory and tmp.
cp settings.json home/.config/ravel/settings.jsonPuts the example’s settings where Ravel reads them.
chmod +x bin/*Makes each file in bin, here only fake-agent, executable
env -iRuns the rest of the line with an empty environment. Your own variables, such as a RAVEL_EXIT_FILE of an enclosing session, do not enter the example.
HOME="$PWD/home" and TMPDIR="$PWD/tmp"Give Ravel the private directories. Your shell expands $PWD before env runs, so each value is an absolute path.
PATH="$PWD/bin:$PATH"Puts bin first on the search path. Your shell expands $PATH before env -i clears the environment, so your installed ravel stays on the path.
ravel start-sessionAsks Ravel for one session
--config config.rhaiSelects the configuration script of the example, relative to the current directory
--kind questionGives the caller’s kind. This configuration script does not examine it.
--prompt "Explain this change."Gives the caller’s prompt. The script passes it to the fake as its first argument.

The private home and the explicit --config keep your own settings and configuration script out of the example.

What happened in the session? Follow the request from the caller to the result:

OrderWho actsWhat happens in this example
1The callerRuns ravel start-session with the kind question and the prompt Explain this change.
2The configuration scriptselect returns a selection with the program fake-agent and one argument, the prompt
3The Ravel CLIWrites the routing notice ravel: starting provider example, model fake, effort low for kind "question" on standard error, then starts fake-agent
4fake-agentRuns ravel end-session -- "$1", which sends its first argument as the result
5The Ravel CLIStops fake-agent and the rest of its process group, then completes cleanup
6The Ravel CLIWrites Explain this change. on standard output. This text is the result of ravel start-session.

A process group is the operating-system unit that holds the program and the descendants that remain in its group. Ravel stops each process in that group before it returns the result.

The -- in ravel end-session -- "$1" ends the options of end-session. Thus, a result that starts with - stays a result and does not become an option. Ravel writes the result unchanged and adds no newline. This is why the label says “without a final newline”, and why your next shell prompt can start on the same line as the result.

Each later example of this chapter uses the same four steps in a new directory under ~/ravel-chapter-4. For example, step 1 of the next example, refusals, is mkdir -p ~/ravel-chapter-4/refusals/bin, then cd ~/ravel-chapter-4/refusals. Before its complete example, a paragraph names the directory and says how the example is different from fixed-route. An example can have more files, more command lines or different options. The setup appears only in the first command block of each example. A later block uses the directories that the first block made.

The book checker runs the same files and commands with the built Ravel CLI. For each example, it makes a new directory, saves the labelled files and makes the files in bin executable. Then it runs the command blocks in order and compares each output with the output that the book shows. You do the preparation by hand, in the steps above.

Define a configuration script and read the request

The config.rhai of fixed-route has two export const lines and one function. What must a configuration script contain, and where does Ravel find it? Ravel looks in one place. The default script is $HOME/.config/ravel/config.rhai. HOME must be absolute for that default.

--config PATH selects another file, relative to the caller’s working directory. Ravel searches no parent directory and tries no fallback script. Thus, a missing script is an error. It is not a silent change of route.

The file must be readable UTF-8, regular, and at most 1 MiB. A symlink identifies its canonical real file.

A configuration script is a Rhai module. A module is one file of Rhai code, which can export constants for other code to read. Your script exports two constants and defines a function:

DeclarationPurpose
export const SCHEMA_VERSION = 4;Name the version of the interface between Ravel and the script that this file uses.
export const VERSION = "fixed-route-1";Name this revision of your script. The JSON routing notice shows it.
fn select(request, context)Return a selection or a refusal.
fn load_context(request)Optionally assemble selection context before select.

SCHEMA_VERSION is independent of the Ravel release and of the version of the context document. VERSION is a nonblank string that you change when your script changes. The functions need the parameter counts shown above. Top-level statements run once before the functions.

The select function of fixed-route used this Rhai:

  • #{ ... } makes a map, which is a set of named fields.
  • request.prompt reads the field prompt of the map request.
  • [request.prompt] makes an array with one element.
  • The last expression of a function, without a semicolon, is its return value. return value; returns earlier.

What can the script read about the request? The request is a map with these fields. Optional inputs have the value () when they are absent. In Rhai, () means “no value”, and it becomes null in JSON.

FieldValue
schemaVersionThe integer 4.
kindThe caller’s kind token, from --kind.
originalPromptThe caller’s prompt, unchanged.
promptThe prompt to give to the harness. With skill delivery disabled, it holds the same bytes as originalPrompt. With delivery enabled, it holds the discovery instructions, then the caller’s prompt in one delimited block.
cwdThe caller’s working directory.
paramsA map of strings from --param NAME=VALUE.
taskFileThe absolute --task-file path, or (). Ravel does not read it.
taskIdThe --task-id string, or ().
contextThe caller’s checked context document, or ().
skills() with skill delivery disabled. Otherwise a map with descriptorPath, sources, directories and mandatory.
limitsThe effective bounds that the script can read.

Put request.prompt into the arguments of the harness. Read request.originalPrompt when the script examines the task, for example when it asks a model to classify the task.

The owner can enable skill delivery in the settings. Then request.prompt also holds the skill directories and the discovery instructions, and the script needs no change for them. A harness that reads only some directories needs a read grant for the skill snapshots. Make that grant from request.skills, for example one argument for each snapshotRoot. The shipped example harness-table.rhai does this for its lead harness.

Excerpt from crates/ravel/examples/harness-table.rhai: the function that lists the snapshot roots. The function harness_args puts each root after --add-dir.

fn skill_reads(skills) {
    if skills == () { return []; }
    let reads = [];
    for source in skills.sources { reads.push(source.snapshotRoot); }
    reads
}

Skill delivery at launch gives the parts of the prepared prompt and the members of request.skills.

The second argument, context, is the delivered selection context: JSON data for the choice. A loader can make it different from request.context. Neither value gives the script authority to end the session.

Ravel checks undefined variables when it compiles the script. It also refuses an absent map property when the script reads it. Check request.params.contains("name") before you read an optional parameter. The documented request fields themselves are always present.

Select a command or refuse

Your configuration script gives one of two answers to each request. A selection tells Ravel which program to start. A refusal prevents the start of the final managed process. Why refuse? You may want a request that does not fit your routes to stop with a clear message, and not go to the wrong harness.

A selection is the map that select returns when it chooses a program. Its status field is "selected". A selected map holds seven fields, and Ravel needs each of them. Ravel refuses an unknown field.

FieldWhat the script supplies
statusThe literal "selected".
programA nonblank program name or path, without NUL.
argsAn array of strings after the program. Empty strings are valid, but NUL is not.
providerA nonblank owner label for the model origin.
modelA nonblank owner label for the model.
effortA nonblank owner label for the effort.
reasonA nonblank explanation of the choice.

A bare program name resolves through the caller’s PATH. A relative path with / resolves from the caller’s working directory. Each member of args is one argument. Ravel performs no word splitting, shell expansion or template substitution. A prompt with spaces therefore stays one argument when the script puts it there.

program and args are the command that Ravel starts. provider, model, effort and reason are labels. A label describes the choice of the script, but it does not configure the program. For example, model: "haiku" records a label, whereas args: ["--model", "haiku"] gives a model option to a program. Your script sets both, and Ravel does not compare them.

Why have labels, then? The caller usually wants to know what the script chose, but the caller does not read your script. Ravel tells the caller in the routing notice. Before it starts the program, the Ravel CLI writes one line on the caller’s standard error. For the first example of this chapter, fixed-route, that line was:

Fragment: the routing notice of fixed-route, on standard error. This block omits the command, the reports of cleanup operations and the result on standard output.

ravel: starting provider example, model fake, effort low for kind "question"

With --json, a JSON document replaces this line after the supervision attempt. That document also gives the reason. Chapter 5, “Read notices separately from results”, teaches both forms.

A refusal is a map with status: "refused" and three more strings. Each string is nonblank:

FieldWhat the script suppliesWho reads it
codeA short token that names the reason, such as unknown_kindA caller program that acts on the reason
messageA sentence that says what is wrongThe person who reads standard error
remedyA sentence that says what to changeThe person who reads standard error

After a refusal, Ravel reports config_refused with exit status 3. Your code appears separately as configCode in the JSON failure document. No managed process starts, and Ravel tries no other route. The caller receives no result.

The next example is refusals. Make its directory as for fixed-route: ~/ravel-chapter-4/refusals, with a bin subdirectory. It has the same two file paths. bin/fake-agent has the same contents, but config.rhai is different. The example has three command blocks. Run them in order:

  • The first block makes home and tmp, then asks with the kind question.
  • The second block asks with the kind unknown.
  • The third block asks with the kind question and adds --param model=other.

Complete example: accept one kind and refuse an unknown kind or a forbidden parameter.

settings.json

{"skills":{"schemaVersion":1,"sources":[]}}

config.rhai

export const SCHEMA_VERSION = 4;
export const VERSION = "checked-route-1";

fn refuse(code, message, remedy) {
    #{ status: "refused", code: code, message: message, remedy: remedy }
}

fn select(request, context) {
    if request.kind != "question" {
        return refuse("unknown_kind", "This configuration script accepts question only.",
                      "Use --kind question.");
    }
    if request.params.contains("model") {
        return refuse("owner_model", "The owner chooses the model.",
                      "Remove the model parameter.");
    }
    #{ status: "selected", program: "fake-agent", args: [request.prompt],
       provider: "example", model: "fake", effort: "low", reason: "question route" }
}

ravel end-session -- "$1"

$ env -i HOME="$PWD/home" TMPDIR="$PWD/tmp" PATH="$PWD/bin:$PATH" ravel start-session --config config.rhai --kind unknown --prompt "hello"

Standard output

Standard error

ravel: refused (config_refused, stage selection): the configuration script <scratch>/work/config.rhai refused the selection: This configuration script accepts question only.
  input: --kind unknown
  source: <scratch>/work/config.rhai
  config code: unknown_kind
  remedy: Use --kind question.

Exit status

Command

$ env -i HOME="$PWD/home" TMPDIR="$PWD/tmp" PATH="$PWD/bin:$PATH" ravel start-session --config config.rhai --kind question --param model=other --prompt "hello"

Standard output

Standard error

ravel: refused (config_refused, stage selection): the configuration script <scratch>/work/config.rhai refused the selection: The owner chooses the model.
  input: --kind question
  source: <scratch>/work/config.rhai
  config code: owner_model
  remedy: Remove the model parameter.

Exit status

3

How do you read a refusal? The first command succeeded, and the two refused commands wrote nothing on standard output. Each refusal wrote these lines on standard error. The values come from the third command:

LineWhat it tells you
ravel: refused (config_refused, stage selection)Ravel’s code for the outcome, and the stage in which it occurred. The script refused during selection.
the configuration script … refused the selection: The owner chooses the model.The file of the configuration script, then the script’s message
input: --kind questionThe kind of the request
source: <scratch>/work/config.rhaiThe file of the configuration script. <scratch>/work stands for the example’s directory. In your shell, the line shows the absolute path of your refusals directory.
config code: owner_modelThe script’s own code
remedy: Remove the model parameter.The script’s remedy, which tells the caller what to change

The exit status 3 tells a caller program that a refusal occurred, without a read of the text. With --json, the same fields are in the JSON failure document, and the script’s code is in configCode.

if chooses a branch. != compares values for inequality. fn refuse(...) is an ordinary helper function, with no special Ravel role. Rhai functions have no parameter type declarations here. Ravel checks the value when it crosses the boundary from the script.

A return of () gives selection_abstained. An uncaught error in select gives selection_threw with a source location. A wrong field or type gives selection_malformed. After any of these outcomes, Ravel starts no managed process.

Validate a configuration script with fake agents

How do you know that your configuration script does what you want before a real harness runs? You test it with fake agents. Ravel has no separate command that type-checks a script or evaluates it without the start of a program. start-session evaluates the script and then runs the selected program. Thus, each test of a script is a real session. A fake agent makes that session cheap and safe, because it only sends back its arguments.

For each route, test the arguments and the result. For each refusal, test the diagnostic and absence of a managed process. Include absent optional inputs, unknown kinds and malformed local data. The examples in this chapter show success and failure at that same boundary.

You may also want to see values while the script runs. print(text) writes to the script’s standard output, and debug(text) writes to the script’s standard error. Ravel captures both streams of the Rhai worker. Then it writes the captured text on its own standard error, before the routing notice. Each line starts with config stdout: or config stderr:.

These lines are diagnostics for you. They are not the opaque result on Ravel’s standard output, which Ravel does not interpret. The managed process sends that result with end-session. With --json, the JSON routing notice holds the captured text in its diagnostics member.

The next example is print-and-parse, in ~/ravel-chapter-4/print-and-parse with a bin subdirectory. It has a third file, broken.rhai, beside config.rhai. broken.rhai is a second configuration script with a syntax error. bin/fake-agent is the same as in fixed-route. The second command block selects broken.rhai with --config broken.rhai.

Complete example: print a value from the script, then find a syntax error.

settings.json

{"skills":{"schemaVersion":1,"sources":[]}}

config.rhai

export const SCHEMA_VERSION = 4;
export const VERSION = "print-1";

fn select(request, context) {
    print("kind is " + request.kind);
    #{ status: "selected", program: "fake-agent", args: [request.prompt],
       provider: "example", model: "fake", effort: "low", reason: "print demo" }
}

broken.rhai

export const SCHEMA_VERSION = 4;
export const VERSION = "broken-1";

fn select(request, context) {
    #{ status: "selected", program: "fake-agent" args: [] }
}

  source: <scratch>/work/broken.rhai

+ joins two strings. The first command wrote the line of print on standard error, and the result hello on standard output. The second script has no comma after "fake-agent". Ravel found the error when it compiled the file, in the stage load. location: 5:50 names line 5, column 50 of broken.rhai. Ravel started no program.

The table says where to look for each outcome that a test can show:

ObservationWhat to examine
A parse failureThe source file, line and column in the diagnostic.
config_invalidThe exports, their types and the function parameter counts.
selection_threwThe property or expression at the reported location.
selection_malformedThe shape and field types of the returned map.
config_refusedThe configCode, message and remedy of your script.
selection_timeoutThe work before the selection and its whole deadline.

debug(text) also names the file, the line and the position of the call. Use --json when a program, not a person, reads the routing notice and the diagnostics.

Put each kind and its route in one table

As your configuration script gets more kinds, you may want to see each kind and its route in one place. A table in the script does this. It also makes a review of your choices easy, because each route is one line. Ravel ships an example configuration script, static.rhai, that has one such table. You can start from it. Its program, provider and model are placeholders that you replace.

Excerpt from crates/ravel/examples/static.rhai: the example’s kinds and efforts.

export const ROUTES = #{
    question: agent("low"),
    bugfix: agent("medium"),
    feature: agent("high"),
    migration: agent("xhigh"),
    architecture: agent("xhigh"),
};

These kinds and efforts belong to this example. Ravel defines neither list. A kind is the caller’s token, and only your script gives it a meaning. The helper select_route refuses an unknown kind with incomplete_mapping. When a route matches, it returns a command. The helper adds status and reason.

A smaller table can store strings directly. const declares a value that the script does not reassign. A function reads a top-level constant through global::, as the next example shows. let declares a local variable that the script can change.

The next example is route-table, in ~/ravel-chapter-4/route-table with a bin subdirectory. It has the same two file paths as fixed-route, but both files are different. This bin/fake-agent sends its first two arguments, joined by |. The three command blocks ask with the kinds question, feature and other.

Complete example: route two kinds through a local table.

settings.json

{"skills":{"schemaVersion":1,"sources":[]}}

config.rhai

export const SCHEMA_VERSION = 4;
export const VERSION = "table-1";
const EFFORTS = #{ question: "low", feature: "high" };

fn select(request, context) {
    if !global::EFFORTS.contains(request.kind) {
        return #{ status: "refused", code: "unknown_kind",
                  message: "The table has no route for this kind.",
                  remedy: "Use question or feature." };
    }
    let effort = global::EFFORTS[request.kind];
    #{ status: "selected", program: "fake-agent", args: [effort, request.prompt],
       provider: "example", model: "fake", effort: effort, reason: "table lookup" }
}

bin/fake-agent

#!/bin/sh
ravel end-session -- "$1|$2"

Command

$ cp settings.json home/.config/ravel/settings.json
$ env -i HOME="$PWD/home" TMPDIR="$PWD/tmp" PATH="$PWD/bin:$PATH" ravel start-session --config config.rhai --kind question --prompt "hello"

Standard output, without a final newline

low|hello

Exit status

0

Command

$ env -i HOME="$PWD/home" TMPDIR="$PWD/tmp" PATH="$PWD/bin:$PATH" ravel start-session --config config.rhai --kind feature --prompt "hello"

Standard output, without a final newline

high|hello

Exit status

0

Command

$ env -i HOME="$PWD/home" TMPDIR="$PWD/tmp" PATH="$PWD/bin:$PATH" ravel start-session --config config.rhai --kind other --prompt "hello"

Standard output

Standard error includes

config_refused

Exit status

3

The ! operator means logical negation. table[key] selects a property whose name comes from a variable. The earlier contains check prevents an absent property error. Rhai functions do not capture the variables around their definitions. Pass local values as parameters, or use a top-level constant through global::.

Import other Rhai files

When a configuration script grows, you may want to keep the route table or some helper functions in a separate file. You may also want two scripts to share the same helpers. An import does this. It gives a name to another module, which is another Rhai file.

import "routes" as routes; reads routes.rhai beside the file that imports it. An absolute import uses that path. Ravel appends .rhai when the path has no extension. For a symlinked script, imports start from the real file’s directory.

Imports and measured reads use different bases. An import starts from the directory of the file that imports it. A measured read starts from the caller’s directory. Thus, the same relative name can find two different files:

OperationBase for a relative path
import "routes" as routes;The directory of the file that imports it.
ravel::read_text("choice.txt")The caller’s request.cwd.
ravel::read_json("choice.json")The caller’s request.cwd.
--config config.rhaiThe caller’s working directory.

Each distinct imported module loads once per evaluation. Ravel measures the entry and each module with its canonical path, byte count and SHA-256 digest. The JSON routing notice reports those measurements in config.modules. Thus, the notice shows exactly which bytes of which files made the choice.

A missing module, import cycle or 65th distinct imported module gives config_import_failed. Ravel embeds no Rhai module of its own. Imports name files, which can be copies of the shipped examples.

The next example is imports, in ~/ravel-chapter-4/imports with a bin subdirectory. It has a third file, routes.rhai, beside config.rhai. routes.rhai is the module that config.rhai imports. bin/fake-agent is the same as in route-table. The command is the same as in fixed-route, with the prompt hello.

Complete example: call an imported function and use its exported constant.

settings.json

{"skills":{"schemaVersion":1,"sources":[]}}

config.rhai

import "routes" as routes;
export const SCHEMA_VERSION = 4;
export const VERSION = "import-1";

fn select(request, context) {
    routes::choose(request, routes::EFFORT)
}

routes.rhai

export const EFFORT = "medium";

fn choose(request, effort) {
    #{ status: "selected", program: "fake-agent", args: [effort, request.prompt],
       provider: "example", model: "fake", effort: effort, reason: "local module" }
}

Let a repository choose an arrangement

You may want one repository to get more careful work than another. For example, a repository with a production service can ask for a higher effort. But you still want to decide which arrangements exist. A checkout choice does this. The repository names one arrangement in a file, and your configuration script accepts only the names that you offer.

The shipped sample.rhai reads .ravel-choice from request.cwd, the caller’s working directory. That filename is a rule of the sample, not a Ravel configuration search rule.

The sample imports an adjacent copy of static.rhai. An absent choice file uses default. A file with exactly careful changes the selected effort to high and rebuilds the arguments. It leaves an unknown kind as a refusal.

Choice fileSample behaviour
AbsentUse default.
defaultUse the route table of static.rhai unchanged.
carefulUse the route table of static.rhai with effort high.
Empty or more than one offered nameRefuse with one_choice.
An unoffered nameRefuse with choice_unoffered.
An unreadable file or a directoryRefuse with choice_unreadable.

The sample implements this behaviour with ravel::read_text and ordinary Rhai string operations. No special choice-file API is necessary. The next example uses a smaller choice rule so you can test every branch.

The next example is checkout-choice, in ~/ravel-chapter-4/checkout-choice with a bin subdirectory. It has the same two file paths as fixed-route. bin/fake-agent has the same contents, but config.rhai is different. The example directory stands for the caller’s repository. Do not save a .ravel-choice file:

  • The first command block runs without .ravel-choice.
  • The second block first writes careful to .ravel-choice with printf.
  • The third block replaces that text with other.

Run the three blocks in order, because each block uses the file that the block before it left.

Complete example: accept careful, use a default when absent, and refuse any other text.

settings.json

{"skills":{"schemaVersion":1,"sources":[]}}

config.rhai

export const SCHEMA_VERSION = 4;
export const VERSION = "choice-1";

fn select(request, context) {
    let choice = "default";
    try {
        choice = ravel::read_text(".ravel-choice", 4096).text;
        choice.trim();
    } catch (error) {
        if error.code != "source_missing" { throw error; }
    }
    if choice != "default" && choice != "careful" {
        return #{ status: "refused", code: "choice_unoffered",
                  message: "The choice is not offered.",
                  remedy: "Write default or careful in .ravel-choice." };
    }
    let effort = if choice == "careful" { "high" } else { "low" };
    #{ status: "selected", program: "fake-agent", args: [effort],
       provider: "example", model: "fake", effort: effort, reason: "checkout choice" }
}

bin/fake-agent

#!/bin/sh
ravel end-session -- "$1"

Command

$ mkdir -p home/.config/ravel tmp && chmod +x bin/*
$ cp settings.json home/.config/ravel/settings.json
$ env -i HOME="$PWD/home" TMPDIR="$PWD/tmp" PATH="$PWD/bin:$PATH" ravel start-session --config config.rhai --kind question --prompt "hello"

Standard output, without a final newline

low

Exit status

0

Command

$ printf 'careful\n' > .ravel-choice
$ env -i HOME="$PWD/home" TMPDIR="$PWD/tmp" PATH="$PWD/bin:$PATH" ravel start-session --config config.rhai --kind question --prompt "hello"

Standard output, without a final newline

high

Exit status

0

Command

$ printf 'other\n' > .ravel-choice
$ env -i HOME="$PWD/home" TMPDIR="$PWD/tmp" PATH="$PWD/bin:$PATH" ravel start-session --config config.rhai --kind question --prompt "hello"

Standard output

Standard error includes

config_refused

Exit status

3

What happened in the three commands? Without .ravel-choice, the read threw source_missing. The script caught it and kept default, so the effort was low. With careful in the file, the effort was high. With other in the file, the script refused, and the caller received no result.

The script used this Rhai:

  • try runs a block whose ordinary error can reach catch (error).
  • throw error passes an error onwards.
  • A measured read throws a map with code and message, and a name where applicable. The script catches only the absent file here.
  • trim() removes spaces and line ends at the start and the end of the string. It changes the string in place.

A successful read returns text and source. source holds the canonical name, the byte count and sha256. The JSON routing notice records the read in reads, with the evaluation phase: load for the top level, context for load_context or selection for select.

The context budget is your limit on the bytes of selection context, and “Set bounds and environment grants in the settings” explains it. The default read bound is the smaller of 65,536 bytes and the context budget. The optional second argument changes it, up to the context budget. The example gives 4096 bytes. A choice is one short word, so a small bound is enough.

Read failureCan the script catch it?
source_missing, source_not_regular, source_unreadable, source_not_utf8Yes.
source_invalid_json from read_jsonYes.
source_too_large, too_many_sourcesNo. They end evaluation.
A malformed host requestNo. It ends evaluation as host_request_invalid.

Supply selection context

You may want facts about the task to change the choice. For example, a large change can go to a higher effort, or a task with a security label can go to a particular model. Selection context carries such facts to select.

The book uses the word “context” for three different things. This section is about the second:

NameWhat it isWho uses it
Session-result contextThe path in RAVEL_EXIT_FILE, through which end-session sends the resultThe managed process. The configuration script never receives it.
Selection contextA JSON document of data for the choiceselect, as its second argument
The caller’s working directoryThe directory of the request, in request.cwdThe script, for measured reads and relative program paths

Selection context is data for the decision, not authority to execute a command. The caller can supply a JSON context document with --context PATH. Without a loader, select receives that document with its measured source, or () when no document exists.

Why write a loader? You may want facts that the caller does not send, such as a file in the caller’s repository. A load_context(request) function can assemble a different document. Ravel calls it before select.

The loader can read the caller’s input at request.context and read local sources. Ravel checks its return value before it calls select. A loader that returns () gives context_invalid. A loader can also return a refusal, in which case select never runs.

Document fieldPurpose
schemaVersionRequired integer 1.
summaryOptional text for the configuration script.
factsOptional map of JSON values.
assessmentsOptional map of judgements, each with by and value.
sourcesOptional attributed source records, each with name and a digest or version.
acceptanceCriteriaOptional array of strings that state the caller’s criteria.

Ravel adds measured to the delivered document. The caller and loader do not supply that field themselves. It lists the caller’s context file first, then the reads made by the loader. A read in select appears in the routing notice but changes no delivered context.

Attributed sources describe claims. measured records the bytes Ravel read. Neither measurement establishes that a claim is true.

The next example is selection-context, in ~/ravel-chapter-4/selection-context with a bin subdirectory. It has a third file, task.json, beside config.rhai. task.json holds the task data that the loader reads from the caller’s working directory. This bin/fake-agent sends its first three arguments, joined by |. The command uses the kind review.

Complete example: read task data, build selection context, and use it in the selection.

settings.json

{"skills":{"schemaVersion":1,"sources":[]}}

config.rhai

export const SCHEMA_VERSION = 4;
export const VERSION = "context-1";

fn load_context(request) {
    let task = ravel::read_json("task.json");
    #{ schemaVersion: 1, summary: task.value.title,
       facts: #{ effort: task.value.effort } }
}

fn select(request, context) {
    #{ status: "selected", program: "fake-agent",
       args: [context.summary, context.facts.effort,
              context.measured.len().to_string()],
       provider: "example", model: "fake", effort: context.facts.effort,
       reason: "task facts" }
}

task.json

{"title":"Review the change","effort":"high"}

bin/fake-agent

#!/bin/sh
ravel end-session -- "$1|$2|$3"

Command

$ mkdir -p home/.config/ravel tmp && chmod +x bin/*
$ cp settings.json home/.config/ravel/settings.json
$ env -i HOME="$PWD/home" TMPDIR="$PWD/tmp" PATH="$PWD/bin:$PATH" ravel start-session --config config.rhai --kind review --prompt "hello"

Standard output, without a final newline

Exit status

0

Follow the data from the file to the result:

OrderWho actsWhat happens in this example
1load_contextReads task.json with ravel::read_json and builds a document with a summary and one fact
2The Ravel CLIChecks the document and adds measured, with one record for task.json
3selectPuts the summary, the effort and the count of measured records in args
4fake-agentSends `Review the change

read_json returns value and source. The loader chooses which values enter the document. .len() counts the measured records, and .to_string() makes a string for args. Ravel refuses an integer in that array, because each argument of a program is a string.

The context budget applies to the caller’s file and to the delivered JSON, with measured. The default is 262,144 bytes. At most 256 measured sources can contribute to one evaluation. A file counts when its bytes arrive, even if the bytes are not valid JSON or UTF-8. An absent file does not consume a source.

Check JSON with the data helpers

Your configuration script reads JSON from files, and later from models. You may want to be sure that this JSON has the shape that you expect before the script acts on it. A model can give an answer with a wrong field or a value that you did not offer. The data helpers let the script parse, check and write JSON strictly.

Ravel supplies strict JSON operations in the ravel namespace. They run inside the Rhai worker and send no request to the Ravel CLI. Use them for data that crosses between the script and another program. The Rhai built-in parse_json accepts syntax that strict JSON rejects. Its to_json can produce string escapes that are not JSON.

HelperUse
ravel::json_parse(text)Parse strict JSON into a Rhai value.
ravel::json_encode(value)Encode JSON with sorted object keys and no whitespace.
ravel::json_check(value, schema)Check a JSON Schema subset and return the value unchanged.
ravel::uuid_v4()Generate a random version-4 UUID string.
ravel::path_resolve(base, path)Join a path to an absolute base and normalise . and ...
ravel::path_parent(path)Return the parent of an absolute path.

The path helpers read no file and resolve no symlink. An absolute second path replaces the base in path_resolve. A relative base or relative path_parent input throws path_not_absolute. Use a measured read when you also need to establish what a file contains.

Values at the boundary are JSON values: null, booleans, numbers, strings, arrays and maps. Rhai’s () represents null. Characters, function pointers, ranges and other non-JSON types do not cross. Ravel also refuses non-finite numbers. Arrays and maps nest at most 100 levels. A JSON integer outside the signed 64-bit range becomes a float.

The next example is data-helpers, in ~/ravel-chapter-4/data-helpers with a bin subdirectory. It has the same two file paths as fixed-route. bin/fake-agent has the same contents, but config.rhai is different. The command is the same as in fixed-route, with the prompt hello.

Complete example: check data, handle a failed check, and encode the result.

settings.json

{"skills":{"schemaVersion":1,"sources":[]}}

config.rhai

export const SCHEMA_VERSION = 4;
export const VERSION = "data-1";

fn select(request, context) {
    let schema = #{ type: "object",
                    properties: #{ effort: #{ type: "string", enum: ["low", "high"] } },
                    required: ["effort"], additionalProperties: false };
    let good = ravel::json_check(ravel::json_parse("{\"effort\":\"high\"}"), schema);
    let failure = "none";
    try { ravel::json_check(#{ effort: "other" }, schema); }
    catch (error) { failure = error.code; }
    let values = [];
    for value in [good.effort, failure] { values.push(value); }
    #{ status: "selected", program: "fake-agent", args: [ravel::json_encode(values)],
       provider: "example", model: "fake", effort: good.effort, reason: "checked data" }
}

bin/fake-agent

#!/bin/sh
ravel end-session -- "$1"

Command

$ mkdir -p home/.config/ravel tmp && chmod +x bin/*
$ cp settings.json home/.config/ravel/settings.json
$ env -i HOME="$PWD/home" TMPDIR="$PWD/tmp" PATH="$PWD/bin:$PATH" ravel start-session --config config.rhai --kind question --prompt "hello"

Standard output, without a final newline

["high","json_mismatch"]

Exit status

0

What did the script do? It parsed {"effort":"high"} and checked it against the schema, which passed. Then it checked #{ effort: "other" }, which failed with the code json_mismatch. The script caught that code and sent both values to the fake agent as one JSON array.

for value in array visits each element. push adds an element to the array. The schema requires a string whose value is low or high. It also rejects properties other than effort. A schema checks shape and permitted values. The script still decides their meaning.

The supported schema keywords are type, properties, required, additionalProperties, items, enum, title and description. The last two are descriptive only. additionalProperties is a boolean, and enum holds scalar JSON values. Unknown keywords and schema depth beyond 32 levels throw schema_invalid. A mismatch throws json_mismatch. Its message names the location and can include property names, but omits property values.

json_parse throws json_invalid for invalid JSON or excessive depth. json_encode and json_check throw json_unencodable for a non-JSON value. Ravel disables eval, so a model’s answer cannot become code through that function. Keep model answers as data that the script checks and interprets.

Set bounds and environment grants in the settings

You may want to give a slow configuration script more time, or let the script read an environment variable of the caller. You may also want to name the harnesses that the script can run. Your settings do each of these things. They are the second part of your configuration, in a JSON file next to the default script.

Ravel reads the settings from $HOME/.config/ravel/settings.json. It reads this file also when --config selects another script. Thus, a script that you copy to another place does not carry its own settings. An absent file sets nothing. Each key is optional, and Ravel refuses unknown keys.

KeyMeaning and range
timeoutMsWhole evaluation deadline, 1,000–600,000 ms. Default: 30,000.
contextBytesContext budget, 1–8,388,608 bytes. Default: 262,144.
configEnvAdditional caller environment names that ravel::env can read.
harnessesNamed programs available to ravel::run_harness.
budgetsAn object whose optional harnessRuns is an integer from 0 to 16. Default: 0.
skillsLocal skill sources and mandatory guidance. An empty sources array disables delivery. See skill settings.

The caller can also change two bounds for one session. --timeout-ms and --context-bytes replace the settings of those bounds. Ravel checks the settings first, so a flag cannot override an invalid file. --config-env NAME adds a grant to those in configEnv. Duplicate grants count once.

request.limits holds selectionMs, contextBytes, sourceBytes, sources, messageBytes, diagnosticsBytes and harnessRuns. These values describe the effective bounds. A change to the map cannot raise them. ravel::remaining() reports what remains of the evaluation: time in ms, harnessRuns and sources.

The whole deadline covers module loads, the context loader, selection and all host calls. It does not bound the final managed process. At expiry, Ravel reports selection_timeout, exit status 124. Cancellation or failed harness cleanup can take precedence. Cleanup takes additional time. Other fixed bounds include 1 MiB worker messages and 262,144 bytes of diagnostics from the script.

Bound reachedEffect
Whole deadlineEnd evaluation and start no managed process.
Source, context, message or diagnostics boundEnd evaluation and start no managed process.
Engine operation, depth or data boundEnd evaluation with script_limit.
Worker heap bound of 1 GiBEnd evaluation with memory_limit.
Harness-run budgetEnd evaluation with harness_budget_exhausted.
One harness run’s deadline or output boundReturn a status to the script if the whole evaluation can continue.

The script cannot catch an evaluation bound with try. This rule also applies inside a closure passed to an array or map helper. A deadline or output bound of one harness run is different: it returns data, as the next section explains.

Why would a configuration script read an environment variable? The caller can set a variable, for example a name for the effort that it wants, and the script can use it in the choice.

But the Rhai worker has an empty environment. Thus, the script asks the Ravel CLI: ravel::env is a host capability that returns the caller’s value of one variable. It permits HOME, PATH, TMPDIR, LANG, LC_* and explicit grants. An unset permitted name returns (). An ungranted name throws env_not_granted, even if it is unset. A value outside UTF-8 throws env_not_utf8.

The next example is settings-grants, in ~/ravel-chapter-4/settings-grants with a bin subdirectory. It has a third file, settings.json, at the top of the example directory. This bin/fake-agent sends its first three arguments, joined by |. The command block has three lines:

  • The first line prepares the directories and executable files.
  • The second line copies settings.json into home/.config/ravel/. Ravel reads the settings only from that path in the private home.
  • The third line sets BOOK_EFFORT and BOOK_HIDDEN after env -i, and adds --timeout-ms 10000.

Complete example: grant one variable and observe a CLI bound replacement. The command installs settings only in the example’s private home.

settings.json

{"timeoutMs":5000,"contextBytes":4096,"configEnv":["BOOK_EFFORT"],"skills":{"schemaVersion":1,"sources":[]}}

config.rhai

export const SCHEMA_VERSION = 4;
export const VERSION = "settings-1";

fn select(request, context) {
    let effort = ravel::env("BOOK_EFFORT");
    let hidden = "none";
    try { ravel::env("BOOK_HIDDEN"); }
    catch (error) { hidden = error.code; }
    #{ status: "selected", program: "fake-agent",
       args: [effort, request.limits.selectionMs.to_string(), hidden],
       provider: "example", model: "fake", effort: effort, reason: "settings" }
}

bin/fake-agent

#!/bin/sh
ravel end-session -- "$1|$2|$3"

Command

$ mkdir -p home tmp && chmod +x bin/*
$ mkdir -p home/.config/ravel && cp settings.json home/.config/ravel/settings.json
$ env -i HOME="$PWD/home" TMPDIR="$PWD/tmp" BOOK_EFFORT=high BOOK_HIDDEN=unused PATH="$PWD/bin:$PATH" ravel start-session --config config.rhai --kind question --timeout-ms 10000 --prompt "hello"

Standard output, without a final newline

high|10000|env_not_granted

Exit status

0

What happened in this example? The settings granted BOOK_EFFORT, so ravel::env returned high. The caller also set BOOK_HIDDEN, but the settings did not grant it. Thus, ravel::env threw env_not_granted, and the script caught the code. The caller’s --timeout-ms 10000 replaced the timeoutMs of 5000 in the settings, so request.limits.selectionMs was 10000.

Environment grants control this helper, not the environment of a managed process or harness run. Those programs receive the caller’s environment, with the control variables of an enclosing session or driver removed or replaced. Ravel refuses grants for its reserved variables and dynamic-loader variables. The reference lists the reserved classes.

A measured read can reach files that the user can read. These grants therefore do not create a filesystem sandbox for the script. You control the configuration script and choose which data it reads.

Run a harness during evaluation

You may want an LLM to choose the best harness, model, effort and prompt additions for a kind and a prompt. You can do this with a harness run in your configuration script. A harness run is one run of a program from your harnesses settings, during evaluation. The script starts it with the host capability ravel::run_harness. Then the script reads the output of the run as data and makes its own choice.

How does the answer get back to the script? Follow one harness run:

OrderWho actsWhat happens
1The configuration scriptCalls ravel::run_harness with the name of a settings entry, the arguments and the text for standard input
2The Rhai workerSends the request to the Ravel CLI and waits
3The Ravel CLIFinds the entry’s program, checks the run budget and starts the program in a new POSIX session with its own result channel
4The harness runReads its standard input, writes its answer on standard output and exits
5The Ravel CLIStops the processes that remain in the group of the run, completes cleanup and sends a map back to the worker
6The configuration scriptReads status, exitCode and stdout from the map, then continues

The harness run returns its answer to the script before Ravel starts the final managed process.

Figure: The evaluation harness run and the managed process are two separate programs that Ravel starts.

Open the evaluation harness sequence at full size.

A result channel is a private temporary directory with a path for one result file. The path goes to the program in RAVEL_EXIT_FILE. A harness run gets a result channel of its own. Thus, a run that uses end-session sends its value to the script, not to the session.

The answer goes back to the script, not to the caller. The caller sees the script’s selection in the routing notice and receives the final session’s result after cleanup. The JSON routing notice also records each harness run, but not its prompt or its answer.

Fragment: the harnessRuns member of the JSON routing notice, from a real run of the next section’s Claude Code example with --json. This block omits the other members of the document. The value of elapsedMs changes in each run.

{"harnessRuns":[{"elapsedMs":314,"exitCode":0,"harness":"claude","sentResult":false,"signal":null,"status":"exited"}]}

The record names the settings entry claude. The run exited with status 0 after 314 milliseconds, and no signal stopped it. sentResult: false says that the run did not use end-session. It wrote its answer on standard output.

A harness entry fixes the program that a host call can run. Its name has 1–64 lowercase letters, digits or dashes. Its first character is a letter or digit. The entry has a required program and optional capabilities labels. Ravel gives those labels no meaning.

Fragment: settings for a harness run. This block omits the configuration script and the program.

{
  "harnesses": {
    "classifier": {"program":"my-classifier","capabilities":["classify"]}
  },
  "budgets": {"harnessRuns":1}
}

ravel::harnesses() reports the entries in name order and runs no program. Each map has name, program, available, resolved, reason and capabilities. An available program has a resolved path and reason: (). An unavailable one has resolved: () and a reason of not_found or not_executable. Discovery reports availability, not a recommendation or live model list.

ravel::run_harness accepts this request:

FieldMeaning
harnessRequired name of a settings entry.
argsRequired array of argument strings, without NUL.
stdinOptional text for standard input. Without it, input is at end of file.
timeoutMsOptional positive integer in milliseconds, capped by the time left for evaluation.

The call is synchronous: the script waits until the run ends. Only one host call runs at a time.

The run starts in request.cwd, without a terminal, in a new POSIX session. A POSIX session is the operating system’s group of process groups, which setsid makes. It is not a Ravel session. It receives the environment of a managed process. Ravel does not restrict its filesystem access, as chapter 2 states. The arguments that the script supplies select the sandbox of the harness, such as codex exec --sandbox read-only.

The script supplies every argument and chooses where the prompt goes. Ravel builds no argument that is specific to a harness.

The returned map describes the completed run:

FieldValue
statusexited, signalled, timed_out, output_limit or unavailable.
exitCodeAn integer or ().
signalA signal name or ().
stdout, stderrCaptured text. Invalid UTF-8 becomes the replacement character.
resultThe value sent by the run with end-session, or ().
elapsedMsElapsed milliseconds.
error(), or a map with code and message for an unavailable run.

A run can finish by natural exit and return standard output to the script. It does not have to call end-session. If it does, that result belongs only to this run. It cannot complete the outer session. The final managed process later receives a different result channel.

An unavailable run reports unknown_harness, harness_not_found, harness_not_executable or harness_launch_failed and consumes no run budget. Each run that starts consumes one. Each output stream has a 1 MiB bound. A per-run timeout or output overflow stops the run and returns its status. A cleanup failure ends evaluation as harness_cleanup_failed, exit status 5.

Can a harness run start a Ravel session of its own? No. Ravel puts the marker RAVEL_CONFIG_RUN in the environment of each harness run, and each child of the run inherits it. start-session finds the marker and refuses as config_reentry_refused, before it reads the settings or a configuration script. Thus, a harness run cannot start a second evaluation inside the first.

Do you need the route of another kind during evaluation? Call that route’s function in your script directly. The final managed process never receives the marker, so a session that the final harness nests stays allowed.

The marker does not protect against code that removes it. A descendant that leaves the process group is outside group cleanup.

Ask a model through Claude Code

Do you want an LLM in Claude Code to help your configuration script choose? This section gives the recipe. An LLM call is a harness run that asks a model a question. Claude Code runs the call under your own sign-in, so the call can use your subscription. Ravel stores no model credential.

The script reads the answer and decides what it means. A model can give an unexpected answer, so the script asks for structured output. Structured output is an answer in JSON, in a shape that a JSON Schema describes. The script then checks the answer against the same schema with ravel::json_check. The fake below uses the same arguments as a real call, but it returns a fixed answer.

The recipe uses Claude Code 2.1.310. The author checked the installed help on 2026-10-26. The repository’s real smoke call also succeeded with that version. Its arguments and response field are the basis of this example.

ArgumentPurpose
-pPrint an answer and exit.
--model haikuSelect the model for this call.
--tools ""Give the call no tools.
--no-session-persistenceDo not retain the session in local history.
--output-format jsonReturn a JSON document.
--json-schema TEXTRequest an answer of this shape, in structured_output.

The prompt goes to stdin, not into args, as in the recorded real call. Thus, the arguments stay the same for each prompt. The script checks the run status and exit code before it parses standard output. It then checks structured_output against the same schema. The fixed answer permits only yes or no. It never supplies a program name or arbitrary command argument.

The next example is claude-question, in ~/ravel-chapter-4/claude-question with a bin subdirectory. It has four files:

Path in claude-questionPurpose
settings.jsonNames the harness entry claude and permits one harness run. The first command block copies it into the private home.
config.rhaiAsks the harness run a question, checks the answer and selects fake-agent
bin/claudeA fake Claude Code. bin comes first on the search path, so a real Claude Code does not run.
bin/fake-agentThe same fake agent as in fixed-route

The fake claude writes two files when it runs: call.args and call.prompt. The first command block also writes the result to a file, result. Then it prints the three files. The second command block first makes an empty file, invalid-answer, with touch. That file makes the fake give an answer that the schema does not permit.

Complete example: ask through a fake Claude Code and refuse an invalid answer.

settings.json

{"harnesses":{"claude":{"program":"claude"}},"budgets":{"harnessRuns":1},"skills":{"schemaVersion":1,"sources":[]}}

config.rhai

export const SCHEMA_VERSION = 4;
export const VERSION = "claude-question-1";

fn refuse() {
    #{ status: "refused", code: "no_answer", message: "The call gave no valid answer.",
       remedy: "Check the harness and its output." }
}

fn select(request, context) {
    let schema = #{ type: "object",
                    properties: #{ prime: #{ type: "string", enum: ["yes", "no"] } },
                    required: ["prime"], additionalProperties: false };
    let run = ravel::run_harness(#{
        harness: "claude",
        args: ["-p", "--model", "haiku", "--tools", "", "--no-session-persistence",
               "--output-format", "json", "--json-schema", ravel::json_encode(schema)],
        stdin: "Is 17 a prime number? Answer with the JSON object only.",
        timeoutMs: 20000,
    });
    if run.status != "exited" || run.exitCode != 0 { return refuse(); }
    let answer = ();
    try {
        let document = ravel::json_parse(run.stdout);
        if type_of(document) != "map" || !document.contains("structured_output") {
            return refuse();
        }
        answer = ravel::json_check(document.structured_output, schema);
    } catch (error) { return refuse(); }
    let result = if answer.prime == "yes" { "prime" } else { "not-prime" };
    #{ status: "selected", program: "fake-agent", args: [result],
       provider: "example", model: "fake", effort: "low", reason: "checked model answer" }
}

bin/claude

#!/bin/sh
printf '%s\n' "$@" > call.args
cat > call.prompt
if test -f invalid-answer; then
    printf '%s\n' '{"structured_output":{"prime":"perhaps"}}'
else
    printf '%s\n' '{"structured_output":{"prime":"yes"}}'
fi

ravel end-session -- "$1"

config_refused

Exit status
