Follow the first command block from the script to the result:

OrderWho actsWhat happens in this example
1The configuration scriptCalls ravel::run_harness for the entry claude, with the arguments in the table above and the question on stdin
2The fake claudeWrites its arguments to call.args and its standard input to call.prompt. Then it prints {"structured_output":{"prime":"yes"}}.
3The Ravel CLIGives the script a map with status: "exited", exitCode: 0 and that line in stdout
4The configuration scriptParses stdout, checks structured_output against the schema and selects fake-agent with the argument prime
5fake-agentSends prime with end-session, and prime becomes the result of ravel start-session

The first command block then prints call.args, call.prompt and the result. Thus, the output shows each argument that a real Claude Code would receive. In the second command, the fake answers perhaps. The schema permits only yes or no, so the script refuses.

type_of reports the Rhai type name. The map check and contains test protect the read of structured_output. The schema rejects perhaps, and the script returns its own refusal. A different script could choose a fixed fallback after an unusable answer. It still cannot catch an exhausted evaluation budget. An evaluation budget is a limit that you set in the settings for one evaluation, such as the number of harness runs in budgets.harnessRuns.

The shipped llm.rhai asks Claude Code to classify the caller’s prompt. It sends request.originalPrompt to the classifier. It limits the answer to keys from the route table of static.rhai. After an unusable answer, it uses the caller’s kind instead. It imports static.rhai, so both files must be present.

Ask a model through Codex

Do you use Codex and not Claude Code? The recipe is almost the same. The script asks for structured output, reads the answer from standard output and checks it with the same schema.

One difference matters. Codex accepts its structured-output schema as a file path, not as text. Keep that file where the run can read it. The script cannot write the schema file itself. The example places it in the caller’s working directory and reads the same file with ravel::read_json.

The recipe uses Codex CLI 0.164.0. The author checked the installed help on 2026-10-26. The repository’s real smoke call succeeded with that version and these arguments. The final message on standard output is the JSON answer. This recipe does not use --json, which selects JSON Lines events instead.

ArgumentPurpose
execRun noninteractively.
--ephemeralDo not retain the session in local history.
--skip-git-repo-checkPermit the call outside a Git repository.
--sandbox read-onlySelect the harness’s read-only sandbox.
-c 'model_reasoning_effort="low"'Set the effort for this call.
--output-schema PATHRead the answer schema from a file.
-Read the prompt from standard input.

The next example is codex-question, in ~/ravel-chapter-4/codex-question with a bin subdirectory. It has the shape of claude-question, with two differences:

  • settings.json names the harness entry codex, and bin/codex is a fake Codex in place of bin/claude.
  • A fifth file, answer-schema.json, holds the schema of the answer. The configuration script reads it, and the fake Codex receives its path.

The second command block makes an empty file, failed-call, with touch. That file makes the fake Codex fail. In the output of the first block, <scratch>/work stands for the example’s directory. In your shell, the line shows the absolute path of your codex-question directory.

Complete example: ask through a fake Codex, check the schema and inspect every argument.

settings.json

{"harnesses":{"codex":{"program":"codex"}},"budgets":{"harnessRuns":1},"skills":{"schemaVersion":1,"sources":[]}}

answer-schema.json

{"type":"object","properties":{"prime":{"type":"string","enum":["yes","no"]}},"required":["prime"],"additionalProperties":false}

config.rhai

export const SCHEMA_VERSION = 4;
export const VERSION = "codex-question-1";

fn refuse() {
    #{ status: "refused", code: "no_answer", message: "The call gave no valid answer.",
       remedy: "Check the harness and its output." }
}

fn select(request, context) {
    let schema = ravel::read_json("answer-schema.json").value;
    let run = ravel::run_harness(#{
        harness: "codex",
        args: ["exec", "--ephemeral", "--skip-git-repo-check", "--sandbox", "read-only",
               "-c", "model_reasoning_effort=\"low\"", "--output-schema",
               ravel::path_resolve(request.cwd, "answer-schema.json"), "-"],
        stdin: "Is 17 a prime number? Answer with the JSON object only.",
        timeoutMs: 20000,
    });
    if run.status != "exited" || run.exitCode != 0 { return refuse(); }
    let answer = ();
    try { answer = ravel::json_check(ravel::json_parse(run.stdout), schema); }
    catch (error) { return refuse(); }
    let result = if answer.prime == "yes" { "prime" } else { "not-prime" };
    #{ status: "selected", program: "fake-agent", args: [result],
       provider: "example", model: "fake", effort: "low", reason: "checked model answer" }
}

    printf '%s\n' 'The fake call failed.' >&2

fi
printf '%s\n' '{"prime":"yes"}'

Command

$ touch failed-call
$ env -i HOME="$PWD/home" TMPDIR="$PWD/tmp" PATH="$PWD/bin:$PATH" ravel start-session --config config.rhai --kind question --prompt "hello"

Standard output

Standard error includes

config_refused

Exit status

3

In the first command block, the fake codex printed {"prime":"yes"}. The script checked that answer against the schema from answer-schema.json, and fake-agent sent prime as the result. The output also shows the full path of the schema file. The script made that path from request.cwd, so Codex can find the file when the caller runs the example from its directory.

In the second command block, the fake failed. The failed run returns status: "exited" and exitCode: 1 to the script. The script then refuses. Ravel itself assigns no meaning to the harness’s text. A retry would need another run in the budget and time before the whole deadline.

The fake examples prove the arguments, prompt placement and the checks of each answer. They do not prove that a real harness accepts the arguments. Two opt-in tasks in a Ravel checkout prove the recipes with real calls:

TaskWhat it provesWhat it needs
task harness:smoke:claudeThe Claude Code recipe, with a real callAn installed Claude Code with the owner’s sign-in. Task and the Rust toolchain, because the task builds Ravel from the checkout. The call spends subscription usage.
task harness:smoke:codexThe Codex recipe, with a real callAn installed Codex with the owner’s sign-in. Task and the Rust toolchain, because the task builds Ravel from the checkout. The call spends subscription usage.

Run the appropriate task in the root directory of the checkout after a harness upgrade. Chapter 10, “Prepare a checkout”, gives the tools that a checkout needs.

Those tasks use a temporary home with its own configuration. The home holds one link to your real home: the sign-in store of the harness.

HarnessThe one link to your real home
Claude Code on macOSLibrary/Keychains
Claude Code on Linux.claude/.credentials.json
Codex.codex/auth.json. The task sets CODEX_HOME to the temporary .codex

The tasks do not need edits to your own settings.

A Claude Code task stops before it runs Claude Code if your environment holds CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR, CLAUDE_SECURESTORAGE_CONFIG_DIR or one of the three CLAUDE_CODE_PLUGIN_* location variables. A Codex task stops if CODEX_HOME names a directory in your real home. Run the task again without that variable.

The harness owns its authentication. See the Claude Code setup documentation and Codex authentication documentation for account setup. This chapter assumes an installed, signed-in harness for real calls. Ravel reports no token usage. Its notice lists run metadata, not the prompt or answer. A script that copies an answer into diagnostics or delivered context exposes it there.

Select Claude Code or Codex for the caller’s work

The harness runs above answer a question for the configuration script. Usually, you also want a harness to do the caller’s work, such as the review of chapter 1. Then the harness is the managed process: the program that Ravel starts and supervises for the session. An interactive harness waits for more input when its LLM completes the task. Thus, the caller’s prompt tells the LLM to run ravel end-session RESULT, and that command ends the session.

A final interactive harness is the managed process, not a harness run. Select its program and args directly. This selection needs no harnesses entry or harness-run budget.

The caller’s prompt gives the complete task. In the review of chapter 1, the prompt named the two files to review. It gave two permitted results, approved and changes-needed, and the condition for each result. It told the LLM to send the result with ravel end-session. The orchestrator then acted on each result.

These values are the caller’s convention. Ravel assigns them no meaning, and it returns the value unchanged after cleanup.

Your script passes request.prompt to the harness. With skill delivery enabled, that field also holds discovery instructions before the caller’s unchanged text. request.originalPrompt always holds only the caller’s text. Ravel does not add the instruction to end the session.

How does ravel end-session find the session? Ravel puts the path of the session’s result file in the RAVEL_EXIT_FILE environment variable of the harness. The harness runs each command of the LLM in a child process, for example a shell. Each child inherits RAVEL_EXIT_FILE from its parent unless the parent changes it. Thus, ravel end-session finds the path in its own environment. Chapter 1, “How end-session finds its session”, shows the paths of a real nested run.

Follow the review with a real Claude Code, from the selection to the result:

OrderWho actsWhat happens in the review
1The configuration scriptSelects claude with the caller’s prompt as its last argument
2The Ravel CLIWrites the routing notice, puts the path of the result file in RAVEL_EXIT_FILE and starts claude
3The LLM in Claude CodeReviews the two files, finds a problem and writes review-notes.md. Then it asks Claude Code to run ravel end-session changes-needed.
4ravel end-sessionFinds the path in RAVEL_EXIT_FILE, writes changes-needed there and asks Ravel to end the session
5The Ravel CLIStops Claude Code and the rest of its process group, then completes cleanup
6The Ravel CLIWrites changes-needed on standard output. It is the result of ravel start-session.
7The orchestratorReads review-notes.md, then requests a session of the kind fix
Intended useAction of the configuration scriptHow it completes
Ask a question before selectionCall ravel::run_harness with noninteractive arguments.Read its output, status or run result.
Run an interactive LLM sessionReturn a selected map for the harness.A program in the session calls end-session.

The noninteractive recipes above are for the first row. The next example uses interactive arguments from the same installed versions. The author checked those arguments against their help on 2026-10-26. Both routes use the harness’s configured model default. Only the Claude Code route sets an explicit effort.

-- separates the prompt from options, even when its first character is -. Codex’s --no-daemon makes Codex run without its shared background server. Ravel supervises the selected process group. A process that leaves that group is outside its cleanup.

The next example is interactive-routes, in ~/ravel-chapter-4/interactive-routes with a bin subdirectory. It has three files: config.rhai, a fake Claude Code at bin/claude and a fake Codex at bin/codex. bin comes first on the search path, so neither real harness runs. Each fake sends all its arguments as the result, one per line, each in < and >.

The first command block is a for loop over two kinds, claude-demo and codex-demo. Type it as one command over five lines. Your shell shows a continuation prompt for the second to fifth lines. The second block asks with the kind other.

Complete example: pass an instruction to end the session to two fake interactive harnesses.

settings.json

{"skills":{"schemaVersion":1,"sources":[]}}

config.rhai

export const SCHEMA_VERSION = 4;
export const VERSION = "interactive-1";

fn select(request, context) {
    let program = "";
    let args = [];
    let effort = "configured-default";
    if request.kind == "claude-demo" {
        program = "claude";
        effort = "medium";
        args = ["--effort", effort, "--", request.prompt];
    } else if request.kind == "codex-demo" {
        program = "codex";
        args = ["--no-daemon", "--no-alt-screen", "--sandbox", "workspace-write",
                "--ask-for-approval", "on-request", "--", request.prompt];
    } else {
        return #{ status: "refused", code: "unsupported_kind",
                  message: "No interactive route matches this kind.",
                  remedy: "Use claude-demo or codex-demo." };
    }
    #{ status: "selected", program: program, args: args,
       provider: "agent-configured", model: "configured-default",
       effort: effort, reason: "attended harness route" }
}

bin/claude

#!/bin/sh
set -eu
result=$(printf '<%s>\n' "$@")
ravel end-session -- "$result"

bin/codex

#!/bin/sh
set -eu
result=$(printf '<%s>\n' "$@")
ravel end-session -- "$result"

Command

$ mkdir -p home/.config/ravel tmp && chmod +x bin/*
$ cp settings.json home/.config/ravel/settings.json
$ for kind in claude-demo codex-demo; do
    printf '%s\n' "$kind"
    env -i HOME="$PWD/home" TMPDIR="$PWD/tmp" PATH="$PWD/bin:$PATH" ravel start-session --config config.rhai --kind "$kind" --prompt 'Review the change. If it is correct, run ravel end-session approved. Otherwise, run ravel end-session changes-needed.' || break
    printf '\n'
  done

<workspace-write>
<--ask-for-approval>

<-->
<Review the change. If it is correct, run ravel end-session approved. Otherwise, run ravel end-session changes-needed.>

Exit status

0

Command

$ env -i HOME="$PWD/home" TMPDIR="$PWD/tmp" PATH="$PWD/bin:$PATH" ravel start-session --config config.rhai --kind other --prompt "hello"

config_refused

Exit status

3

The fakes return their arguments. They do not act on the prompt. This check establishes prompt delivery, not whether an LLM obeys the instruction. Real interactive harnesses need a foreground terminal. Ravel can lend that terminal to the managed process and recover it afterwards.

The harness needs permission to run ravel and write its inherited result path. Keep ravel on its PATH, or name the installed absolute path in the prompt. The harness’s sandbox and approvals must permit these actions, and the operating system’s file permissions must allow the result write. A natural exit without end-session, even exit status 0, returns no result to the caller.

Copy a configuration script and its files

You may want to move a configuration script to another account, or start from one of the example scripts that Ravel ships. A script can be more than one file. It can import modules, read data files and need settings entries.

Copy a configuration script with its imported modules and data files. Preserve relative import paths, and check paths relative to request.cwd separately. Copy only the settings entries and environment grants that the new script needs. The script does not carry its own settings file through --config.

The shipped examples are under share/ravel/examples/ in a Ravel 2.0.0 installation. In a checkout, their source is crates/ravel/examples/. The examples are editable copies, not embedded imports.

ExampleFiles and purpose
static.rhaiA fixed route table with placeholder commands and labels.
sample.rhaiA checkout choice. It also needs static.rhai.
llm.rhaiAn LLM-assisted choice through Claude Code. It needs static.rhai and harness settings.

Before real use, replace placeholder commands and test every route with fake agents. Check that the script refuses unknown kinds as intended. Check that absent data and unsuccessful harness runs produce the outcome you chose. Then put the script at $HOME/.config/ravel/config.rhai, or select it with --config.

Review your configuration script before real use

In short, the caller asks with a kind and a prompt. Ravel evaluates your Rhai configuration script in the Rhai worker, a second copy of the ravel executable. The script can read measured files, read granted environment variables and run harnesses through ravel:: functions. Each of these host capabilities asks the Ravel CLI to do the work, within the limits of your settings. Then select returns a selection or a refusal.

After a selection, Ravel reaps the worker and starts the selected program as the managed process. Ravel writes the routing notice on the caller’s standard error. The text notice comes before the start of the program, and the JSON notice follows the supervision attempt. The program sends a result with ravel end-session, as the caller’s prompt tells it. After cleanup, that result becomes the result of ravel start-session, unchanged. The caller gives the result its meaning.

Before you use a configuration script with real harnesses, test each of these points with fake agents:

Point to testWhat a good script does
Each routeStarts the program with the arguments that you expect, and the fake agent sends them back
Each unknown kindRefuses with a code, a message and a remedy that the caller can act on
Absent optional inputsGives the outcome that you chose, without an error from an absent map property
Absent or malformed data filesRefuses or uses a default, as you chose
Each harness runChecks the status, the exit code and the answer before the script uses the answer
An unusable answer from a modelRefuses or uses a fixed fallback, as you chose
The interactive routesPass the caller’s prompt, with its instruction to run ravel end-session

Run sessions

Chapter 1 followed one code review. An orchestrator, a program that runs LLM work as steps of its own work, wanted an LLM to review the last commit. It wrote a prompt file, review-prompt.txt. The prompt told the LLM to run ravel end-session approved if it found no problem. If it found a problem, the LLM wrote review-notes.md and ran ravel end-session changes-needed. Then the orchestrator ran this command:

Fragment: the request of chapter 1’s review. This block omits the code of the orchestrator that runs the command.

ravel start-session --kind review --prompt-file review-prompt.txt

The LLM found a problem. Ravel wrote changes-needed on standard output, and ravel start-session exited with status 0. Then the orchestrator read review-notes.md and requested a session of the kind fix.

Now you want to write the orchestrator’s side of that review, or of your own work. What does your program give to Ravel? What does each output and each exit status mean, and what does your program do next? This chapter answers these questions with the review and with other real examples.

The chapter also shows how to read the routing notice. That notice tells the caller which harness, model and effort the configuration script chose. It shows how to run a session inside another session. It also states what the managed process receives from Ravel.

First, the words that this chapter uses on every page. The owner’s configuration has two parts, the configuration script and the settings. The last column connects each word to the review:

WordWhat it namesIn the review
SessionOne run of ravel start-session, from the request to the resultThe review
CallerThe person or program that runs that command. The caller gives a prompt and a kind.The orchestrator
KindA short token that names the sort of workreview
OwnerThe person who controls the configuration of Ravel for the account. The owner can be you.The person who chose a harness for review
Configuration scriptA file of Rhai code that selects a program and its arguments, or refuses the request. Rhai is a small computer language, and its engine is part of the ravel executable.The script that selected the harness for review
SettingsThe values in the JSON file settings.json, such as limits and the harnesses that the script can runThe default values
Managed processThe program that the configuration script selected. It is usually a harness, such as Claude Code or Codex, the program in which an LLM works on the prompt. Ravel launches it and supervises it.Claude Code, or a fake agent in the book’s run
ResultThe text that a program in the session sends with ravel end-session. Ravel does not interpret it. Thus, the book calls it an opaque result.changes-needed
Ravel CLIThe ravel command-line program. It evaluates the configuration script in a separate process, launches the selected program and returns the result.The program that ran the review and returned changes-needed

What happens between your command and your result? The table restates the order from chapter 1, “From a request to a result”. The column “In the review” gives the values of each row in the review. These values come from chapter 1’s real run, in which a fake agent stood in for the harness. Each section of this chapter adds detail to one or more rows. Read the sections in order, or go to the row that you need:

A cancellation can stop the order at rows 2 to 6. Cancel during selection or supervision explains what you receive then. Did you come here for an exit code? Go to the status table. It gives each normal status of the Ravel CLI and the three cancellation signals that Ravel handles, with an action for each.

Run the examples of this chapter

Each example also includes settings.json, in addition to the files in the inventory below. Its empty skill-source array disables discovery instructions. The setup copies that file into the private home before Ravel runs.

Every complete example in this chapter uses fake programs. A fake agent is a small shell script that stands where a harness stands. Most fake agents run ravel end-session themselves, as an LLM in a harness does when the prompt tells it to. Ravel evaluates the real configuration script of each example. No example needs an agent account or a model service.

First, follow chapter 3 to install Ravel and check its version. You need the installed ravel and a shell.

Each example has a name and a directory of its own. Put these directories in one workspace directory, ~/ravel-chapter-5, in your home directory. Why a new directory for each example? Several examples use the same file names, such as config.rhai and bin/review-agent, with different contents. Some commands also write files, such as result.txt and review-notes.md. A file from one example must not change the result of another example.

The chapter has seven examples. Each row names the example, its directory under ~/ravel-chapter-5 and its task files. Each also needs settings.json.

ExampleSectionWhat the example showsFiles that you save
prompt-and-dataSupply the prompt and caller dataThe prompt and the caller data that the configuration script receivesconfig.rhai, bin/echo-prompt, prompt.txt and caller-context.json

review-callerAccept a result only after successAn orchestrator script that acts on the result of the reviewconfig.rhai, bin/review-agent, bin/fix-agent, review-prompt.txt and orchestrator.sh

Do these steps for each example, in a shell where ravel --version succeeds:

  1. Make the example directory and the subdirectories of its files, then cd into the example directory. The paragraph before each example gives the two commands.
  2. Save the files of the complete example at their labelled paths. Each label is a path in the example directory.
  3. Type each command line of the example without its first $ , which stands for the prompt of your shell.
  4. Run echo "$?" immediately after the last line of each command block to see its exit status. Compare the output and the status with the example.

The first example, prompt-and-data, shows each step in full. A tree shows its paths, and a table says who makes each path and why. After its complete command, a second table explains each part of that command. Each later example has a paragraph that names its directory and says how it is different from prompt-and-data.

Each example labels its output. A label “Standard error includes” means that standard error contains those lines. It can also contain other lines, such as reports of cleanup operations. A label “without a final newline” means that Ravel added no newline after the result. Thus, the next prompt of your shell can appear on the same line as the result.

The book checker runs the same files and commands with the built Ravel CLI. For each example, it makes a new directory, saves the labelled files and makes the files in bin executable. Then it runs the command blocks in order and compares each output with the output that the book shows. You do the preparation by hand, in the steps above.

Supply the prompt and caller data

What does the selected program receive, and how can you give the configuration script more facts about the task? You may want the LLM to receive your instructions exactly as you wrote them. You may also want the configuration script to choose a program from facts such as a profile name or a task file. This section shows the inputs of start-session that do these things.

First, look at the prompt. The orchestrator of the review wrote this file before it ran start-session:

Fragment: the prompt file review-prompt.txt of chapter 1’s review. The orchestrator writes this file. Ravel reads its text and gives it unchanged to the configuration script. Ravel does not interpret the text.

Review the changes to src/parse.rs and tests/parse.rs in the last commit.
Check that the code is correct and that the tests cover each change.

When the review is complete, end this session with one command:
- If you found no problem, run: ravel end-session approved
- If you found a problem, write your findings to review-notes.md.
  Then run: ravel end-session changes-needed

Your prompt has the same four parts as this one:

Part of the promptIn the reviewWhy your prompt needs it
The workReview two named filesTells the LLM what to do
The permitted resultsapproved and changes-neededGives the only values that your program expects
The condition for each resultNo problem, or a problem with notes in review-notes.mdTells the LLM which value to send
The command to end the sessionravel end-session RESULTTells the LLM how to end the session and send the value

Ravel adds no instructions to end the session. An interactive harness does not exit when its LLM completes the task. It waits for more input. Thus, your prompt tells the LLM to run ravel end-session RESULT when the task is complete. RESULT is a placeholder for the text to send.

The values and their meanings are your convention. Ravel gives them no meaning.

Pass exactly one of --prompt TEXT and --prompt-file PATH. Ravel gives the text to the configuration script unchanged in request.originalPrompt, also when it is empty or ends with newlines. With skill delivery enabled, request.prompt also holds discovery instructions before that text. It never reads a prompt from its standard input. A prompt is UTF-8, contains no NUL and has a maximum size of 1 MiB. Ravel does not truncate input that is not valid, but stops the invocation with status 2.

InputWhen to use itWhat Ravel reads
--prompt TEXTA short prompt that you write in the commandThe argument, as the shell gives it to Ravel
--prompt-file PATHA prompt whose exact text is important, for example with trailing newlinesThe file, relative to the caller’s working directory

The argument limits of the operating system can be smaller than 1 MiB. These limits also apply when the configuration script puts a prompt from a file into the arguments of the selected program.

Why is a file safer for exact text? Shell command substitution removes trailing newlines. Thus, --prompt "$(cat prompt.txt)" can change the text before Ravel receives it. --prompt-file prompt.txt reads the file directly. With skill delivery disabled, Ravel changes no text before the configuration script receives it. Then the script makes the arguments of the selected command and decides how to give the prompt to that program.

The kind is a required caller token that is not empty. Ravel has no catalogue of kinds and does not get a kind from a file name. A configuration script can select a program for review, refuse it or treat it as any other kind. The caller and the owner agree on these tokens.

The caller can also use the kind to extend or modify the prompt. For example, if the harness supports skills, the caller can add a line to the prompt for the kind review. That line tells the LLM which skill to load. The configuration script can also put text before the prompt for a kind. Ravel gives neither the kind nor the prompt a meaning.

These inputs give data to the configuration script. They do not tell Ravel to read a task or to give the task a meaning:

InputWhat the configuration script receives
--param profile=carefulrequest.params.profile with the string careful. Each name is not empty, contains no = and occurs one time. The value can be empty or contain more = characters. The limit for all names and values together is 65,536 UTF-8 bytes.
--task-file tasks/check.mdAn absolute UTF-8 request.taskFile. Ravel makes it from the given path and the caller’s working directory. Ravel does not read that file, and the file does not have to exist. Ravel gets no kind or identity from it.
--task-id example-17An opaque request.taskId. It is UTF-8, is not empty and has a maximum of 1,024 bytes. Ravel does not search for a stored task.
--context caller-context.jsonA JSON document of facts about the task, which Ravel measures and validates. If the configuration script has no loader, the document becomes the second argument to select. A loader can make a different document for the script. The compact JSON text of the document that select receives has a maximum of 262,144 bytes by default. The owner’s contextBytes setting or --context-bytes changes that limit.

That last document is selection context, which is data that the configuration script uses to make its choice. The complete example below shows a context document with its required schemaVersion field. Chapter 6, “--context PATH”, gives the fields and their values. The book uses the word “context” for three different things, and it always names which one:

NameWhat it isWho uses it
Selection contextThe JSON document from --context, or the document that the load_context function of the configuration script makesThe configuration script, to make its choice
Session-result contextThe path of the result file, which Ravel puts in the RAVEL_EXIT_FILE environment variable of the program that it launchesravel end-session, to send the result
Working directory, cwdThe current directory of the callerThe selected program, which runs there

Chapter 4 teaches selection context and loaders and how to set bounds and environment grants in the settings.

Ravel does not run your configuration script in the Ravel CLI’s own process. It launches a second copy of the ravel executable as a child process. That child is the Rhai worker. The worker evaluates the configuration script and sends the selection or the refusal to its parent. Chapter 1 gives three reasons for a separate process:

  • The deadline. At the deadline, the parent ends evaluation and can signal the worker. A kernel stall can delay the worker’s exit.
  • Requests from the script. The worker only computes. When the script reads a file or runs a harness, the worker asks the parent, and the parent does the work.
  • Isolation of a failure. If the script exhausts the memory of the worker or overflows its stack, only the worker stops. The parent reports the failure and launches no program.

The worker ends before Ravel launches the selected program. Ravel waits until the worker exits and collects its exit status. Only then does Ravel launch the managed process.

The request of the script in the second reason uses a host capability, a ravel:: function such as ravel::read_text or ravel::run_harness. Each such request is a host call.

How long can evaluation take? The evaluation deadline defaults to 30 seconds and covers every host call. An LLM call is a harness run that asks a model a question. It uses that same deadline.

The owner can set timeoutMs in the settings. For one invocation, --timeout-ms replaces that value. The permitted range is 1,000–600,000 ms. This bound applies to the evaluation of the configuration script, not to the time that the managed process works.

The owner also sets budgets.harnessRuns, from 0 to 16, in the settings. This evaluation budget limits the number of harness runs in one evaluation. Its default is 0, and callers have no flag that raises it.

The selected program runs in the caller’s working directory. The form of its program path sets the base that Ravel uses to find the program:

Selected programResolution base
Absolute pathThe path itself
Relative path that contains /The caller’s working directory
Bare program nameThe caller’s PATH

Ravel gives the argument strings directly to the program, without shell word splitting or expansion.

How does the managed process get its environment? A child process gets a copy of the environment of its parent. Thus, the managed process gets the environment of the Ravel CLI, which is the caller’s environment. Ravel makes these changes to that copy before the launch:

  • It removes RAVEL_RUN_ID, RAVEL_STATE_DIR, HARNESS_DISPATCH_EXIT_FILE, HARNESS_DISPATCH_RUN_ID and HARNESS_DISPATCH_STATE_DIR. These variables are controls of an enclosing session or of another launcher.
  • It replaces RAVEL_EXIT_FILE with the path of the result file of the new session.
  • It replaces RAVEL_SKILL_DIRECTORIES with this session’s descriptor path, or removes it when skill delivery is disabled. The descriptor records the retained skill directories. It grants no authority to end the session.

Thus, end-session in the program and its children finds only the path of the session that Ravel made for them. Put the installed ravel on the PATH of the managed process. As an alternative, write the absolute path of ravel in the prompt’s instructions to end the session.

Now run these inputs yourself in the first example of this chapter, prompt-and-data. Its caller gives a prompt file and each kind of caller data. Its configuration script passes the prompt and the parameter profile to a fake agent. The fake agent writes the profile, which you see on standard error. Then it sends the prompt back as the result, so the result shows what the agent received. When you finish the steps, the directory holds these paths:

~/ravel-chapter-5/
└── prompt-and-data/
    ├── settings.json      you save it
    ├── config.rhai           you save it
    ├── prompt.txt            you save it
    ├── caller-context.json   you save it
    ├── bin/                  you make it
    │   └── echo-prompt       you save it
    ├── home/                 the first command line makes it
    └── tmp/                  the first command line makes it

Each path has one purpose in the example:

Path in prompt-and-dataWho makes itPurpose
settings.jsonYouDisables skill delivery with an empty source array. The setup copies it into the private home.
config.rhaiYouThe configuration script. The option --config config.rhai selects it. It gives the prompt and the value of profile to echo-prompt.
prompt.txtYouThe prompt, with a final newline. The option --prompt-file prompt.txt gives it to Ravel.
caller-context.jsonYouThe selection context. The option --context caller-context.json gives it to the configuration script, which does not use it here.
bin/echo-promptYouThe fake agent that the configuration script selects. It writes profile=careful, then sends its first argument as the result.
home/The first command lineA private home directory. The setup copies the example’s settings here and disables skill delivery.
tmp/The first command lineA private temporary directory. Ravel makes the result channel of the session here.

The command also names tasks/check.md with --task-file. You do not make that file. Ravel gives its absolute path to the configuration script and does not read it.

Do the four steps of Run the examples of this chapter. Step 1 is this fragment.

The ~ stands for your home directory, so these commands work from any current directory.

Part of the commandWhat it does in prompt-and-data

PATH="$PWD/bin:$PATH"Puts bin first on the search path. Your shell expands $PATH before env -i clears the environment, so your installed ravel stays on the path.

The private home and the explicit --config keep your own settings and configuration script out of the example.

The table follows this example from the command to the result that the caller receives:

OrderWho actsWhat happens in this example
1The callerRuns start-session with the prompt file, one parameter, the task fields and the selection context
2The configuration scriptSelects echo-prompt, with the prompt and careful as its two arguments
3The Ravel CLIWrites the routing notice ravel: starting provider local, model fake, effort none for kind "review" on standard error
4echo-promptWrites profile=careful on its standard output. Ravel sends that output to the caller’s standard error.
5echo-promptRuns ravel end-session with the prompt as the result
6The Ravel CLIStops the program’s process group, completes cleanup and writes the prompt unchanged on standard output. It exits with status 0.

The final newline came from prompt.txt. Ravel added no newline. The printf output of the fake agent goes to the caller’s standard error, because Ravel uses standard output only for the result. This example does not need a tasks/check.md file.

Give the agent the owner’s skill directories

The owner can configure skill sources in the skills object of the settings. Without that configuration, Ravel reads the sources that packages install in its default skill directory. With Homebrew, that directory is $(brew --prefix)/share/ravel/skills. "sources": [] in the skills object turns the default skill directory off.

When there are sources, each start-session prepares them before it evaluates the configuration script. The caller and the configuration script need no new input for this. Ravel finds no skill and gives no meaning to a skill name. The agent finds and reads the skills that apply to its work.

Ravel puts the discovery instructions before the caller’s prompt in request.prompt. The caller’s bytes follow, unchanged, between two delimiter lines. request.originalPrompt keeps the caller’s prompt alone. Your instructions to end the session stay exact, because Ravel does not read or change them. A caller instruction to load a skill “first” comes after the mandatory reads. Write “after any mandatory guidance” when that order matters to you.

The managed process and each harness run also receive RAVEL_SKILL_DIRECTORIES, the path of the descriptor of the directory set. An agent that loses the path from its prompt can read it there. With delivery disabled, Ravel removes that name from their environment. When the agent loses both paths, it reports that the discovery context is missing. Ravel supplies no other path, and the cache holds no pointer to a latest descriptor.

The snapshots and the descriptor stay after the session. At the end of the session, Ravel stops only the processes in the process group of the managed process. A delegate that left that group can read the resources after the session ends. So can a harness that you start with the output of ravel skills resolve. The resources also stay after a change or removal of the source and after a change of the settings.

A skill refusal stops the launch before the worker starts. For example, a missing source or a missing mandatory file refuses with status 3, and no managed process starts.

Ravel refuses a skill source that overlaps a protected path before it copies the source. That refusal is skill_access_unavailable, with status 3. Keep each source apart from these paths:

Protected pathWhy Ravel protects it
~/.config/ravel and the settings fileThey hold the owner’s configuration, which is no skill content
The directory of the configuration scriptIt holds the owner’s configuration, which is no skill content
The skill cache, ~/.cache/ravel/skills/v1It holds the snapshots of the skill sources
The result channel of an enclosing sessionIt carries the result of that session

Skill delivery at launch gives the parts of the prepared prompt and the refusals. A resolution also has fixed bounds and a deadline, which Bounds, deadline and cancellation gives.

Accept a result only after success

When can your program trust the result? You may want to act on the result as soon as a program in the session sends it. But Ravel can still fail after that moment, for example during cleanup. Thus, keep the result bytes and the status of start-session together, and accept the result only with status 0.

Two operating-system words explain the conditions of status 0:

WordWhat it meansWhy status 0 depends on it
Process groupA set of processes that the operating system can signal together. Ravel puts the managed process in a new group. Its descendants stay in that group unless they leave it.When the group is gone, no process of the session that stayed in the group still runs.
ReapA parent collects the exit status of its child process.After the reap, Ravel knows that the managed process ended.

Status 0 means that all of these conditions are true:

  • A program in the session sent a valid result.
  • Ravel reaped the direct child, which is the managed process.
  • Ravel confirmed that the process group of the managed process is gone.
  • The required cleanup succeeded.
  • The final write and flush of the result succeeded.

An empty sent result is a successful result with zero bytes. If a managed process exits with status 0 and sends no result, the status is 1. Ravel then returns no result to the caller. But if a program sends a result and the child then exits with a nonzero status, start-session can still give status 0. Ravel’s status shows whether Ravel delivered a sent result after cleanup. It does not show whether the work in the prompt was correct.

end-session takes one UTF-8 argument with a maximum size of 1 MiB. Quote it, and put -- before a value that looks like an option, such as --help. Ravel writes an empty, multiline or Unicode value unchanged to standard output and does not interpret it. The operating system can apply a smaller limit for command arguments before end-session runs.

How does Ravel receive the result? end-session writes it through the result channel, the private directory and result file that Ravel made for the session.

Ravel keeps the first complete result that it receives. When that first result arrives, Ravel starts to stop the harness. Thus, a second end-session call and the shutdown are in a race: either can occur first. For example, another process in the group can send a result before Ravel stops that process. Ravel keeps the first result, even if the second command succeeds.

Run the next example in the directory outcomes. Step 1 is mkdir -p ~/ravel-chapter-5/outcomes/bin, then cd ~/ravel-chapter-5/outcomes. You save settings.json, config.rhai and bin/outcome. The fake agent outcome examines the kind. For the kind natural, it exits without a result. For any other kind, it sends the prompt as the result.

This example is different from prompt-and-data in two ways. It has no prompt file, so each command gives the prompt with --prompt. It also has three command blocks. Only the first block makes home and tmp. The other two blocks use those directories. Run echo "$?" after each block.

Complete example: compare three outcomes. In the first, the program sends a result and then tries a second call before it exits with status 7. The shutdown can stop the program before that call or that exit. In the second, the program sends an empty result. In the third, it sends no result and exits naturally with status 0.

The table follows each command to what the caller receives:

CommandWhat the program doesWhat the caller receives
--kind send --prompt '--help'Sends --help, then tries to send a second value and to exit with status 7The result --help and status 0. Ravel kept the first result.
--kind send --prompt ''Sends an empty resultAn empty result and status 0
--kind natural --prompt ''Exits with status 0 and sends no resultNo result, the diagnostic session_result_missing and status 1

This chapter writes INT, TERM, HUP and KILL without the SIG prefix. Other signal names keep that prefix. Code and diagnostic text keep their literal names.

end-session returns before the enclosing session finishes its cleanup. When Ravel detects the sent result, a job that still runs gets 2 seconds to finish. Then Ravel sends TERM, waits for up to 5 more seconds and sends KILL if necessary. These waits end early if the direct child exits. Ravel then stops the other group members before it reaps the child. Those members do not get a new 2-second wait.

Send the result after the work that has to finish, because Ravel can stop code that comes after end-session.

For an automated caller that needs trailing newlines intact, redirect standard output to a file. Read the file only after you check the status. Shell command substitution removes trailing newlines. Use it only when the caller’s result convention accepts this change. A failed final write can leave only the first part of the result in the file, so the bytes alone do not prove success. Ravel has no persistent store for results, so it cannot recover a result after a failed delivery.

How does a caller put these rules together? Return to the review. After the session, the orchestrator has two facts: the status of start-session and the bytes on standard output. It decides its next action from both:

Outcome of ravel start-sessionWhat the orchestrator does next
Status 0 and the result approvedMarks the review as complete and moves to its next task
Status 0 and the result changes-neededReads review-notes.md, then requests a session of the kind fix with the notes in its prompt
Status 0 and another valueTreats the review as failed, because its prompt permitted only two values
A status other than 0Reads standard error, then decides whether to request the review again. It does not use standard output as a result.

Run the next example in the directory review-caller. Step 1 is mkdir -p ~/ravel-chapter-5/review-caller/bin, then cd ~/ravel-chapter-5/review-caller. You save settings.json and these five files:

Path in review-callerPurpose
config.rhaiThe configuration script. It selects review-agent for the kind review and fix-agent for the kind fix.
bin/review-agentThe fake harness of the review. It writes review-notes.md and sends changes-needed, as the LLM did in chapter 1.
bin/fix-agentThe fake harness of the fix session. It sends fixed.
review-prompt.txtThe prompt of chapter 1’s review
orchestrator.shThe caller. It runs both sessions and acts on each result.

This example is different from prompt-and-data. Your command line does not run ravel start-session itself. It runs sh orchestrator.sh, and the script runs ravel start-session two times. Both inner command lines inherit the private HOME, TMPDIR and PATH that env -i gave to sh. The script is not in bin, so it needs no execute permission.

The run also writes four files in the example directory. review-agent writes review-notes.md. The script writes result.txt, fix-prompt.txt and fix-result.txt. You do not make these files.

Complete example: an orchestrator script runs the review and acts on each outcome.

fn select(request, context) {
    if request.kind == "review" || request.kind == "fix" {
        return #{
            status: "selected",
            program: request.kind + "-agent",
            args: [request.prompt],
            provider: "local",
            model: "fake",
            effort: "low",
            reason: "Each kind goes to its own fake agent.",
        };
    }
    #{
        status: "refused",
        code: "unknown_kind",
        message: "This configuration script has no rule for the kind " + request.kind + ".",
        remedy: "Use the kind review or fix.",
    }
}

bin/review-agent

#!/bin/sh
set -eu
echo "parse_line accepts an empty line, and no test covers it." >review-notes.md
ravel end-session -- "changes-needed"

bin/fix-agent

#!/bin/sh
set -eu
ravel end-session -- "fixed"

review-prompt.txt

Review the changes to src/parse.rs and tests/parse.rs in the last commit.
Check that the code is correct and that the tests cover each change.

When the review is complete, end this session with one command:
- If you found no problem, run: ravel end-session approved
- If you found a problem, write your findings to review-notes.md.
  Then run: ravel end-session changes-needed

orchestrator.sh

#!/bin/sh
set -u
if ravel start-session --kind review --config config.rhai --prompt-file review-prompt.txt >result.txt; then
  status=0
else
  status=$?
fi
if [ "$status" -ne 0 ]; then
  echo "orchestrator: the review failed with status $status"
  exit 1
fi
case $(cat result.txt) in
  approved)
    echo "orchestrator: the review is complete"
    ;;
  changes-needed)
    echo "orchestrator: request a fix for these notes:"
    cat review-notes.md
    {
      echo "Fix the problems in these review notes:"
      cat review-notes.md
      echo "When the fix is complete, run: ravel end-session fixed"
    } >fix-prompt.txt
    ravel start-session --kind fix --config config.rhai --prompt-file fix-prompt.txt >fix-result.txt || exit "$?"
    echo "orchestrator: the fix session sent $(cat fix-result.txt)"
    ;;
  *)
    echo "orchestrator: an unexpected result, so the review failed"
    exit 1
    ;;
esac

Command

$ mkdir -p home/.config/ravel tmp && chmod +x bin/*
$ cp settings.json home/.config/ravel/settings.json
$ env -i HOME="$PWD/home" TMPDIR="$PWD/tmp" PATH="$PWD/bin:$PATH" sh orchestrator.sh

Standard output

orchestrator: request a fix for these notes:
parse_line accepts an empty line, and no test covers it.
orchestrator: the fix session sent fixed

Standard error includes

ravel: starting provider local, model fake, effort low for kind "review"

Exit status

0

The standard output here is the output of the orchestrator script, not of Ravel. The table follows the script from its first command to its last action:

OrderWho actsWhat happens in this example
1The orchestratorRuns start-session with the kind review and sends its standard output to result.txt
2The configuration scriptSelects review-agent for the kind review
3review-agentWrites review-notes.md and sends changes-needed
4The Ravel CLICompletes cleanup, writes changes-needed to result.txt and exits with status 0
5The orchestratorChecks the status first. The status is 0, so it reads result.txt.
6The orchestratorFinds changes-needed, writes a new prompt with the notes and requests a session of the kind fix
7fix-agentSends fixed. Ravel returns it to the orchestrator after cleanup.

The script reads result.txt only after status 0. It uses command substitution, $(cat result.txt), because its two permitted values have no trailing newlines. A status other than 0 ends the script with an error. A real orchestrator also keeps standard error and decides whether to request the review again.

Keep nested sessions separate

You may want the program of one session to start another session. For example, an orchestrator can run as a harness and ask Ravel for a review session as one step of its work. Then it uses the review’s result in its own work. The program that asks is the caller of the inner session. How does each end-session reach the right session?

The answer is the path in RAVEL_EXIT_FILE. Chapter 1, “How end-session finds its session”, explains it, and this section restates it for nested sessions. Before each launch, Ravel makes a result channel, which is a private temporary directory with a path for one result file. Ravel puts that path in the RAVEL_EXIT_FILE environment variable of the program that it launches. The book calls this path the session-result context.

A child process gets a copy of the environment of its parent. Thus, each descendant of the managed process inherits the same path, unless its parent changes the environment. ravel end-session finds the path in its own environment. end-session takes no session ID and no target argument. An inner start-session gives a new path only to its own managed process:

ProcessWhich process starts itIts RAVEL_EXIT_FILEWhat its end-session call sends
Outer ravel start-sessionThe callerNone, unless the caller itself runs in a sessionIt does not call end-session
Outer managed processThe outer Ravel CLIThe outer pathThe result of the outer session
Inner ravel start-sessionThe outer managed processThe outer path, inheritedIt does not call end-session
Inner managed processThe inner Ravel CLIA new inner pathThe result of the inner session. Ravel returns it to the outer process after the inner cleanup.
Outer managed process, after the inner sessionThe outer Ravel CLIThe outer path, unchangedThe result of the outer session

The figure uses the paths of the separate run in chapter 1.

Each child inherits its parent’s path unless its parent changes it. Ravel sets a new path for the inner harness.

Figure: The nested run gives each session’s children its own result path.

Open the inherited result paths at full size.

Here is a real run of the nested example below. Each fake agent also printed its own RAVEL_EXIT_FILE on standard error. The outer agent printed its value before and after the inner session.

Fragment: standard error of a real nested session on macOS with TMPDIR=/tmp. This block omits the cleanup reports. The random parts of each path change in each run. macOS gives /tmp the real path /private/tmp.

ravel: starting provider local, model fake, effort none for kind "outer"
outer: RAVEL_EXIT_FILE=/private/tmp/ravel-session-DW2gTg/signal-c8008b48ae03d4d4a5e671806d997d6a
ravel: starting provider local, model fake, effort none for kind "inner"
inner: RAVEL_EXIT_FILE=/private/tmp/ravel-session-47e8Ar/signal-803d525c20ad7bff18912ce6edb1d662
outer: RAVEL_EXIT_FILE=/private/tmp/ravel-session-DW2gTg/signal-c8008b48ae03d4d4a5e671806d997d6a

Ravel made a directory under TMPDIR for each session. The inner agent received the new path. The outer agent kept its own path, also after the inner session. Thus, each end-session goes to the session that encloses it.

When the inner invocation returns, the outer process decides what to send for the outer session. Ravel does not change the inner result into an outer result. The outer process does this itself. When the inner process sends its result, the outer session does not finish.

With skill delivery enabled, the inner start-session also prepares the skill sources again. It copies the current bytes of each local source, so the inner agent sees a source change that the outer agent does not see. Ravel gives the descriptor path of the inner session in RAVEL_SKILL_DIRECTORIES only to the inner managed process. The outer managed process keeps its descriptor path, its directories and its result path, and its snapshots do not change.

An evaluation harness run is different. A harness run is a program that the configuration script starts with ravel::run_harness during evaluation, to ask a question before it chooses. Ravel gives that run a result path of its own, so its result goes back to the script, not to a caller. The harness entry in the settings names its program, so the script makes no selection for it.

Ravel also gives a harness run the RAVEL_CONFIG_RUN marker, an environment variable. If a program in that run calls start-session, Ravel refuses with config_reentry_refused, status 3. The refusal occurs before Ravel reads the settings or the configuration script.

The final managed process receives no such marker and can start nested sessions. Each nested session has its own evaluation budgets. The marker does not protect against code that removes it: such code escapes the refusal. Such an escape can leave a separate process group outside the outer cleanup.

Run the next example in the directory nested. Step 1 is mkdir -p ~/ravel-chapter-5/nested/bin, then cd ~/ravel-chapter-5/nested. You save settings.json and three other files. config.rhai selects outer for the kind outer and inner for every other kind. bin/outer is the outer fake agent, which runs the inner session. bin/inner is the inner fake agent.

This example is different from prompt-and-data, because one configuration script serves both sessions. The script gives outer the absolute path of config.rhai as its argument. outer then gives that path to the inner ravel start-session with --config. The inner session inherits the private HOME, TMPDIR and PATH from outer.

Complete example: the inner program sends inner, and the outer program uses that result in inner|outer. These values have no trailing newlines, so command substitution keeps them unchanged.

settings.json

{"skills":{"schemaVersion":1,"sources":[]}}

config.rhai

export const SCHEMA_VERSION = 4;
export const VERSION = "nested-demo";

fn select(request, context) {
    #{
        status: "selected",
        program: if request.kind == "outer" { "outer" } else { "inner" },
        args: [ravel::path_resolve(request.cwd, "config.rhai")],
        provider: "local",
        model: "fake",
        effort: "none",
        reason: "Give each nested session its own result",
    }
}

bin/inner

#!/bin/sh
set -eu
ravel end-session -- "inner"

bin/outer

#!/bin/sh
set -eu
enclosing=$RAVEL_EXIT_FILE
result=$(ravel start-session --kind inner --config "$1" --prompt "Send inner.")
[ "$RAVEL_EXIT_FILE" = "$enclosing" ]
ravel end-session -- "$result|outer"

Command

$ mkdir -p home/.config/ravel tmp && chmod +x bin/*
$ cp settings.json home/.config/ravel/settings.json
$ env -i HOME="$PWD/home" TMPDIR="$PWD/tmp" PATH="$PWD/bin:$PATH" ravel start-session --kind outer --config config.rhai --prompt 'Run the inner session. Then add |outer to its result and send it.'

Standard output, without a final newline

inner|outer

Exit status

0

The table follows this example from the caller’s command to the caller’s result:

OrderWho actsWhat happens in this example
1The callerRuns start-session with the kind outer
2The outer Ravel CLILaunches outer with the outer path in RAVEL_EXIT_FILE
3outerKeeps its path in enclosing, then runs start-session with the kind inner
4The inner Ravel CLILaunches inner with a new inner path
5innerSends inner to the inner path
6The inner Ravel CLICompletes the inner cleanup and writes inner on its standard output, which outer captures
7outerChecks that its path did not change, then sends `inner
8The outer Ravel CLICompletes the outer cleanup and writes `inner

Each layer’s Ravel CLI supervises only the group of its own managed process, and it does the cleanup only for that group. Thus, if an inner Ravel CLI stops abruptly before it completes its cleanup, the group of its managed process can remain. The outer Ravel CLI does not stop that group. The limits of the cleanup guarantee apply at each layer. The example checks that the results stay separate. It does not check interactive job control for nested sessions.

Know what the managed process receives

Ravel launches the selected program as an ordinary child process.

Ravel does not restrict the filesystem access of the managed process. The process can read and write each path that your account permits, within the limits of the harness sandbox. The configuration script selects that sandbox through the arguments of the harness, such as --sandbox workspace-write for Codex. Evaluation harness runs have the same rule. Chapter 2 states this trust limit.

Ravel 2.0.0 refuses --confine and --runtime-read with confinement_withdrawn and status 2. It refuses before it reads the settings or starts the worker. Remove both flags, and select the sandbox of the harness in the configuration script. Ravel 1.1 keeps --confine.

The managed process inherits the caller’s environment, with the changes that Supply the prompt and caller data lists. --config-env grants a variable to ravel::env, the environment helper of the configuration script. It adds no variable to the managed process or to a harness run. Managed-process environment gives each name.

Ravel puts the private result channel of the session in the caller’s temporary directory. That is TMPDIR, or /tmp when TMPDIR is not set. --exit-dir DIR puts the channel in DIR instead. Name a writable directory that exists. Before selection, Ravel refuses a path that is not a directory with exit_dir_unusable and status 2. If Ravel cannot allocate the channel after selection, it stops with session_channel_unavailable and status 5.

Ravel launches the managed process in a new process group in the caller’s POSIX session. A POSIX session is an operating-system set of process groups that can share one controlling terminal. It is not a Ravel session.

To lend the terminal, Ravel makes the group of the managed process the foreground group of the terminal. Then that group receives the keyboard input. Ravel lends the terminal only if its own process group is the foreground group of the terminal. After the session, Ravel attempts recovery if it lent the terminal. Recovery also depends on which group holds it then, as chapter 9 explains.

The managed process can also read the caller’s standard input. Chapter 9, “Jobs, signals and the terminal”, teaches the mechanism.

Use this table to see what the managed process receives:

PropertyManaged process
Filesystem accessEach path that your account and the harness sandbox permit. Ravel restricts no path.
Caller environmentInherited, except for the five controls that stay absent, the new RAVEL_EXIT_FILE and the replaced or removed RAVEL_SKILL_DIRECTORIES
Session-result contextNew RAVEL_EXIT_FILE, beneath the temporary directory or --exit-dir
Process groupNew process group in the caller’s POSIX session
Caller terminalLent when Ravel’s group is the foreground group of the terminal
Standard inputThe caller’s standard input
NetworkRavel restricts no network access

Read notices separately from results

Your program needs the result. It can also want to know what the configuration script chose. For example, the orchestrator of the review can record which harness, model and effort did each review. Ravel gives these two kinds of information on two different streams. This section shows what each stream carries and when Ravel writes to it.

In chapter 1’s run of the review, the two streams had these lines:

StreamLine in the reviewWho reads it
Standard outputchanges-needed, with no newlineThe orchestrator, as the result
Standard errorravel: starting provider local, model fake, effort low for kind "review"The person or the log that watches the session

The line on standard error is the routing notice. Where do its values come from? The configuration script gives back a selection or a refusal.

A selection is the map that select returns when it chooses a program. It holds program and args, the command that Ravel runs. It also holds the labels provider, model, effort and reason. In the review, the labels were local, fake, low and Reviews go to the fake agent.

A label describes the choice of the configuration script, but it does not configure the harness. For example, a script that selects Claude Code puts the model option of Claude Code in args. The model label only tells the caller which model the script chose. These labels are owner text, not integrations with model providers.

The caller does not read the owner’s configuration script. Thus, Ravel tells the caller what the script chose in the routing notice:

  • Who writes it: the Ravel CLI.
  • When: in text form, after the configuration script selects a program and before Ravel launches it. In JSON form, after the supervision attempt.
  • Where: the caller’s standard error. Standard output carries only the result.
  • Who reads it: the caller, or the person who watches the terminal.
  • Why: this notice is the way in which Ravel tells the caller which harness, model and effort the configuration script chose.

The text form is one line that starts with ravel: starting provider. It gives the selected provider, model, effort and kind. --json replaces that line with the JSON routing notice, a JSON document on one line. The document gives more detail, such as the reason and the program file that Ravel found. When Ravel cannot complete a session, --json also replaces the text diagnostic with a JSON document, the failure document.

Run the next example in the directory notices. Step 1 is mkdir -p ~/ravel-chapter-5/notices/bin, then cd ~/ravel-chapter-5/notices. You save settings.json and three other files. config.rhai selects review-agent for the kind review and refuses every other kind. bin/review-agent is a fake harness that writes one line, then sends changes-needed. review-prompt.txt is the prompt of chapter 1’s review, the same file as in review-caller.

This example is different from prompt-and-data in its three command blocks. Only the first block makes home and tmp. The second block adds --json. The third block uses the kind deploy, which the script refuses.

Complete example: run the review in text form, the same review with --json, and a request that the configuration script refuses. The script and the prompt are those of chapter 1.

settings.json

{"skills":{"schemaVersion":1,"sources":[]}}

config.rhai

export const SCHEMA_VERSION = 4;
export const VERSION = "notice-demo";

fn select(request, context) {
    if request.kind == "review" {
        return #{
            status: "selected",
            program: "review-agent",
            args: [request.prompt],
            provider: "local",
            model: "fake",
            effort: "low",
            reason: "Reviews go to the fake agent.",
        };
    }
    #{
        status: "refused",
        code: "unknown_kind",
        message: "This configuration script has no rule for the kind " + request.kind + ".",
        remedy: "Use the kind review.",
    }
}

bin/review-agent

#!/bin/sh
set -eu
echo "review-agent: the review is complete"
ravel end-session -- "changes-needed"

review-prompt.txt

Review the changes to src/parse.rs and tests/parse.rs in the last commit.
Check that the code is correct and that the tests cover each change.

When the review is complete, end this session with one command:
- If you found no problem, run: ravel end-session approved
- If you found a problem, write your findings to review-notes.md.
  Then run: ravel end-session changes-needed

Command

$ mkdir -p home/.config/ravel tmp && chmod +x bin/*
$ cp settings.json home/.config/ravel/settings.json
$ env -i HOME="$PWD/home" TMPDIR="$PWD/tmp" PATH="$PWD/bin:$PATH" ravel start-session --kind review --config config.rhai --prompt-file review-prompt.txt

Standard output, without a final newline

changes-needed

Standard error includes

ravel: starting provider local, model fake, effort low for kind "review"
review-agent: the review is complete

Exit status

0

Command

$ env -i HOME="$PWD/home" TMPDIR="$PWD/tmp" PATH="$PWD/bin:$PATH" ravel start-session --json --kind review --config config.rhai --prompt-file review-prompt.txt

Standard output, without a final newline

changes-needed

Standard error includes

"schemaVersion":2,"selection":{"effort":"low","model":"fake","provider":"local","reason":"Reviews go to the fake agent."}

Exit status

0

Command

$ env -i HOME="$PWD/home" TMPDIR="$PWD/tmp" PATH="$PWD/bin:$PATH" ravel start-session --json --kind deploy --config config.rhai --prompt 'Deploy the last commit.'

Standard output

Standard error

{"diagnostics":{"stderr":"","stdout":""},"error":{"code":"config_refused","configCode":"unknown_kind","exit":3,"input":"--kind deploy","message":"the configuration script <scratch>/work/config.rhai refused the selection: This configuration script has no rule for the kind deploy.","remedy":"Use the kind review.","source":"<scratch>/work/config.rhai","stage":"selection"},"schemaVersion":2}

Exit status

3

Here <scratch>/work stands for the example’s directory. In your shell, the document shows the absolute path of your notices directory, for example /Users/you/ravel-chapter-5/notices/config.rhai. Ravel supplies the diagnostic prefix. The configuration script supplies the refusal code, message and remedy.

The table follows the three commands to what the caller receives:

CommandWhat Ravel writes on standard error, in orderWhat the caller receives
Text formThe routing notice, then the line of the fake agentchanges-needed and status 0
--jsonThe line of the fake agent, then the JSON routing noticechanges-needed and status 0
--json --kind deployOnly the failure document. The configuration script refused, so Ravel launched no program.No result and status 3

In text form, the notice comes first, because Ravel writes it before the launch. With --json, the notice comes last, because Ravel writes it after the supervision attempt. The text form tells you the choice before the program starts. The JSON form also records what happened during the session.

Fragment: standard error of the --json review command of notices from a real run on macOS. The run used the directory /tmp/ravel-chapter-5/notices in place of ~/ravel-chapter-5/notices, and macOS shows /tmp as /private/tmp. Ravel writes the JSON document as one line. This block shows it on several lines and omits the members bounds, context, diagnostics, operationReports, configEnvironment, reads and worker. The value of selectionMs changes in each run.

review-agent: the review is complete
{
  "harnessRuns": [],
  "config": {
    "modules": [
      {
        "bytes": 619,
        "name": "/private/tmp/ravel-chapter-5/notices/config.rhai",
        "sha256": "9d4526b5db2b7f9d9c8812bfc183a841133f1b2b18b20981049770e90efb1b91"
      }
    ],
    "path": "/private/tmp/ravel-chapter-5/notices/config.rhai",
    "version": "notice-demo"
  },
  "promptSource": "/private/tmp/ravel-chapter-5/notices/review-prompt.txt",
  "resolution": "/tmp/ravel-chapter-5/notices/bin/review-agent (found on PATH in /tmp/ravel-chapter-5/notices/bin)",
  "schemaVersion": 2,
  "selection": {
    "effort": "low",

  },
  "selectionMs": 3,
  "skills": null

--json changes the routing notice and the failure document of Ravel into JSON on standard error. It does not change the result into JSON, and it does not change other output on standard error. The managed process can still write any text there. Keep standard error as a diagnostic stream, and do not parse the full stream as one JSON document. The managed process can write a line that looks like a Ravel notice. Thus, the form of a line does not prove that Ravel wrote it.

Read standard output only as the result of a successful session. Keep standard error as a mixed diagnostic stream, even with --json:

OutputDestinationEffect of --json
Unchanged opaque result after a successful sessionThe caller’s standard output, with no added newlineNone
Standard output and standard error of the managed processThe caller’s standard errorNone. Any program text stays.
Standard output, standard error and optional result of an evaluation harness runReturn data for the configuration scriptNone. They reach a caller stream only if the script copies them into visible output.
Routing notice and failure document of RavelThe caller’s standard errorJSON documents replace their text form

The JSON routing notice gives selection, config, worker, selectionMs, the delivered context and the effective bounds. It also gives configEnvironment, resolution, promptSource, the captured diagnostics of the configuration script and skills.

The member skills is null with skill delivery disabled. Otherwise it gives the descriptor path, the sources, the resolution time and the byte count and digest of each prompt. It also gives the default skill directory when that directory supplied the sources.

The member config describes the configuration script. The member context is the selection context, not the result channel. Grant diagnostics show the name of each environment variable and whether it is set. They never show its value.

The configuration script can still write values itself. Thus, keep its diagnostics with the care that those values need.

The notice also records what evaluation used:

FieldRecorded data
worker.rhaiVersionVersion of the embedded Rhai engine
config.modulesEntry first, then imported modules, each with its canonical name, byte count and SHA-256 digest
readsSuccessful measured reads, with their operation, phase, canonical name, byte count and digest
harnessRunsEach harness run, with its harness name, status, exit code or signal, elapsed time and whether it sent a result
bounds.harnessRunsEffective run budget and its origin

An unavailable call appears in harnessRuns even though no program started. The run record contains no prompt, answer or token usage. The configuration script can still expose that data through its own diagnostics or context.

Ravel writes this JSON notice after the supervision attempt and before it decides the final outcome. The runner is the part of the Ravel CLI that launches and supervises the managed process. If the runner completed supervision and returned its record of how the job ended, operationReports holds the operational reports that the runner kept. The array is empty if there are no reports. If supervision failed before the runner returned that record, the notice has no such field.

Some reports, such as a failed stop attempt, stay visible even when later checks confirm successful cleanup. A report alone does not decide success, and the routing notice also does not. Check the final status.

The failure document has an error object with code, stage, message, remedy and exit. It adds the fields that apply, such as input, source, location, configCode, bound and signal. It also adds the captured diagnostics of the configuration script when they are available. The refused command of the example above shows configCode, the owner’s own code. Read the code and the stage together. Chapter 6, “JSON failure document”, gives every field of the document and its values.

These examples distinguish a decision of the configuration script from failures before and after launch:

CodeStageMeaning
config_refusedselectionThe configuration script refused the request.
worker_identity_mismatchworkerThe re-executed Ravel binary reports another build. Ravel stops before it evaluates the configuration script.
harness_budget_exhaustedevaluationThe configuration script requested a run with no run budget left. Evaluation ends.
harness_cleanup_failedevaluationRavel could not complete the cleanup of an evaluation harness run. No managed process starts.
session_result_missingexecThe managed process ended without a sent result.

The text form of each failure starts with ravel: refused, also when no configuration script refused the request. The code and the status show the difference between a refusal and an operational failure.

For worker teardown and final-session cleanup, an original failure keeps its code and status if cleanup also fails. Ravel reports those cleanup problems together with the original failure.

An evaluation harness run has an exception. If its cleanup fails after the whole deadline expires, harness_cleanup_failed replaces selection_timeout. The status is then 5, not 124. Cancellation has precedence over both.

If cancellation occurs, Ravel sets the cancellation status, keeps the original and cleanup diagnostics and returns no result to the caller. If required cleanup fails, Ravel also returns no result to the caller, even when everything else succeeds. Thus, keep the complete standard error stream, even after the caller classifies the exit status.

Interpret the exit status

Your program has the status of start-session. What does it mean, and what does your program do next? This table gives the normal outcomes of the Ravel CLI. It comes from the status constants of the Ravel CLI, its launch and final-outcome code and its code for signals.

A caller checks the status of start-session. It does not use the status of the managed process or the earlier status of end-session. For a session result, accept standard output only with status 0 from start-session. A help or version request is a separate invocation for information, and it sends no result.

These sections teach the subject of each row:

  • statuses 0 and 1: Accept a result only after success
  • status 2: Supply the prompt and caller data and Know what the managed process receives
  • status 3: chapter 4’s Select a command or refuse
  • status 5: Read notices separately from results
  • status 124: chapter 4’s Set bounds and environment grants in the settings
  • statuses 126 and 127: program resolution, in Supply the prompt and caller data
  • statuses 129, 130 and 143: Cancel during selection or supervision
StatusMeaningWhat the caller does
0start-session wrote the sent result unchanged to standard output after Ravel confirmed the reap, the absence of the group and the required cleanup. An explicitly empty result is valid. A later nonzero exit of the child does not make it invalid. For end-session, status 0 means that it sent the result, or that a valid first result was already present. A help or version request also exits with 0.Interpret the result of a successful session with the caller’s own convention. Ravel does not interpret it. Wait for start-session. The success of end-session alone does not confirm cleanup.
1start-session: session_result_missing, even if the child exited with status 0. end-session: it could not send the result. For example, RAVEL_EXIT_FILE was missing, empty or stale, the result channel was not valid, or the value was larger than its limit.Check that the managed process calls the installed ravel end-session with one result before it exits. Check also that it makes the call inside its live enclosing session. Keep any cleanup diagnostics too. Do not treat a missing result as an empty result.
2Malformed command-line input or settings, invalid prompt or data flags, an unusable --exit-dir, or a withdrawn confinement flag. A missing subcommand shows the help with status 2. An argument-count error of end-session also uses 2.Correct the named input or setting. Run the command again only after you apply the remedy of the diagnostic.
3A refusal by the configuration script, or Ravel’s refusal of the script. The script refused, or Ravel could not accept its entry, behaviour, shape, returned data or selection context. Script limits, the worker heap bound, exhausted evaluation budgets and a start-session from inside a harness run (config_reentry_refused) also use 3. Ravel tries no other route. Skill-source, resource and cache refusals also use 3. Read their skills stage and remedy.Read code, stage, the location and the remedy. For config_refused, examine the owner’s configCode and explanation. Change the request or the configuration script deliberately. Do not silently substitute a program.
5A worker failed, or supervision failed after the runner reported the launch. The result data was not valid, or required cleanup or terminal recovery failed. A group survived, or the result output was unavailable or failed. A failed re-execution or worker identity mismatch also uses 5, as does harness_cleanup_failed during evaluation.Keep the full diagnostics.
