Repair the installation, or the process or filesystem problem that the diagnostic names. Examine the unresolved processes or temporary directories that Ravel reports before you try again. Discard any partial result bytes.
124The whole evaluation deadline expired. It covers worker startup, module loads, the context loader, selection and every host call. Ravel launched no managed process. A failed harness cleanup replaces this outcome with status 5. Cancellation also has priority. Skill resolution has a separate deadline, skills.resolutionTimeoutMs, and reports skill_resolution_timeout with 124 before worker evaluation.Examine the work of the configuration script. Change --timeout-ms or the owner’s timeoutMs only if you want that longer evaluation. A single harness run’s timed_out status is data for the script. This is not a time limit for the managed process.
126Ravel could not execute the selected program, or the launch failed, before the runner reported that the program started. The cause is not ENOENT (no such file).Check the execute permissions, the format, the interpreter, the platform and the execution environment. A launch failure before the start can also need terminal recovery. Keep those diagnostics. Ravel runs no substitute.
127Program resolution found no executable, or a launch failed with ENOENT before the start. An executable script can exist while its interpreter is missing.Check the caller’s PATH, the selected path and its interpreter. Install or correct the program that you intended.
129Cancellation by HUP (signal 1), during skill resolution, selection or supervision. Ravel returns no result to the caller, even if it already received one.Treat the invocation as cancelled. Keep the cleanup reports. Try again only if the caller still wants the work.
130Cancellation by INT (signal 2), during skill resolution, selection or supervision. Ravel returns no result to the caller, even if it already received one.Treat the invocation as cancelled. Distinguish a signal to Ravel from a terminal signal to only the group of the managed process. Cancel during selection or supervision explains the difference.
143Cancellation by TERM (signal 15), during skill resolution, selection or supervision. Ravel returns no result to the caller, even if it already received one.Treat the invocation as cancelled. Keep the cleanup reports. Try again only if the termination was not intended.

The last three numbers follow the shell convention: 128 plus the signal number. Ravel reports the cancellation, attempts the cleanup and then raises the signal again. Thus, a process API reports termination by that signal, not a numeric exit. If the propagation of the signal fails, Ravel reports that failure and exits with the same number as a fallback. A caller that uses a process API handles both forms.

A signal such as KILL can stop the Ravel CLI at any point. The normal procedure of Ravel does not apply to such a signal. Then the shell status does not show that cleanup or result delivery occurred. Also, a new signal that arrives after the final cancellation decision can interrupt the result write. No atomic guarantee covers both the arrival of a signal and an arbitrary write to standard output.

The following view expands one step of chapter 3’s outcome state machine: the step where Ravel decides the final outcome. Cancellation has priority over an original failure. After evaluation decides its failure, the final cleanup does not replace that failure’s status. During evaluation, failed harness cleanup can first replace a deadline with harness_cleanup_failed. Only a valid result with confirmed cleanup reaches the final write.

The view shows the precedence of outcomes, not the order of function calls. Ravel attempts the applicable cleanup before it reports an outcome.

The view covers start-session. The table also covers end-session and the help and version invocations.

Open the final-outcome view at full size.

Cancel during selection or supervision

You may need to stop a session before it ends. For example, the person at the terminal presses Ctrl-C, or your orchestrator stops its wait. A cancellation is an INT, TERM or HUP signal that reaches the Ravel CLI. Ravel then stops the work, attempts cleanup and returns no result to the caller. What Ravel stops depends on the phase of the session:

PhaseWhat runsWhat Ravel does at a cancellation
Skill resolutionThe copy and the storage of the skill sources, when the owner settings enable deliveryRemoves its private temporary directory. Starts no worker. The refusal is skill_resolution_cancelled.
SelectionThe Rhai worker, and any harness run that the configuration script startedAttempts to stop and reap the harness run, then the worker. Launches no managed process.
SupervisionThe managed process and its process groupAttempts to stop the group, to reap the managed process and to recover the terminal. Returns no result, even after a sent result.

A cancellation during selection stops evaluation and prevents the managed launch. Ravel abandons a read in progress and does not wait for it. If a harness run is in progress, Ravel first stops its group and attempts its reap. Then it stops and reaps the worker and removes the private directories. Ravel reports the cancellation with any cleanup problems.

The worker has no session-result context. Thus, it cannot send a result for an enclosing session through the usual end-session interface.

An evaluation harness run can therefore need cleanup before any managed launch. The example below uses a fake harness that sends INT to its parent, the Ravel CLI. The configuration script itself cannot send a signal.

In these UML sequences, no lifetime ends with an X. Ravel attempts cleanup, and a failed attempt does not confirm a reap or a removal.

The selection sequence shows that Ravel attempts harness cleanup before worker teardown. The fake evaluation harness sends the signal directly to the Ravel CLI. Its own result channel is separate from the channel of a final managed process.

Open the selection cancellation sequence at full size.

Run the next example in the directory cancel-selection. Step 1 is mkdir -p ~/ravel-chapter-5/cancel-selection/bin, then cd ~/ravel-chapter-5/cancel-selection. You save three files:

Path in cancel-selectionPurpose
settings.jsonThe settings. They name the harness entry cancel with the program cancel-selection, and they permit one harness run.
config.rhaiThe configuration script. It asks for a run of the harness entry cancel before it chooses.
bin/cancel-selectionThe fake evaluation harness. It sends INT to the Ravel CLI, then waits.

This example is different from prompt-and-data, because it needs settings. Ravel reads the settings from .config/ravel/settings.json in its home directory. The first command line makes home/.config/ravel together with tmp. The second line copies your settings.json into that directory. The third line runs the session.

Complete example: a fake evaluation harness sends INT to the Ravel CLI. The configuration script waits for the harness run. No final managed process starts.

settings.json

{"harnesses":{"cancel":{"program":"cancel-selection"}},"budgets":{"harnessRuns":1},"skills":{"schemaVersion":1,"sources":[]}}

config.rhai

export const SCHEMA_VERSION = 4;
export const VERSION = "cancel-selection";

fn select(request, context) {
    ravel::run_harness(#{ harness: "cancel", args: [] });
    #{ status: "refused", code: "unexpected_return",
       message: "The call returned without cancellation.",
       remedy: "Check that the fake harness sent INT." }
}

bin/cancel-selection

#!/bin/sh
set -eu
kill -INT "$PPID"
while :; do sleep 1; done

Command

$ mkdir -p home/.config/ravel tmp && chmod +x bin/*
$ cp settings.json home/.config/ravel/settings.json
$ env -i HOME="$PWD/home" TMPDIR="$PWD/tmp" PATH="$PWD/bin:$PATH" ravel start-session --kind check --config config.rhai --prompt 'No managed process should launch.'

Standard output

Standard error includes

ravel: refused (selection_cancelled, stage evaluation): selection with the configuration script <scratch>/work/config.rhai was cancelled by SIGINT: worker teardown was attempted and nothing was launched

Exit status

130

Here <scratch>/work stands for the example’s directory. In your shell, the line shows the absolute path of your cancel-selection directory. The details of worker teardown can change from run to run. Thus, the transcript claims only the stable diagnostic line. Here, the diagnostic’s “nothing was launched” refers to the final managed process. The evaluation harness did run before cancellation.

The owner enables this call in the settings, with a named harness entry and a run budget of one. The run has no terminal, so its signal does not depend on keyboard input. Chapter 4 explains the call’s inputs and output.

The table follows this example to what the caller receives:

OrderWho actsWhat happens in this example
1The callerRuns start-session with the kind check
2The configuration scriptCalls ravel::run_harness for the harness entry cancel and waits
3The Ravel CLIStarts cancel-selection as a harness run
4cancel-selectionSends INT to its parent, the Ravel CLI
5The Ravel CLIAttempts to stop and reap the harness run, then the worker. It launches no managed process.
6The Ravel CLIWrites selection_cancelled on standard error. The caller receives no result and status 130.

The whole deadline uses the same stop order as cancellation. Cleanup takes additional time. A signal consumed before the final outcome decision has priority over a deadline, and the report keeps the earlier failure. A stop of an LLM call ends local work only. A model service can still complete a request that it already accepted.

During supervision, a cancellation stops the group of the managed process. Ravel attempts the reap, the terminal recovery and the temporary cleanup before it reports the signal. Ravel returns no result to the caller, even when it already received a result. A cleanup failure does not change the cancellation into success. Ravel reports the failure together with the cancellation.

The supervision sequence shows why a cancellation returns no result to the caller, even after the managed process sent a result. The result reaches the temporary result channel, but Ravel does not write it to standard output. The runner runs inside the Ravel CLI. The group of the managed process includes the descendants that stay in that group. The cleanup steps in the figure are attempts, and Ravel keeps their failures as diagnostics.

Open the supervision cancellation sequence at full size.

Run the next example in the directory cancel-session. Step 1 is mkdir -p ~/ravel-chapter-5/cancel-session/bin, then cd ~/ravel-chapter-5/cancel-session. You save settings.json and two other files. config.rhai selects cancel-after-result for every request. bin/cancel-after-result is the fake agent.

The command block prepares the directories and copies the settings, as in prompt-and-data. Its final line gives the prompt with --prompt and gives no caller data.

Complete example: the program sends a candidate result and sends INT directly to the Ravel CLI. Then it stays alive until the supervisor stops the job.

settings.json

{"skills":{"schemaVersion":1,"sources":[]}}

config.rhai

export const SCHEMA_VERSION = 4;
export const VERSION = "cancel-session";

fn select(request, context) {
    #{
        status: "selected",
        program: "cancel-after-result",
        args: [],
        provider: "local",
        model: "fake",
        effort: "none",
        reason: "Cancel after a sent result",
    }
}

bin/cancel-after-result

#!/bin/sh
set -eu
ravel end-session -- "candidate"
kill -INT "$PPID"
while :; do sleep 1; done

Command

$ mkdir -p home/.config/ravel tmp && chmod +x bin/*
$ cp settings.json home/.config/ravel/settings.json
$ env -i HOME="$PWD/home" TMPDIR="$PWD/tmp" PATH="$PWD/bin:$PATH" ravel start-session --kind check --config config.rhai --prompt 'Show cancellation after a sent result.'

Standard output

Standard error includes

ravel: refused (session_cancelled, stage exec): session selected by <scratch>/work/config.rhai was cancelled by SIGINT

Exit status

130

Here <scratch>/work stands for the example’s directory. In your shell, the line shows the absolute path of your cancel-session directory.

The program sent candidate, and Ravel received it. Then INT reached the Ravel CLI. Ravel attempted to stop the group and to complete cleanup. Then it wrote session_cancelled on standard error. The caller receives no result and status 130, although a program sent a result.

A signal that you send to the Ravel CLI is different from Ctrl-C at the terminal. After Ravel lends the terminal, the group of the managed process is the foreground group of the terminal. Ctrl-C then makes the terminal send INT to that group, not to the Ravel CLI. If only that group receives INT and exits with no sent result, the Ravel CLI reports session_result_missing with status 1. It does not report cancellation with status 130.

An interactive program can also handle or ignore the keyboard signal itself. Use the status and the diagnostic of the Ravel CLI. Do not infer the outcome from the key that you pressed. Chapter 9 explains signal inheritance and terminal recovery.

Fragment: run an interactive route and press Ctrl-C at the terminal. This fragment omits the configuration script, the interactive program and the physical interaction with the terminal. It is not one of the complete examples of this chapter and has no directory. The automated examples do not test keyboard delivery or foreground recovery.

ravel start-session --kind interactive --config config.rhai --prompt-file prompt.txt

Know when Ravel cannot guarantee cleanup

What can an unsuccessful invocation leave behind? Ravel’s cleanup guarantee does not apply under the conditions in this table. Use the table to decide what the caller examines after such an invocation:

ConditionConsequence
Code that runs as the same user makes unauthorised changes to the result channel or the installation.Private paths and the inherited session-result context keep ordinary sessions apart. They are not a defence against hostile code of the same user. The worker identity check confirms that the re-executed binary reports the expected build. It does not check a trusted signature.
The Ravel CLI stops abruptly, for example because of KILL.It cannot complete supervision or cleanup. The group of the managed process and the temporary directories can remain. There is no successful completion and no persistent recovery record.
A managed descendant deliberately leaves its process group.It is outside the cleanup guarantee of that group. The absence of the group does not prove that the detached process is gone.
An evaluation harness run starts a descendant that leaves its process group.Cleanup of the run does not reach that descendant. The Rhai worker itself starts no program.
Another process changes the foreground group of the terminal during recovery, or the system gives a recorded group ID to an unrelated group.Recovery assumes two conditions between the runner’s check and its handback. No other process or thread changes the foreground group. No unrelated group gets the recorded group ID. Ravel reports a failed handback. Do not treat a launch failure as proof that Ravel restored the terminal. Chapter 9 explains the conditions.
The host is different from the measured platforms.The project measured how Ravel handles inherited signal settings, cancellation, cleanup and terminal recovery. It measured them on aarch64 macOS and aarch64 GNU/Linux. The project built Ravel for x86_64 GNU/Linux and ran it only under emulation. Native x86-64 qualification is not complete. These checks do not cover all combinations of kernel, libc and compiler.

Chapter 2 explains these trust limits. Ravel issue 1 tracks the native x86-64 qualification. The examples in this chapter check the choice of command, the delivery of the result and the status. They do not show the behaviour of real agents, interactive terminal operation or whether a new reader can use the book.

Check your caller before real use

In short, the caller runs ravel start-session with a kind, a prompt and optional caller data. The configuration script selects a program, and the Ravel CLI tells the caller about that choice in the routing notice on standard error. Ravel launches the program with a new path in RAVEL_EXIT_FILE. A program in the session sends one result to that path with ravel end-session. Ravel stops the program and its process group, completes cleanup and writes the result unchanged on standard output.

The status of start-session tells the caller whether it can use that result. In the review, the orchestrator received changes-needed with status 0. Thus, it read review-notes.md and requested a fix session. The meaning of changes-needed came from the orchestrator’s own prompt, not from Ravel.

Before your program runs sessions with real harnesses, check each of these points with fake agents:

Point to checkWhat a good caller does
The promptTells the LLM how and when to run ravel end-session, with the permitted result values
The statusUses the result only after status 0 from start-session
The result bytesRedirects standard output to a file when trailing newlines are important
A missing resultTreats status 1 as no result, not as an empty result
Standard errorKeeps it as a mixed diagnostic stream, and takes no line as proof of what Ravel wrote
A cancellationTreats 129, 130, 143 and termination by a signal as no result
A nested sessionSends its own result after the inner session returns
The harness sandboxSelects the sandbox of each harness in the configuration script, because Ravel restricts no path

Chapter 6, “Reference”, gives the reference entries for the inputs and outputs that this chapter teaches.

Reference

Do you have a question about one option, one exit status, one line on standard error or one file? This chapter answers it in one entry. You can come here from any page of the book, or directly from an error message. You do not have to read the earlier chapters first.

Each entry explains its terms and links to the chapter that teaches the subject. The entries repeat short explanations so that each entry makes sense alone.

Many entries use the code review of chapter 1 as their example. An orchestrator, a program that runs LLM work as steps of its own work, wants an LLM to review the last commit. It writes a prompt file, review-prompt.txt, and runs this command:

Fragment: the request of chapter 1’s review. This block omits the code of the orchestrator that runs the command.

ravel start-session --kind review --prompt-file review-prompt.txt

The prompt tells the LLM to run ravel end-session approved if it finds no problem. If it finds a problem, the LLM writes review-notes.md and runs ravel end-session changes-needed.

Chapter 1 shows a real run of this request. Its short configuration script selects a fake agent in place of Claude Code. The fake agent wrote review-notes.md and sent changes-needed, as the LLM does when it finds a problem. Ravel wrote changes-needed on standard output, and ravel start-session exited with status 0. The orchestrator gives the two values their meaning. Ravel does not.

First, the words that this chapter uses on every page. The owner’s configuration has two parts, the configuration script and the settings. The last column connects each word to the review:

WordWhat it namesIn the review
SessionOne run of ravel start-session, from the request to the resultThe review
CallerThe person or program that runs that command. The caller gives a prompt and a kind.The orchestrator
KindA short token that names the sort of workreview
OwnerThe person who controls the configuration of Ravel for the account. The owner can be you.The person who chose a harness for review
Configuration scriptA file of Rhai code that the owner writes. Ravel gives the request to the script. The script selects a program and its arguments, or it refuses the request.The script that selected the harness for review
SettingsThe values in the JSON file settings.json, such as limits and the harnesses that the script can runThe default values
Rhai workerThe second copy of the ravel executable that evaluates the configuration script. Each session has a new worker, and it ends before Ravel launches the selected program.The process that ran the script for review
Managed processThe program that the configuration script selected. It is usually a harness, such as Claude Code or Codex, the program in which an LLM works on the prompt.Claude Code, or a fake agent in the book’s run
ResultThe text that a program in the session sends with ravel end-session. Ravel does not interpret it. Thus, the book calls it an opaque result.changes-needed
Ravel CLIThe ravel command-line program. It evaluates the configuration script in the Rhai worker, launches the selected program and returns the result.The program that ran the review and returned changes-needed

Ravel uses config in file names, options and diagnostic codes, such as config.rhai, --config-env and config_refused. Its diagnostic prose uses “configuration script”.

Why does Ravel evaluate the script in a separate process? The Ravel CLI launches a second copy of its own executable as a child process, the Rhai worker. The parent stays in control while the script runs:

  • The deadline. Each evaluation has a deadline. When it expires, the parent ends evaluation and can signal the worker process. Rhai cannot interrupt some of its own functions, such as sleep. A kernel stall can delay the worker’s exit.
  • Requests from the script. The worker only computes. When the script reads a file or runs a harness, the worker asks the parent. The parent does the work and sends the data back.
  • Isolation of a failure. If the script exhausts the memory of the worker or overflows its stack, only the worker stops. The parent continues, reports the failure to the caller and launches no program.

The worker does not run at the same time as the selected program. Ravel waits until the worker exits and collects its exit status. Only then does Ravel launch the program that the script selected.

What is Rhai? Rhai is a small computer language for programs that are written in Rust. Its engine is part of the ravel executable, so you install no separate language runtime. Rhai looks like a mix of JavaScript and Rust, and its values have dynamic types.

A Rhai script changes things outside itself only through functions that the host program gives it. In Ravel, these are the ravel:: functions. Some are host capabilities, which ask the Ravel CLI to do work. Other helpers run in the worker.

The Rhai Book documents the language, and its language reference lists each statement and operator. The online playground runs Rhai code in your browser. It does not have the ravel:: functions or Ravel’s limits. Chapter 4 teaches the Rhai that a configuration script needs.

Which entry answers your question? Use the table, or the headings of this chapter:

Your questionEntry
Which options does ravel start-session accept?start-session and the entries after it
How does a program send its result?end-session RESULT
What does an exit status mean?Exit statuses
What does a line on standard error mean?Streams and notices
What does a code such as config_refused mean?Diagnostic codes
Which environment variables does Ravel read, set or remove?Environment variables
Where is the configuration script, and which settings exist?Configuration script and settings
Which limits apply to evaluation?Bounds and Engine and conversion bounds
What does the configuration script receive and return?Configuration script and request and Selection and refusal
Which ravel:: functions can the configuration script call?Selection context and host capabilities
Which example configuration scripts does Ravel install?Example configuration scripts
Where are the installed files?Installed layout

The entries describe the version that this book names, installed with Homebrew.

This chapter writes INT, TERM, HUP and KILL without the SIG prefix. Other signal names keep that prefix. Code and diagnostic text keep their literal names.

Command line

ravel, help and version

Do you want to know which verbs Ravel has, or which version you installed? Ask the Ravel CLI. ravel takes one of three commands: start-session, end-session or skills resolve. It has no help subcommand. The table gives the help and version requests.

RequestOutputStatus
ravel without a verbHelp on standard error.2
ravel --help, ravel start-session --help, ravel end-session --help, ravel skills resolve --helpHelp on standard output. -h is the short option on each of these command lines.0
ravel --version, ravel -VThe package version of the Ravel CLI on standard output. The version option belongs only to the top-level command.0

Fragment: the output of ravel --help from the checkout build on 2026-11-02. This block omits the command and its status, 0.

Route and supervise a session, returning its opaque result

Usage: ravel <COMMAND>

Commands:
  start-session  Route the prompt, with any configured skill directories, through an owner Rhai configuration script and supervise its selected process
  end-session    Send exactly one opaque result to Ravel and ask Ravel to end the enclosing session
  skills         Resolve the skill sources of the owner settings, without a configuration script or a harness

Options:
  -h, --help     Print help
  -V, --version  Print version

Examples:
  ravel start-session --kind feature --prompt 'Implement the parser; finish with ravel end-session done'
  ravel end-session done
  ravel skills resolve

Help and version text is informational output, never an opaque result. See the first complete session for the two verbs in use.

After --, Ravel reads no more options. For end-session, put -- before a result that looks like an option. Inside a live session, ravel end-session -- '--help' sends the literal value --help. See result arguments.

start-session

You use ravel start-session when you want a harness, or another program, to do one piece of work and give your program one result. ravel start-session --kind TEXT also needs exactly one of --prompt TEXT and --prompt-file PATH.

What happens after you run the command? The table follows one successful session from your command to your result:

OrderWho actsWhat happens
1The callerRuns ravel start-session with a kind, a prompt and optional caller data
2The Ravel CLIReads the settings, checks the file of the configuration script and launches a second copy of its own executable as the Rhai worker
3The configuration scriptRuns in the Rhai worker and returns a selection: a program, its arguments and labels
4The Ravel CLIReaps the worker, writes the text routing notice on the caller’s standard error and launches the selected program. With --json, the notice follows the supervision attempt.
5The managed processDoes the work, then runs ravel end-session RESULT
6The Ravel CLIStops the program and the rest of its process group, completes cleanup and writes the result on standard output

To reap a process is to collect its exit status from the operating system. After the reap, the operating system can use the process ID again. Ravel launches the managed process in a process group of its own. Each child of the managed process is in that group too, unless the child leaves it. The routing notice is the line that tells the caller which harness, model and effort the configuration script chose.

At row 3, the configuration script can also refuse. After a refusal, Ravel launches no final managed process and tries no alternative route. Before it returns, the script can run harnesses of its own. These harness runs return their output to the script, not to the caller.

At row 6, Ravel writes the sent result unchanged to standard output. This write occurs only after the reap, the confirmed absence of the process group and the required cleanup. Ravel does not interpret the result. Then ravel start-session exits with status 0.

Fragment: the first command of the example notices in chapter 5, “Read notices separately from results”, and its standard error. That example runs the review of chapter 1 in the directory ~/ravel-chapter-5/notices. This block omits the configuration script, the prompt file review-prompt.txt, the fake agent review-agent and the environment of the command. Chapter 5 runs this session as a complete example, and the book’s example check compares these two lines.

$ ravel start-session --kind review --config config.rhai --prompt-file review-prompt.txt
ravel: starting provider local, model fake, effort low for kind "review"
review-agent: the review is complete

The first line is the routing notice of the Ravel CLI. The second line comes from the fake agent. The fake agent then ran ravel end-session -- "changes-needed". Thus, standard output held changes-needed, with no added newline, and the status was 0. See run a complete session and interpret the outcome.

Note: The project measured inherited signal settings, cancellation, cleanup and terminal recovery on aarch64 macOS and aarch64 GNU/Linux. For x86_64 GNU/Linux, the project built Ravel and ran it only under emulation. Native x86-64 qualification is still open. The row for a different host in Know when Ravel cannot guarantee cleanup gives the scope of these measurements.

All thirteen options below belong after start-session. Only --param and --config-env accept more than one occurrence. A repeated single-value option or switch is a command-line error. -h and --help are also available on this verb.

Ravel 2.0.0 refuses --confine and --runtime-read, which Ravel 1.1 accepts. Either flag, in any position, gives confinement_withdrawn with status 2. Ravel refuses before it reads the settings or starts the worker. Remove both flags, and select the sandbox of the harness in the configuration script. A word that is the value of another option, such as the text of --prompt --confine, is caller data and gives no refusal. See know what the managed process receives.

--kind TEXT

Why does the caller give a kind? The kind is a short token that tells the configuration script what sort of work the caller wants, such as review or bugfix. The script can use the kind to choose a harness, a model and an effort. It can also use the kind to extend or modify the prompt.

--kind is required and supplies request.kind unchanged. Its value is nonempty UTF-8. An absent or empty kind gives status 2.

Ravel has no catalogue of kinds and gets no kind from a task file. Ravel gives the token no meaning. Only the configuration script and the caller do. See supply the prompt and caller data.

--prompt TEXT and --prompt-file PATH

The prompt is the text of the task for the LLM. It also tells the LLM how to end the session, for example “Then run: ravel end-session changes-needed” in chapter 1’s review. Ravel adds no instructions to end the session. Thus, your prompt gives them.

Exactly one of these two prompt inputs is required.

OptionSource of request.originalPrompt and request.prompt
--prompt TEXTThe argument text, also when its first character is -.
--prompt-file PATHOne read of the file, relative to the caller’s working directory.

The prompt can be empty. It contains no NUL, is valid UTF-8 and has a maximum of 1,048,576 bytes (1 MiB). Ravel puts its bytes unchanged in request.originalPrompt, with trailing newlines.

With skill delivery disabled, request.prompt holds the same bytes. With delivery enabled, it holds the prepared prompt. The configuration script decides how to give that text to its selected program. Usually the script puts it in an argument of the harness, and the script can add text before or after it.

An unreadable prompt file reports prompt_unreadable. Invalid encoding, a NUL or too many bytes reports prompt_invalid. Both give status 2. A command with no prompt input, or with both prompt inputs, also exits 2. Ravel does not implicitly read the prompt from standard input, and it refuses a prompt file that is a terminal. See supply the prompt and caller data.

Note: Shell command substitution removes trailing newlines before Ravel receives an argument. --prompt-file preserves those bytes. An operating system can set a smaller argument limit. This limit also applies when the configuration script puts a prompt from a file into the arguments of the selected command.

--task-file PATH and --task-id ID

You may want to tell the configuration script which task the session serves. These two options carry that caller data to the script. The script can use it to choose a route, or to extend or modify the prompt. Ravel itself does nothing with the values. Invalid input gives status 2.

OptionRequest fieldValue and effect
--task-file PATHrequest.taskFileRavel joins the caller’s working directory and the given path to make an absolute UTF-8 path. It does not canonicalise or read the path. The file does not have to exist, and the path supplies no kind or task identity.
--task-id IDrequest.taskIdNonempty opaque UTF-8 with a maximum of 1,024 bytes. Its first character can be -. Ravel does not search for a task record.

For example, --task-id T-17 gives the script request.taskId with the value "T-17". Without the option, request.taskId is (), Rhai’s empty value. The request entry shows a real request with this value. See caller data for both fields.

--config PATH

You may want to try a configuration script before you install it, or use one script for one project. --config selects that script file, which the book calls the entry. The path is relative to the caller’s working directory. Without the option, Ravel uses $HOME/.config/ravel/config.rhai, which needs an absolute HOME. There is no repository search and no environment variable that selects a different configuration script.

The entry resolves to a readable regular UTF-8 file of at most 1 MiB. Its canonical path is UTF-8. A symlink selects its real file, and the directory of that file is the base for relative imports.

An empty option value is malformed input, status 2. If Ravel cannot accept the entry file, the status is 3. A different entry does not move the settings file from $HOME/.config/ravel/settings.json. See define a configuration script and import other Rhai files.

--param NAME=VALUE

You may want to give the configuration script a value that only your caller knows, such as the name of a repository. The script can use the value to choose a route, or to extend or modify the prompt. --param adds a string to request.params, and it is repeatable with different names.

For example, --param repo=ravel gives the script request.params with the value #{repo: "ravel"}. The request entry shows a real request with this value. The table gives the parts of each argument and their limit.

PartRule
NameNonempty UTF-8 without =. Each name occurs only once.
SeparatorThe first = separates the name from the value.
ValueUTF-8, possibly empty or with more = characters.
Whole argumentIts first character can be -.
Combined sizeAll names and values together have a limit of 65,536 bytes. The separators do not count.

A repeated name, a missing separator, invalid encoding or a total above the limit gives status 2. Ravel gives names and values no meaning. See caller parameters.

--context PATH

You may want facts about the task to change the choice of the configuration script. For example, a large change can go to a higher effort. Selection context is a JSON document of such facts, such as a summary and acceptance criteria. The script receives it as the second argument of select.

The book uses “context” for three different things. This option supplies only the first:

NameWhat it is
Selection contextJSON data about the task, for the choice of the configuration script
Session-result contextThe path in RAVEL_EXIT_FILE, through which end-session sends the result
The caller’s working directoryThe directory of the request, in request.cwd

--context reads a regular file, relative to the caller’s working directory, as selection context. Ravel validates it and records its canonical name, byte count and SHA-256. The selection-context entry shows a real document as select receives it.

Without load_context, the delivered document is this data with the measured sources attached. With a loader, the data is input to the loader, and the loader assembles the document that select receives. With no caller document and no loader, select receives () as its selection-context argument.

Ravel refuses an unreadable, invalid or oversized document with status 3. Malformed command-line syntax still gives status 2. This option supplies selection context, never a session-result context. See supply selection context.

--timeout-ms MS

You may want more time for evaluation, for example when the configuration script asks a model through a harness run. Evaluation is the work of the Rhai worker, up to the selection or refusal. --timeout-ms replaces the timeoutMs setting for this invocation. The table gives its syntax and values. Invalid syntax or range gives status 2.

PropertyValue
Input syntaxDecimal digits.
Inclusive range1,000–600,000 milliseconds.
Default, with no flag or setting30,000 milliseconds.

Ravel validates the settings before the flag replaces a value. Thus, an invalid setting fails even with a valid flag.

The deadline begins when the Ravel CLI spawns the Rhai worker. It covers the worker startup, the import of the configuration script, the loader of the selection context and the selection, with every host call. At expiry, Ravel stops any evaluation harness run, then the worker. It reports selection_timeout, status 124, unless cancellation or failed harness cleanup takes precedence. Cleanup time is additional.

The deadline does not limit the time that the managed process works. See set bounds and environment grants.

--context-bytes BYTES

You may want a larger budget when the selection context holds a long task description. --context-bytes replaces the contextBytes setting. It takes decimal digits in the inclusive range 1–8,388,608 bytes. With no flag and no setting, the budget is 262,144 bytes.

Invalid syntax or range gives status 2. Ravel validates the settings first. Thus, an invalid setting fails even with a valid flag.

The budget limits the caller document and the encoded selection context that selection receives, with the measured source records. It is also the maximum for a host source read. Ravel refuses data above a bound, and it does not truncate that data. context_too_large uses status 3. See selection context and bounds.

--config-env NAME

You may want the configuration script to read a variable of the caller, for example a mode that selects a different effort. --config-env grants the script one such variable. Repeat the option to grant more names.

Why does the script need a grant? The Rhai worker has an empty environment. When the script calls ravel::env(name), the worker sends the request to the Ravel CLI over a private socket. The Ravel CLI checks the name against the base names and the grants. Then it answers with the value from the caller’s environment, or with () when the variable is unset.

For example, chapter 4 grants BOOK_EFFORT, and the caller sets BOOK_EFFORT=high. Then ravel::env("BOOK_EFFORT") returns "high". A name without a grant throws env_not_granted, also when the caller sets it. See the variables that the script can read for the base names.

Grant propertyBehaviour
SettingsThe flags add to the configEnv array of the settings.
Duplicate namesRavel merges them into one grant, in the order of their first occurrence.
ValueA grant carries the caller’s current value. An unset variable returns () through the helper.
RecipientThe configuration script reads the value through ravel::env. This option changes no managed-process or harness-run environment.

A name is nonempty UTF-8 and contains no =. It cannot match a reserved grant class. A malformed name reports malformed_input, and a reserved name reports excluded_grant. Both give status 2. Grant diagnostics show each name and whether it is set, never its value. See set bounds and environment grants.

--exit-dir DIR

The result channel is a private temporary directory, with a path for one result file in it. end-session writes the result to that file, and Ravel reads it from there. By default, Ravel makes the channel in the caller’s temporary directory: TMPDIR, or /tmp when TMPDIR is not set. --exit-dir chooses a different parent directory for the channel.

The parent is a writable directory that exists. Ravel resolves it from the caller’s working directory.

The option chooses a parent, not a result filename or a persistent store. An invalid parent can report exit_dir_unusable with status 2. If Ravel later cannot allocate the result channel, it reports session_channel_unavailable with status 5. See know what the managed process receives.

--json

Your program may want to read the routing notice and the failures as data, not as text. The routing notice tells the caller which harness, model and effort the configuration script chose. A failure document tells the caller why a session failed.

--json replaces Ravel’s text routing notice and failure document with JSON documents on standard error. It does not change the result on standard output or the output of the managed process. It is off when it is absent, and it belongs to start-session.

The time of the notice also changes. Ravel writes the text notice before the launch. Ravel writes the JSON notice after the supervision attempt, so the JSON document also records what happened during the session.

Fragment: the selection member of the JSON routing notice of chapter 1’s review request, run again with --json. The script of that run selects a fake agent, so the labels are local, fake and low. This block omits the other members of the document.

{"selection":{"effort":"low","model":"fake","provider":"local","reason":"Reviews go to the fake agent."}}

See streams and notices for the fields and the order of output, and read notices for their use.

Note: After a successful parse of the command line, a value such as --prompt '--json' is caller data, not a switch. If the parse fails, Ravel searches the argument words for an exact --json to choose the error format. Help, version and the help for a missing verb keep their usual display format.

end-session RESULT

A harness usually cannot exit by itself when its LLM finishes the task. It waits for more input. Thus, the caller’s prompt tells the LLM to run ravel end-session RESULT when the work is complete. This command sends one UTF-8 argument, the result, to the live session that encloses it. It also tells Ravel to stop that session.

How does end-session find its session? It takes no session ID and no target argument. Before the launch, Ravel makes a private result channel for the session. Ravel puts the path of its result file in the RAVEL_EXIT_FILE environment variable of the program that it launches. Each child process gets a copy of its parent’s environment, unless the parent changes it. The table shows the path when each parent leaves it unchanged:

ProcessWhich process starts itIts RAVEL_EXIT_FILE
ravel start-sessionThe callerNone, unless the caller itself runs in a session
The harness, which is the managed processRavelA new path that Ravel makes for this session
A shell for a command of the LLMThe harnessThe same path, inherited from the harness
ravel end-session changes-neededThat shellThe same path, inherited from the shell. The command writes the result there.

Each child inherits its parent’s path unless its parent changes it. Ravel sets a new path for the inner harness.

Figure: The nested run gives each session’s children its own result path.

Open the inherited result paths at full size.

The book calls this path the session-result context. The RAVEL_EXIT_FILE entry shows real paths, also for a nested session. The verb accepts no settings option, and it reads no configuration script or settings. -h and --help show the help of this verb. -- permits a result that looks like an option.

What happens after the send? Ravel finds the result in the channel. Ravel then stops the harness and the rest of its process group, and completes cleanup. Then ravel start-session writes the result on its standard output and exits 0. Thus, the value that the LLM gave to end-session becomes the result of ravel start-session.

The table gives the inputs of end-session. Quote RESULT so that it stays one argument.

InputValue or effect
RESULTOne UTF-8 argument. It can be empty, multiline or Unicode, with a maximum of 1,048,576 bytes (1 MiB).
RAVEL_EXIT_FILEThe inherited path of the live session’s private result channel.
--Ends the options, so a value that looks like an option can follow as RESULT.
-h, --helpShows help, without a send.

Ravel keeps the first complete result that it receives. After that result arrives, Ravel stops the harness. Thus, a second end-session and that shutdown are in a race: either can occur first. For example, another process in the group can send a result before Ravel stops that process. Ravel keeps the first result, even if the second command succeeds. The shutdown can stop the second command, or the removal of the result channel can make it fail.

A successful end-session exits 0 and prints nothing. It means only that the channel accepted the result, or that a valid first result was already present. It does not mean that the job ended or that the result reached the caller. A cancellation or a failure of required cleanup still returns no result to the caller.

end-session exits 1, with a text diagnostic on standard error, if the session-result context is missing, empty, stale or invalid. It also exits 1 for an oversized value or an I/O failure of the send. If the Ravel CLI finds an argument-count or encoding error, it exits 2. The operating system can set a smaller argument limit before the executable runs.

Fragment: standard error of ravel end-session changes-needed in a shell outside any session, from the checkout build on 2026-10-29. This block omits the command and its status, 1.

ravel: end-session: no enclosing Ravel session (RAVEL_EXIT_FILE is unset or empty)

See accept a result only after success.

Note: After Ravel detects the sent result, it gives a job that still runs 2 seconds. Then it sends TERM, waits up to 5 more seconds and uses KILL if necessary. If the direct child exits, Ravel stops the other group members before the reap, with no new grace period. Ravel can stop code that runs after end-session.

skills resolve

Do you want an agent to read the skill sources of the owner, but outside a session? ravel skills resolve prepares those sources and prints the discovery instructions. You then give that text to an independent harness.

The command reads the skills object of the settings. Without sources in that object, it reads the default skill directory instead. It evaluates no configuration script, creates no result channel and launches no harness. It takes no skill name, task, prompt or source override. Ravel reads no SKILL.md file, no frontmatter and no skill name.

Each source is a local directory. Ravel runs no Git or other VCS command and makes no network request for a source. A checkout of a repository is an ordinary local source, and a tool outside Ravel makes and updates it.

For each source, the command copies the current bytes of the whole source directory. The copy leaves out each .git and .jj entry, at every level. The command checks each search directory and each mandatory file in the copy. Then it stores an immutable snapshot of the copy in the cache. It also stores an immutable descriptor of the directory set. The skill discovery specification in the repository, docs/specs/skill-discovery.md, owns the complete contract.

Note: Each run writes to the cache $HOME/.cache/ravel/skills/v1. Ravel keeps the snapshots and the descriptors after the command, the session and an upgrade. They also stay after a change or removal of the source and after a change of the settings. Ravel has no automatic collector and no prune command.

Note: A snapshot has no write permission. Remove an object manually only when no reader of it runs. After the removal, the next resolution copies the source again. When the source is gone, the resolution refuses as skill_source_missing. It does not use another snapshot of that source.

The table gives the inputs and the outputs.

Form or resultStandard outputStatus
ravel skills resolve, delivery enabledThe discovery instructions, with a final newline.0
ravel skills resolve --json, delivery enabledOne JSON object, with a final newline.0
Delivery disabled, plain formNothing.0
Delivery disabled, --json{"schemaVersion":1,"status":"disabled"}, with a final newline.0
A settings problemNothing. The refusal goes to standard error.2
A skill refusalNothing. The refusal goes to standard error.3
An internal failure, such as skill_cleanup_failedNothing. The refusal goes to standard error.5
The resolution deadline passedNothing. The refusal goes to standard error.124
INT, TERM or HUP during the resolutionNothing. The refusal goes to standard error, and Ravel raises the same signal again.130, 143 or 129

Delivery is disabled when a resolution has no source and no mandatory file:

  • "sources": [] with no mandatory file disables delivery.
  • Without sources, a default skill directory that supplies no source disables delivery.
  • Without an absolute HOME, Ravel reads no settings and no default skill directory.

A disabled resolution creates no cache.

With --json, a resolved object holds these members:

MemberValue
schemaVersion1.
status"resolved". A disabled object holds only schemaVersion and status.
descriptorPathThe absolute path of the retained directories.json.
sourcesOne record for each source, in configured order or in the byte order of default source IDs: id, type, resourceDigest, snapshotRoot and originPath.
directoriesOne record for each search directory: source and the absolute path. A repeated path appears once, at its first position.
mandatoryOne record for each mandatory file, in configured order: source and the absolute path.
instructionsThe discovery instructions, without the final newline of the plain form.

The descriptor holds the same schemaVersion, sources, directories and mandatory. The name of its directory is the SHA-256 digest of its bytes. The resourceDigest of a source is the SHA-256 digest of the manifest of its snapshot. Two sources with the same content share one snapshot, and each source keeps its own record.

The discovery instructions give each path as a JSON string literal. They list the search directories, the mandatory files in order and the descriptor path. Then they tell the agent how to read mandatory files, find skills, report problems, recover after a compaction and brief a delegate. They hold no task, and no instruction that ends a session. The instructions are the same text that a launch puts before the task of the caller.

Each run captures current bytes. A run reuses a stored snapshot only when the new copy has the same content digest. An edit of a local source therefore gives a new snapshot, and an earlier snapshot keeps its bytes.

Keep a local source quiet while Ravel copies it. Ravel examines each entry two times: when it lists the entry, and after it copies the entry and its directory. When the entry, its type, its identity, its size or a change time is different, the run refuses as skill_source_changed. Then try again.

This check does not make a copy atomic. An edit that restores the size and both change times is not visible to it. A successful second run does not prove that the first copy was atomic.

Ravel reads back the copy in the cache and compares it with the source. The filesystem of the cache can fail to keep two names apart, for example A.md and a.md on the default macOS volume. It can also change the spelling of a name, for example to another Unicode normalisation form. Then the run refuses as skill_resource_invalid and names the paths. Ravel never writes one entry over another, and it never changes a name. A filesystem that keeps both names keeps both.

Ravel examines each directory on the path to the cache:

DirectoryCondition
Each directory on the pathIt belongs to your account or to root.
Each directory on the pathOther accounts cannot write it, unless it is sticky and your account owns its entry.
The cache root and its directoriesOther accounts have no access.

Ravel keeps the cache directories open while it writes. Before it adds an object, it makes sure that the path still reaches the same directory.

Two runs can add the same object at the same time. A reader sees no object or a complete object. A run that finds an object with the same name reads all of it again. When the object holds other content, the run refuses as skill_cache_corrupt. Ravel does not repair the object, because other readers can use it.

These skill refusals have stage skills and status 3:

CodeCauseRemedy
skill_source_missingA source directory or a configured search directory does not exist. Or a mandatory file names a source that the default skill directory does not hold.Restore or install the source, or change the settings deliberately.
skill_directory_invalidThe installation record is invalid, or an entry of the default skill directory is not a source.Remove or repair the entry, or reinstall the package that installed it.
skill_source_changedA local source changed while Ravel copied it.Keep the source quiet, then try again.
skill_mandatory_invalidA mandatory file is missing, unreadable, not a regular file or not UTF-8.Restore the file as UTF-8 text, or change the reference.
skill_resource_invalidAn entry that a snapshot cannot keep exactly. Examples are an absolute link, a link to a missing target, a link in a cycle and a link out of the source. A special file, an unreadable file and a name that is not exact UTF-8 or holds a control character also refuse. So do two names that the filesystem of the cache cannot keep apart, and a name whose spelling it changes.Repair, rename or remove the named entry.
skill_access_unavailableA source overlaps $HOME/.config/ravel, the settings file or the skill cache, also through a symbolic link. A launch also refuses a source that overlaps the directory of its configuration entry.Move the source.
skill_limit_exceededA count or a size passes a bound of a resolution. At launch, the prepared prompt is more than 1,048,576 bytes.Configure fewer or smaller sources, files or directories, or give a smaller task that refers to other files.
skill_cache_unavailableRavel cannot use the cache. Examples are a permission and full storage. A directory on the path that another account can write, and a link on the path that changed during the run, also refuse.Make the cache a private writable directory of your account, or free storage.
skill_cache_corruptA stored object does not hold the content that its name identifies.Stop its readers, then move it out of the cache or remove it, and resolve again.

A failed resolution leaves no partial descriptor. Complete snapshots and descriptors can remain, for example after cancellation just after storage. A later resolution can reuse them. Ravel removes its own private temporary directories, and no other temporary directories. A failure of that removal after a successful run is skill_cleanup_failed, status 5. After a refusal, the refusal keeps its code and also reports the failure.

A run that stops because of a signal such as KILL can leave a temporary directory. Remove it only when no Ravel run is active.

Default skill directory

Do you install skill sources with a package manager? Then Ravel can read them with no settings. A package, such as a Homebrew formula, exposes its source in the default skill directory. Ravel reads that directory when the settings name no sources. That includes an absent settings file and a settings file without skills. "sources": [] turns the default skill directory off.

Ravel finds the directory through its installation record, the file share/ravel/installation.json below PREFIX. PREFIX is the parent of the directory that holds the canonical path of the Ravel executable. The record is a JSON object with exactly these members:

MemberValue
schemaVersionThe integer 1.
skillDirectoryThe absolute UTF-8 path of the default skill directory. It has no empty segment, ., .., tilde, control character or separator at the end.

Each Homebrew formula of Ravel writes the record at installation. Its skillDirectory is $(brew --prefix)/share/ravel/skills, for example /opt/homebrew/share/ravel/skills. The keg-only ravel@2 names the same directory as the unversioned ravel.

The release archives and task install write no record, because only the installation knows its prefix. A build in target and a copied executable also have no record. Such an installation has no default skill directory. Its settings name the sources, or delivery is disabled.

An absent record is valid. A record that is unreadable, not a regular file, not UTF-8 or not such an object refuses as skill_directory_invalid. A repeated member refuses the same way. Ravel reads no record when the settings name sources.

Ravel lists the default skill directory at each resolution. An absent directory supplies no source and refuses nothing. The table gives the result of each entry:

EntryResult
A name that starts with ., such as .DS_StoreRavel ignores it.
A directory that has a valid source ID as its name and holds skills, craft or bothA source. The entry name is its ID.
A symbolic link whose chain ends at such a directoryA source, as above.
A directory that holds neither skills nor craftRefuses.
A name that is not a valid source IDRefuses.
A regular file, or another object that is not a directoryRefuses.
A link to a missing target, or a link in a cycleRefuses.

Each refusal is skill_directory_invalid, status 3, and it names the entry. Remove the entry, or reinstall the package that installed it. brew cleanup --prune-prefix removes a Homebrew link to a missing target.

The search directories of a default source are skills and craft, in that order, where each exists. Its source path is the path of the entry. Default sources follow the byte order of their IDs. A refusal about a default source names its entry path in place of a settings location.

A mandatory file can name a default source by its ID. An ID that the directory does not hold refuses as skill_source_missing. The JSON routing notice of a launch gives the directory as skills.defaultDirectory.

An upgrade of a package can remove its source for a short time. A resolution in that interval delivers the other sources. A mandatory file in the absent source refuses as skill_source_missing. The default skill directory belongs to the installation, not to a HOME, so a separate HOME receives the same default sources.

A package keeps each source intact. It installs the source as a private copy and adds a relative directory symlink to that copy. A Homebrew formula uses these paths in its keg:

Path in the kegContent
libexec/skill-sources/ID/The private copy of the source, with its executable bits and its links.
share/ravel/skills/IDThe relative directory symlink ../../../libexec/skill-sources/ID.

Homebrew links the symlink into the default skill directory as one entry. Do not install a real source directory under share/ravel/skills. When another keg links into the same directory, Homebrew can replace that source with links to its individual files. Those links go out of the source, so each resolution refuses as skill_resource_invalid. The sources of every other package then fail too.

Bounds, deadline and cancellation

Each resolution has fixed count and byte bounds. A value equal to a bound passes. A larger value refuses as skill_limit_exceeded, status 3. The refusal gives the value, the bound and the subject. Its bound member holds the name and the value of the bound. Ravel never truncates a file, omits a directory or a mandatory file, or disables delivery to meet a bound.

SubjectBoundbound.name
Sources, configured or default16skillSources
Search directories, in all sources together256skillSearchDirectories
Mandatory files256skillMandatoryFiles
One mandatory file65,536 bytesmandatoryFileBytes
One regular file of a source16,777,216 bytes (16 MiB)resourceBytes
The regular files of one source134,217,728 bytes (128 MiB)sourceBytes
The regular files of all sources536,870,912 bytes (512 MiB)totalSourceBytes
The entries of one source, with directories and links20,000sourceEntries
The descriptor1,048,576 bytesdescriptorBytes
The discovery instructions1,048,576 bytespreambleBytes

Ravel checks the three counts before it copies a file, after it lists the default skill directory. It checks the other bounds during the copy, and it stops at the first entry or file that passes a bound. Then the value in the refusal starts with “at least”, because Ravel did not count the rest of the source. Each source adds its bytes to the total, also when two sources name one directory. Ravel checks the descriptor and the discovery instructions before it stores the descriptor.

The setting skills.resolutionTimeoutMs bounds one complete resolution: the copy, the checks and the storage. Its default is 120,000 ms. When that time passes, the run refuses as skill_resolution_timeout, status 124. The refusal gives the deadline and its origin. It also gives the source and the operation that were active. Raise the setting for valid sources on slow storage.

Ravel examines the time between operations: at each entry, at each block of 64 KiB of a copy and at each entry of a check. Ravel cannot interrupt one operating-system call that storage holds. The refusal then comes after that call returns.

An INT, TERM or HUP signal during a resolution stops it at the next examination. Ravel removes its private temporary directory, and it never leaves a partial descriptor. A signal that arrives after Ravel stores the descriptor still cancels the resolution. The complete descriptor then stays, as after any other refusal. The refusal is skill_resolution_cancelled, with status 128 plus the signal number. Ravel then raises the same signal again, so the caller sees that signal.

SignalStatus of skill_resolution_cancelled
INT130
TERM143
HUP129

A signal that arrives before another refusal wins over that refusal. The report keeps the other refusal as the original failure. A launch applies the same deadline and the same cancellation before its worker starts.

Skill delivery at launch

When the settings enable skill delivery, start-session prepares the sources before it evaluates the configuration script. It runs the same capture and checks as skills resolve. This step comes after Ravel reads the settings, the caller inputs and the configuration script entry. It comes before the worker and the result channel. Thus, a skill refusal starts no worker and no managed process.

A launch also protects the directory of the selected configuration entry. A source that overlaps that directory refuses as skill_access_unavailable. With --config, that directory can be outside $HOME/.config/ravel.

The prepared prompt has these parts, in this order:

  1. The discovery instructions that skills resolve prints.
  2. An empty line, then the line Caller task.
  3. The line BEGIN RAVEL CALLER TASK NONCE.
  4. The bytes of the caller’s prompt, unchanged.
  5. A newline, then the line END RAVEL CALLER TASK NONCE, with a final newline.

NONCE is 32 lowercase hexadecimal digits from the random source of the operating system. The nonce occurs only in the two delimiter lines. When it occurs elsewhere in the prompt, Ravel chooses a new nonce. The newline before the last delimiter line belongs to the wrapper, also when the caller’s prompt ends with a newline. An empty caller prompt gives an empty block, and the discovery instructions stay.

The prepared prompt has a maximum of 1,048,576 bytes. A larger prepared prompt refuses as skill_limit_exceeded, and the message gives both byte counts. Ravel never truncates the task and never omits a directory.

This table gives each place where a launch delivers the prepared sources:

PlaceWhat it holds
request.promptThe prepared prompt
request.originalPromptThe caller’s prompt alone
request.skillsdescriptorPath, sources, directories and mandatory, as in the descriptor
RAVEL_SKILL_DIRECTORIESThe descriptor path, in the managed process and in each harness run
The JSON routing noticeThe skills member

Ravel does not change the arguments that the script selects. A script that ignores request.prompt does not deliver the directories.

A source that overlaps $HOME/.config/ravel, the settings file, the skill cache or the result channel of an enclosing session also refuses as skill_access_unavailable, status 3. Give the agent the owner’s skill directories gives each protected path. The snapshots are read-only by their file modes. Ravel does not restrict the other access of the managed process to the cache.

Exit statuses

Your program ran ravel start-session or ravel end-session and received an exit status. What does it mean, and what do you do next? Find the status in the table below. The status is the exit status of the Ravel CLI, not the exit status of the managed process.

ravel skills resolve uses only 0, 2, 3, 5, 124 and 128 plus a signal number, with the meanings in its section.

For start-session, accept the result on standard output only when the final status is 0. Neither the exit status of the managed process nor an earlier successful end-session decides the result of the enclosing session.

Status 0 needs complete cleanup. In cleanup, Ravel stops the processes that remain in the process group of the managed process. Ravel reaps the managed process, which collects its exit status from the operating system. Ravel then confirms that the group is gone and removes the private result channel.

Cancellation has precedence over an original failure. Failed harness cleanup can replace an evaluation deadline with harness_cleanup_failed, status 5. After evaluation, an original failure keeps its status if later cleanup also fails. Required cleanup failures prevent success. All applicable cleanup reports stay as diagnostics. See outcome precedence.

StatusMeaning and next actionTaught in
0start-session wrote the sent result unchanged to standard output and flushed it. This occurs after the reap, the confirmed absence of the group and the required cleanup. An empty result is valid. A later nonzero exit of the managed process does not make the sent result invalid. Ravel does not interpret the result. Interpret it with the caller’s convention. For end-session, status 0 confirms only that it sent the result, or that a valid first result was already present. An explicit help or version request also gives status 0.Accept a result
1start-session reports session_result_missing, even when the managed process exits 0. For end-session, status 1 means that it could not send the result. For example, the session-result context was missing, empty or stale, or the value was too large. Check that the call occurs inside a live session. A missing result is not an empty result.The sent result and its absence
2Malformed command-line input or settings, an unusable --exit-dir, or a withdrawn confinement flag. Without a verb, ravel prints help with this status. Correct the named input or setting before you try again.Caller inputs, what the managed process receives
3The configuration script refused, or Ravel could not accept its entry, behaviour, shape or selection result, or the selection context. Evaluation bounds and a start-session inside an evaluation harness run also use 3. No fallback route runs. Read the code, the stage and the remedy. For config_refused, also read configCode. Skill-source, resource and cache refusals also use 3. Read their skills stage and remedy.Select or refuse
5A worker or supervision failed, or the result was invalid. Required cleanup or terminal recovery failed, a group survived, or the result output failed. A failed worker re-execution, identity mismatch or evaluation harness cleanup also gives 5. Retain the diagnostics, repair the installation or the named resource, and discard any partial result bytes.Operational diagnostics
124The evaluation of the configuration script timed out, and Ravel launched no managed process. Examine the work of the script before you increase timeoutMs or --timeout-ms. Failed harness cleanup or cancellation can replace this outcome. The deadline does not limit the managed process. Skill resolution has a separate deadline, skills.resolutionTimeoutMs, and reports skill_resolution_timeout with 124 before worker evaluation.Bounds
126The selected program is not executable, or the launch failed before the runner reported the start, for a reason other than ENOENT. Check the permissions, the format, the interpreter and the execution environment. Retain any recovery reports.Program resolution
127Ravel did not find the selected program, or the launch failed with ENOENT before the start. A script that exists can have a missing interpreter. Check PATH, the selected path and the interpreter.Program resolution
129Cancellation by HUP (signal 1), during skill resolution, selection or supervision. Ravel returns no result to the caller, even if it already received one. Retain the cleanup reports, and try again only if the caller still wants the work.Cancellation
130Cancellation by INT (signal 2), during skill resolution, selection or supervision. Ravel returns no result to the caller, even if it already received one. A keyboard Ctrl-C that goes only to the group of the managed process is not necessarily a cancellation of Ravel.Cancellation and terminal INT
143Cancellation by TERM (signal 15), during skill resolution, selection or supervision. Ravel returns no result to the caller, even if it already received one. Retain the cleanup reports, and try again only if the caller did not intend the termination.Cancellation

The three signal statuses in the table follow the shell convention: 128 plus the signal number. Ravel attempts the cleanup and then raises the same signal again. Thus, a process API reports termination by a signal. If the propagation fails, Ravel reports the failure and uses the numeric status in its place. A caller that uses a process API handles both cases. If a signal is ignored or blocked when Ravel starts, that signal does not automatically cause a cancellation.

Note: KILL or another abrupt stop can end Ravel before it decides the final outcome. A signal that arrives after the final decision about cancellation can interrupt the result write. Neither case confirms the cleanup or that the caller received the complete result. A failed final write can leave a prefix on standard output. The row for an abrupt stop in Know when Ravel cannot guarantee cleanup describes the first case. Interpret the exit status describes the second.

Streams and notices

Standard streams

Your program reads the result from one stream and the diagnostics from another. Which stream carries what? The table gives the answer for each standard stream of ravel start-session.

Table — Streams: Standard output carries only the result. Standard error combines the managed output and the diagnostics. Standard input serves the managed process and does not implicitly supply a prompt.

StreamSession behaviourTaught in
Standard outputstart-session writes only the result of a successful session, unchanged and with no added newline. Ravel does not interpret it. An empty sent result is zero bytes. A failed final write can leave a prefix, so check the status. end-session writes nothing to standard output.Accept a result
Standard errorThe standard output and standard error of the managed process, Ravel notices and failures, captured diagnostics of the configuration script and operational reports. Without --json, each runner operation report has the form ravel: <report>. --json changes only Ravel’s routing notice and failure document.Read notices and runner operation reports
Evaluation harness outputCaptured standard output, standard error and optional result return to the configuration script as data. They reach a caller stream only if the script copies them into visible output.Harness runs
Standard inputRavel does not implicitly read a prompt from standard input. The worker receives a null standard input. A harness run receives its requested text or EOF. The managed process can use the caller’s standard input.Caller inputs, what the managed process receives

What does the configuration script give back to Ravel? It gives back a selection or a refusal. A selection is the map that select returns when it chooses a program. It holds program and args, the command that Ravel runs. It also holds the labels provider, model, effort and reason. A label describes the choice of the script, but it does not configure the harness.

The caller does not read the owner’s configuration script. Thus, Ravel tells the caller what the script chose in the routing notice:

  • Who writes it: the Ravel CLI.
  • When: in text form, after the configuration script selects a program and before Ravel launches it. In JSON form, after the supervision attempt.
  • Where: the caller’s standard error. Standard output carries only the result.
  • Who reads it: the caller, or the person who watches the terminal.
  • Why: this notice is the way in which Ravel tells the caller which harness, model and effort the configuration script chose.

Fragment: the text routing notice of chapter 1’s real run of the review, as Ravel writes it on standard error. The script of that run selects a fake agent, not Claude Code. This block omits the other lines on standard error.

ravel: starting provider local, model fake, effort low for kind "review"

The selection separates the command that Ravel launches from the labels that the caller reads.

Figure: The review selection reaches the program and the caller by separate paths.

Open the selection and routing notice at full size.

The line names the provider, model, effort and kind. These are the labels of the selection and the caller’s kind. The line does not show program or args.

Ravel writes other lines on standard error too. Each one has a known first part:

First part of the lineWho writes it, and what it means
ravel: starting providerThe Ravel CLI. The text routing notice.
config stdout: or config stderr:The Ravel CLI, with one line of output of the configuration script. print writes to the standard output of the script, and debug writes to its standard error.
ravel: refused (CODE, stage STAGE):The Ravel CLI. A failure. The word “refused” also covers operational failures, and the code and the status show which failure it is. The diagnostic codes entry shows a real example.
ravel: end-session:ravel end-session. It could not send the result.
ravel: and a runner reportThe Ravel CLI. A report of the runner about the stop of a process or the cleanup.

Any other line comes from the managed process, or from a program that it started. The managed process can also write a line that looks like a Ravel line. Thus, the form of a line does not prove that Ravel wrote it. See read notices.

JSON routing notice

You may want your program to record what the configuration script chose and what evaluation used, for each session. With --json, the Ravel CLI writes the routing notice as one JSON document on the caller’s standard error. The text routing notice names only the provider, model, effort and kind. The JSON document also gives the reason, the program file that Ravel found, the files of the configuration script and the bounds.

Ravel prepares the --json routing notice after three steps succeed: the selection, the reservation of the result output and the allocation of the result channel. It prints the notice after the supervision attempt, before it decides the final outcome. The notice does not prove the launch, the cleanup or success. Standard error stays a mixed stream, and the managed process can print text that looks like a Ravel document. See read notices.

Fragment: standard error of the --json review command of the example notices in chapter 5, “Read notices separately from results”. It comes from a real run on macOS. The run used the directory /tmp/ravel-chapter-5/notices in place of ~/ravel-chapter-5/notices, and macOS shows /tmp as /private/tmp. Ravel writes the JSON document as one line. This block shows it on several lines and omits the members bounds, context, diagnostics, operationReports, configEnvironment, reads and worker. The value of selectionMs changes in each run.

review-agent: the review is complete
{
  "harnessRuns": [],
  "config": {
    "modules": [
      {
        "bytes": 619,
        "name": "/private/tmp/ravel-chapter-5/notices/config.rhai",
        "sha256": "9d4526b5db2b7f9d9c8812bfc183a841133f1b2b18b20981049770e90efb1b91"
      }
    ],
    "path": "/private/tmp/ravel-chapter-5/notices/config.rhai",
    "version": "notice-demo"
  },
  "promptSource": "/private/tmp/ravel-chapter-5/notices/review-prompt.txt",
  "resolution": "/tmp/ravel-chapter-5/notices/bin/review-agent (found on PATH in /tmp/ravel-chapter-5/notices/bin)",

    "reason": "Reviews go to the fake agent."
