The table maps question to low, bugfix to medium, and feature to high. It maps migration and architecture to xhigh. A missing kind returns the owner refusal incomplete_mapping. These names belong to the sample, not Ravel’s vocabulary.

The choice sample reads at most 4,096 bytes and splits names at whitespace. It preserves their order and duplicates, and searches no parent directory. Successful reads appear in the notice. See let a repository choose an arrangement.

Choice inputOutcome
Missing file, also a dangling symlinkUse default.
Exactly defaultReturn the starter selection unchanged.
Exactly carefulPreserve program, model and provider. Set effort to high, rebuild the arguments, and use reason careful choice. A starter refusal stays a refusal.
Empty file or multiple offered namesReturn owner code one_choice.
An unoffered nameReturn owner code choice_unoffered.
Unreadable, non-regular or invalid-UTF-8 fileReturn owner code choice_unreadable.
More than 4,096 bytes, or a read limit above contextBytesEnd evaluation as source_too_large. The sample cannot catch a bound breach.

llm.rhai needs a claude settings entry and at least one harness run in the budget. It accepts only an allowed table key from a successful structured answer. After an unavailable, unsuccessful or invalid answer, it uses the caller’s kind instead. An exhausted budget or whole deadline still ends evaluation. The Claude Code recipe teaches that call, and the Codex recipe teaches its file-based schema alternative.

Installed layout

Where are the files of an installed Ravel? You may want the executable for a caller’s configuration, or the example configuration scripts to copy.

PREFIX is the installation root, independent of the owner’s HOME. For the versioned Homebrew formula, brew --prefix ravel@2 gives this path. After promotion, brew --prefix ravel gives the unversioned installation’s path. Chapter 3 selects the versioned executable for this book’s examples.

Ravel 2.0.0 installs one executable with the Rhai engine inside it. It needs no separate language runtime or source checkout. See install Ravel.

Path below PREFIXPurpose
bin/ravelThe Rust CLI, linked runner and embedded Rhai engine. Ravel re-executes this binary for its separate Rhai worker.
share/ravel/examples/static.rhaiEditable starter table.
share/ravel/examples/sample.rhaiEditable choice script, with an import of static.rhai.
share/ravel/examples/llm.rhaiEditable model-assisted script, with an import of static.rhai.
share/ravel/notices/THIRD-PARTY-NOTICES.mdThe licence notices of the Rust dependencies inside bin/ravel.
share/ravel/installation.jsonOnly in a Homebrew installation. The installation record, which names the default skill directory.

The installation, the release archive and the Homebrew formula carry the same notice file. The archive carries the project README.md and LICENSE at its root. The delivery chapter gives release archive details.

The following figures show source responsibilities and installed files. Ravel’s runner is library code in the CLI, not a service or another executable. The Rhai worker is a separate process of the same binary. Ravel completes host calls, reaps the worker and removes its directory before the final managed launch.

Figure — Source responsibilities: This C4 view groups internal code by process responsibility. The same linked runner supervises evaluation harness runs and the final managed process at different times. It does not define a sandbox or a supported library interface.

Figure link: Open the source-responsibility view at full size.

Figure — Installed files: This deployment view groups installed files by directory. One executable supplies both the CLI and the separate Rhai worker. The three Rhai examples remain editable files. Directory outlines do not show concurrent process lifetimes.

Figure link: Open the installed-file view at full size.

Note: Ravel supports no separate component. Another program invokes the CLI as a child process. The private runner can change process-wide signal state and does not restore that state on return. Chapter 1 explains the supported integration route.

Note: Native x86-64 Linux qualification remains open. The row for a different host in Know when Ravel cannot guarantee cleanup distinguishes native measurements from emulation.

The parts of Ravel

Do you want to change Ravel, or find the part that caused a failure? Then you need to know where each part of Ravel lives and why it is a separate part. This chapter gives you that map. It starts with one session that makes each part visible in its output. Then it names the parts, gives their source locations and explains how they work together.

In chapter 3 you ran the first session in ~/ravel-first-session. Its configuration script selected fake-agent, and the caller received finished. That session used every part of Ravel, but its output showed only the routing notice and the result. The example of this chapter adds one harness run. Its fake programs then write down who started them and what they received.

These are the words that this chapter uses. Each row gives the value that the word has in the example of this chapter.

WordWhat it namesIn the example
Ravel CLIThe ravel command-line program. It checks the request, runs the programs and decides the outcome.The process ravel start-session --kind demo …
Configuration scriptThe owner’s Rhai file that selects a program or refusesconfig.rhai
Rhai workerA second copy of the ravel executable that evaluates the configuration scriptIt runs select in config.rhai
Host capabilityAn operation that the script asks the Ravel CLI to do, because the worker cannot do itravel::run_harness
Harness runOne run of a program from the harnesses settings, during evaluation. Its output returns to the script.fake-advisor, which prints short
Managed processThe program that the script selects. It does the caller’s work.fake-agent
Result channelA private temporary directory with one path for a result file. The path goes to a program in RAVEL_EXIT_FILE.One for fake-advisor, one for fake-agent
RunnerThe Rust library code in the Ravel CLI that launches and supervises a jobIt runs both fakes

Watch the parts in one session

The example of this chapter is ravel-parts. Its directory is ~/ravel-parts. It has the same shape as the first session of chapter 3. It uses settings that name one harness, and adds the fake harness itself.

When you finish the steps, the directory holds these paths:

~/ravel-parts/
├── settings.json       you save it
├── config.rhai         you save it
├── bin/                you make it
│   ├── fake-advisor    you save it
│   └── fake-agent      you save it
├── home/               the first command makes it
├── tmp/                the first command makes it
└── …                   the fakes and the command write five files

The process view separates the harness run from the managed process. Each uses its own result channel.

Open the process authority view at full size.

Each path has one purpose:

PathWho makes itPurpose
settings.jsonYouNames the harness entry advisor, permits one harness run and disables skill delivery. The first command copies it into the private home.
config.rhaiYouAsks the harness run for advice. Then it selects fake-agent and gives it the advice as its argument.
bin/fake-advisorYouThe fake harness. It writes advisor.txt and advisor.path, prints short and sends the result advice.
bin/fake-agentYouThe fake agent. It writes agent.txt and agent.path, then sends the result finished.
home/The first commandAn empty home directory. The first command puts the settings file at home/.config/ravel/settings.json.
tmp/The first commandA private temporary directory. Ravel makes each result channel here.
resultThe second commandThe standard output of ravel start-session, which is the result of the session

Do these steps in the shell where ravel --version succeeded in chapter 3:

  1. Make the example directory and its bin subdirectory, then cd into the example directory. The fragment after these steps gives the two commands.
  2. Save the four files of the complete example at their labelled paths.
  3. Type each command of the complete example without its first $ .
  4. Compare the output and the exit status with the example.

Fragment: make the example directory and cd into it. This block omits the four files. The book checker makes its own directory for each example and does not run this block.

mkdir -p ~/ravel-parts/bin
cd ~/ravel-parts

Complete example: a configuration script asks a fake harness for advice, then selects a fake agent. Both fakes write down who started them.

settings.json

{"harnesses":{"advisor":{"program":"fake-advisor"}},"budgets":{"harnessRuns":1},"skills":{"schemaVersion":1,"sources":[]}}

config.rhai

export const SCHEMA_VERSION = 4;
export const VERSION = "parts";

fn select(request, context) {
    let run = ravel::run_harness(#{ harness: "advisor", args: [] });
    print(`The advisor run ended: ${run.status}, result ${run.result}.`);
    #{
        status: "selected",
        program: "fake-agent",
        args: [run.stdout],
        provider: "local",
        model: "fake",
        effort: "none",
        reason: "Show each part of Ravel.",
    }
}

bin/fake-advisor

#!/bin/sh
{
  echo "advisor: parent is $(ps -o args= -p "$PPID" | cut -d ' ' -f 1-2)"
  echo "advisor: RAVEL_CONFIG_RUN is ${RAVEL_CONFIG_RUN-absent}"
} > advisor.txt
printf '%s\n' "$RAVEL_EXIT_FILE" > advisor.path
printf 'short'
ravel end-session -- "advice"

bin/fake-agent

#!/bin/sh
{
  echo "agent: parent is $(ps -o args= -p "$PPID" | cut -d ' ' -f 1-2)"
  echo "agent: RAVEL_CONFIG_RUN is ${RAVEL_CONFIG_RUN-absent}"
  echo "agent: argument is $1"
} > agent.txt
printf '%s\n' "$RAVEL_EXIT_FILE" > agent.path
ravel end-session -- "finished"

Command

$ mkdir -p home/.config/ravel tmp && chmod +x bin/* && cp settings.json home/.config/ravel/settings.json
$ env -i HOME="$PWD/home" TMPDIR="$PWD/tmp" PATH="$PWD/bin:$PATH" ravel start-session --kind demo --config config.rhai --prompt 'Show the parts of Ravel.' > result

Standard output

Standard error includes

config stdout: The advisor run ended: exited, result advice.
ravel: starting provider local, model fake, effort none for kind "demo"

Exit status

0

Command

$ cat advisor.txt agent.txt && cat result && printf '\n'
$ test "$(cat advisor.path)" != "$(cat agent.path)" && echo "The two result paths differ."
$ rmdir tmp && echo "The directory tmp is empty again."

Standard output

advisor: parent is ravel start-session
advisor: RAVEL_CONFIG_RUN is 1
agent: parent is ravel start-session
agent: RAVEL_CONFIG_RUN is absent
agent: argument is short
finished
The two result paths differ.
The directory tmp is empty again.

Exit status

0

The first command block copies settings.json into the private home, because Ravel reads settings only from $HOME/.config/ravel/settings.json. Its second line sends the result to the file result, so that the next block can show it beside the other files. On macOS, standard error can also show ravel: stop attempt lines. Chapter 3 explains these operation reports.

The second command block only reads what the session left. ps -o args= prints the arguments of a process, and $PPID is the process ID of the fake’s parent. cut keeps the first two words. rmdir removes a directory only when it is empty, so its success shows that Ravel removed both result channels.

Each line of the output comes from one part of Ravel:

OutputWhat it showsPart of Ravel
config stdout: The advisor run ended: exited, result advice.The script ran, called a host capability and received the run as data. Ravel shows the script’s print output on its own standard error.The Rhai worker and the host bridge
advisor: parent is ravel start-sessionThe Ravel CLI started the harness run. The worker asked for the run but did not start it.The host bridge and the runner
advisor: RAVEL_CONFIG_RUN is 1A harness run receives a marker. A start-session inside the run would refuse.The harness-run code of the Ravel CLI
ravel: starting provider local, …The Ravel CLI checked the selection and wrote the routing notice, after the worker endedThe Ravel CLI
agent: parent is ravel start-sessionThe Ravel CLI launched the managed process tooThe runner
agent: RAVEL_CONFIG_RUN is absentThe managed process receives no marker, so it can start a nested sessionThe Ravel CLI
agent: argument is shortThe advice went from the harness run, through the script, into the arguments of the managed processThe host bridge and the Ravel CLI
finishedThe result of the managed process reached the caller after cleanupThe result channel
The two result paths differ.Each program received its own result channelThe result channel
The directory tmp is empty again.Ravel removed both result channelsThe result channel and the Ravel CLI

Thus, the Ravel CLI starts every program in a session. The Rhai worker starts none. It computes, and it asks the Ravel CLI for anything else. The rest of this chapter explains each part and why it is separate.

Find each part in the source

The source of Ravel is one Cargo workspace with two Rust packages: ravel and keyed-launch. A package is not always a process. The runner is a package, but it runs inside the Ravel CLI. The Rhai worker is no package of its own, but it is a separate process. The table gives each part with its source location and the process where it runs. Paths are relative to the root of a checkout.

PartSource locationWhere it runsIn the example
Ravel CLIcrates/ravel/src/main.rs, run.rs, choice.rs and the other modules of the executableThe ravel start-session processChecks the request, writes the routing notice and returns finished
Rhai workercrates/ravel/src/rhai_worker/, mainly role.rs, engine.rs and bridge.rsA second ravel process, during evaluation onlyRuns select and calls ravel::run_harness
Host bridge, CLI sidecrates/ravel/src/rhai_worker/front.rs, serve.rs, host.rs, reader.rs and harness.rsInside the Ravel CLI, during evaluation onlyReceives the request for the advisor run and starts it
Skill preparationcrates/ravel/src/skills/Inside the Ravel CLI, before the worker startsThe example’s settings disable delivery and the default skill directory of a Homebrew installation.
Runnercrates/keyed-launch/src/Inside the Ravel CLIRuns fake-advisor, then fake-agent
Result channelcrates/ravel/src/completion.rs, exported by crates/ravel/src/lib.rsInside the Ravel CLI and inside each ravel end-session processCarries advice to the script and finished to the caller

The figure shows the same parts and their relations. The dashed outline is the Ravel CLI process.

Open the source-responsibility view at full size.

The runner is library code, not a service. ravel end-session is another run of the same ravel executable. It is not a request to a daemon that stays alive. Chapter 2 shows the same processes in its process-and-authority figure.

The Ravel CLI coordinates the session

The Ravel CLI is the only part that knows every condition for a successful session. It connects the configuration script’s choice to the supervision of the job, and it decides what the caller receives.

main in crates/ravel/src/main.rs chooses the work of the process. First it compares its own argv[0] with ravel-rhai-config-worker. On a match, the process is the Rhai worker, and main gives control to the worker code. The worker section below explains this name. Otherwise main reads the command line and runs one of three verbs:

VerbFunction that main callsWhat it does
start-sessionrun::run in crates/ravel/src/run.rsRoutes the request and supervises one session
end-session RESULTend_session in crates/ravel/src/completion.rsSends one result to the enclosing session
skills resolveskills::command::run in crates/ravel/src/skills/command.rsCaptures the skill sources and prints the instructions that a prompt would receive, without a session

start-session does its work in a fixed order. Each step needs the step before it. The last column gives the step in the example.

StepWhat the Ravel CLI doesWhereIn the example
1Refuses when RAVEL_CONFIG_RUN is in its environmentrun::runThe caller’s environment has no marker
2Reads the settings and the caller inputs, then finds the configuration scriptchoice::prepareReads home/.config/ravel/settings.json and finds config.rhai
3Resolves the skill sources and prepares the promptskills::launch::prepareThe example disables delivery, so the prompt stays unchanged
4Starts the Rhai worker, answers its host calls, checks its reply and reaps it. Then it finds the selected program on PATH.choice::choose_preparedStarts fake-advisor for the script, receives the selection of fake-agent and finds bin/fake-agent
5Makes the result channel, writes the routing notice and launches the managed processrun::runLaunches fake-agent with its own RAVEL_EXIT_FILE
6Reads the result after cleanup, decides the outcome and writes the resultrun::runWrites finished and exits with status 0

A failure stops the order at its step. For example, a skill refusal in step 3 starts no worker and makes no result channel.

The Ravel CLI also keeps the caller’s environment, terminal and standard output while the worker runs. It reserves standard output for the result. The output of the managed process and the diagnostics of Ravel go to standard error. Thus, a configuration script cannot write the result of the session. In the example, the script’s print output appears on standard error with the prefix config stdout:.

The ravel package has a library target and an executable target. The library exports SessionChannel, publish_result, end_session and the result constants. The executable uses this library and keyed-launch. This division lets the result-channel tests use a channel with the runner, with no configuration script.

Neither the worker nor the runner knows every condition for success. For example, the runner can report a reaped child while the Ravel CLI still has a temporary directory to remove. Thus, a change to the final output or to the order of failures belongs in the coordination code of the Ravel CLI, mainly in run.rs.

The table gives each decision one owner:

DecisionOwnerWhat crosses the boundaryIn the example
Which program to selectConfiguration scriptA command or a refusalfake-agent with the argument short
Whether the script’s reply is validRavel CLIA checked command, or no launchThe reply has every required field
How to launch and stop a jobRunnerLaunch options from the Ravel CLITwo jobs, each with its own channel
Whether to return the resultRavel CLIJob observations and cleanup reportsBoth jobs ended, and cleanup succeeded
What the result meansCallerThe result, unchangedfinished

The Rhai worker evaluates the script in its own process

Why does Ravel run the configuration script in a separate process? Because the Ravel CLI must stay in control while the script runs, whatever the script does. The decision record “Configuration evaluation precedes the launch” gives the reasons. Its file in the repository is docs/adr/configuration-evaluation-precedes-the-launch.md. The table restates the reasons in your terms:

Problem that a thread inside the Ravel CLI would haveWhat the separate process gives
A thread cannot stop a Rhai function that does not yield. Rhai 1.27.0 has two: sleep, and pad when its fill string is empty.At the deadline, the Ravel CLI attempts worker teardown through signals. A kernel stall can delay exit.
Rhai limits the size of each value, but not the heap. A memory exhaustion or a stack overflow on a thread stops the whole Ravel CLI without cleanup.Such a failure stops only the worker. The Ravel CLI reports it and launches nothing.
Host capabilities inside the worker would make the worker the parent of each harness runThe Ravel CLI does each host capability, so a kill of the worker leaves no harness run without a parent

The price is a request and reply protocol for every host call, and the code that starts, watches and reaps the worker. The record says when to reopen the decision: when Rhai can interrupt every native function and Ravel can limit the heap of one thread.

How does the Ravel CLI start the worker? It starts its own executable again, with argv[0] set to ravel-rhai-config-worker. On Linux it executes /proc/self/exe, which names the image that runs now. On macOS it executes the canonical path of its own executable.

The name ravel-rhai-config-worker is an internal process identity. It is not an installed file or a command for you to run. crates/ravel/src/rhai_worker/front.rs holds this launch, and crates/ravel/src/worker.rs supervises the process.

The worker receives very little from the Ravel CLI:

The worker hasThe worker does not have
A private empty directory with mode 0700, as its working directoryThe caller’s environment. Its environment is empty.
Standard input from /dev/nullRAVEL_EXIT_FILE, so it cannot end a session
A private socket at file descriptor 3RAVEL_SKILL_DIRECTORIES in its environment, or other inherited file descriptors
Standard output and standard error, which the Ravel CLI reads with a size limitAccess to files, processes or the network. The Rhai engine has no such functions.

The first message on the socket is the worker’s hello. It holds the protocol number 1, the package version, the build identity and the Rhai version. The Ravel CLI compares these values with its own before it sends the request.

A different build identity refuses as worker_identity_mismatch. A worker that cannot start refuses as worker_missing. The identity shows that the worker is the same build as the Ravel CLI. It does not prove that the installation belongs to its owner.

The worker runs the script on one thread with a fixed set of engine limits. Chapter 6 lists them. An allocator in crates/ravel/src/rhai_worker/heap.rs counts each live byte of the worker and limits its heap to 1 GiB. When an allocation would pass that limit, the worker reports memory_limit and exits.

After it sends its reply, the worker exits. The Ravel CLI reaps it and removes its private directory. Only then can the managed process launch. If the reap or the removal fails, nothing launches. Thus, no code of the configuration script runs while the managed process runs.

Ravel 1.x ran TypeScript configuration scripts in a Bun worker. Ravel 2.0.0 removed that worker. Thus, the installation has no JavaScript runtime, no SDK and no declaration file. It has no separate worker executable under libexec either.

The decision record “The configuration script uses Rhai”, in docs/adr/configuration-script-uses-rhai.md, gives the reason. Ravel chose a small embedded language with no effects of its own, in place of a typed language with packages. Chapter 4 teaches the language.

The host bridge carries each request to the Ravel CLI

The worker cannot read a file or start a program. So how did the example’s script run fake-advisor? It asked the Ravel CLI through the host bridge: the code on both sides of the private socket that carries host calls and their replies. Follow the advisor call:

OrderWho actsWhereWhat happens in the example
1The configuration scriptconfig.rhaiCalls ravel::run_harness(#{ harness: "advisor", args: [] })
2The Rhai workerrhai_worker/bridge.rsSends a host frame with the call run_harness, then waits. The script sees an ordinary call that returns a value.
3The Ravel CLI, runtime threadrhai_worker/serve.rsReceives the frame and gives the call to the reader thread. It continues to watch the deadline, cancellation, the output limit and the worker.
4The Ravel CLI, reader threadrhai_worker/reader.rs, harness.rsFinds the program fake-advisor for the entry advisor, makes a result channel and runs the program through the runner
5The runnercrates/keyed-launch/src/run.rs, run_capturedSupervises fake-advisor in a new POSIX session, captures its output and stops its process group after it exits
6The Ravel CLIrhai_worker/harness.rs, host.rsRemoves the run’s result channel and sends a reply frame with the run’s map
7The Rhai workerrhai_worker/bridge.rsGives the map to the script. run.stdout is short, and run.result is advice.

The sequence separates the two CLI threads. The runtime can watch the worker while the reader waits for the harness run.

Open the host bridge sequence at full size.

Why are there two threads in the Ravel CLI during evaluation? The Ravel CLI serves the socket from a Tokio runtime on one thread. Tokio is the Rust library for asynchronous input and output. That runtime thread never waits for a file or a program. A separate reader thread does all the file work and every harness run.

Thus, a slow read or a long harness run never stops the code that watches the deadline and cancellation. The reader is a plain thread, not a task that spawn_blocking starts. Tokio cannot stop such a task while it runs. Also, the end of a runtime waits for each such task.

Each message on the socket is one frame: a length, then a JSON document. The module comment of crates/ravel/src/rhai_worker/mod.rs lists every frame and its fields:

FrameFromWhat it carriesIn the example
helloWorkerIts identitySent first
evaluateRavel CLIThe entry’s source, the request and the limitsThe source of config.rhai
hostWorkerOne host call and where the script made itThe advisor run
replyRavel CLIThe value of that call, or an error that the script can catchThe run’s map
contextWorkerWhat load_context returnedNot sent: the script has no load_context
deliveredRavel CLIThe selection context that select receivesNot sent
selectionWorkerThe script’s VERSION and what select returnedparts and the selection of fake-agent
failureWorkerA failure of the script, with its locationNot sent

The Ravel CLI reads the entry file itself and sends its source in the evaluate frame. The worker reads no file, also for its own script. An import in the script is a host call too. The Ravel CLI reads the module and sends its source back.

The worker uses seven host calls. Other ravel:: functions are data helpers, which run inside the worker and send no frame:

Host callRhai functionWhat the Ravel CLI does
read_text, read_jsonravel::read_text, ravel::read_jsonReads and measures a file, within the limits of the settings
importAn import statementResolves and reads a module
envravel::envReads a variable of the caller’s environment that the settings grant
harnessesravel::harnessesReports the harness entries of the settings and whether each program is available
remainingravel::remainingReports what the evaluation still has of its budgets and deadline
run_harnessravel::run_harnessRuns a harness entry through the runner and returns the outcome as a map

An LLM call is a harness run. The script runs Claude Code or Codex noninteractively and reads the answer from the run’s output. Ravel has no model adapter, holds no model credential and opens no network connection.

The script is synchronous, so only one host call runs at a time. The worker sends its reply only after the reply to its last host call. Thus, no host call is in progress when evaluation ends. The Ravel CLI then shuts down the runtime, and no task of it remains when the managed process launches.

The configuration module defines the interface

The configuration script is a Rhai module. Its interface to Ravel is four exports and the ravel namespace:

Export or functionPurposeLimit
SCHEMA_VERSIONNames the request and selection shape. Ravel 2.0.0 accepts only 4.Another value refuses
VERSIONNames the script’s own version in diagnosticsA string that is not empty
select(request, context)Selects a command or refusesconfig::judge_result checks the returned map before any launch
load_context(request)Optional. Assembles selection contextSelection context gives no result authority
ravel:: host callsAsk the Ravel CLI for an effectThe settings limit each one
ravel:: data helpers, such as ravel::json_checkCompute in the workerThey give no access to files, processes or the network

The default entry is $HOME/.config/ravel/config.rhai. The option --config names another Rhai file. The settings stay at their path in either case.

Where does a change belong? A new route usually belongs in the configuration script alone, with no change to Ravel. A new host capability crosses every part of the host bridge:

  • the Rhai function and its frame in rhai_worker/bridge.rs,
  • the answer of the Ravel CLI in rhai_worker/host.rs or serve.rs,
  • the conversion and checks of its data in rhai_worker/convert.rs,
  • its limits and its section of the specification configuration-evaluation.md.

The Ravel CLI prepares skill directories before the worker starts

Do you want each agent to find the owner’s skills? A skill is a directory of guidance files for an agent, such as a SKILL.md and the files that it names. The owner names skill sources in the skills settings. Ravel copies them, tells the agent where they are and leaves the choice of skills to the agent.

The Ravel CLI does this work in step 3, before the worker starts:

  1. It finds the source set. Without skills.sources, it reads the default skill directory that the record of its installation names.
  2. It copies each source into its cache, $HOME/.cache/ravel/skills/v1, and checks the copy.
  3. It writes one descriptor, directories.json, with the directories and the mandatory files.
  4. It prepares the prompt: a preamble with the directories, the mandatory files and the descriptor path, then the caller’s prompt.

The prepared prompt and the descriptor path then reach each part in a different way:

WhoThe prepared promptRAVEL_SKILL_DIRECTORIES, the descriptor path
The configuration script, in the workerIn request.prompt. The caller’s prompt stays in request.originalPrompt.Not in its environment. The script reads request.skills.
Each harness runOnly when the script passes itIn its environment
The managed processWhen the script passes it, as before, usually in an argumentIn its environment

In the example, the settings name an empty skill-source array. Thus, delivery is off in both the checkout build and a Homebrew installation. Without that explicit array, a Homebrew installation reads its default skill directory and can add a preamble to the prompt.

Why does the Ravel CLI prepare skills, and not the script? The decision record “Skill preparation precedes evaluation”, in docs/adr/skill-preparation-precedes-evaluation.md, gives the reason. One owner gives the same behaviour to every script, and the script still builds its own command. A helper that each script calls would make delivery depend on each script. The source of this step is in crates/ravel/src/skills/. launch.rs holds the step, and the other modules copy, check, cache and list the sources.

The runner owns the local job mechanics

keyed-launch holds the code that launches and supervises a job: the process group, the escalation from TERM to KILL, the reap and the terminal recovery. A job is the launched program and the other processes in its process group. Argv holds a program and its arguments as separate words. Launch adds the working directory, the environment changes, an optional completion channel, the entry signal state and the escalation times. The runner starts the program directly. A shell runs only when the command selects one.

The runner does not choose a program, and it gives no meaning to provider or model labels. It does not read a result either. Its completion channel tells it only that a file appeared. Its Rust result is Ended, or a LaunchError. It is never the opaque result that the caller receives.

The Ravel CLI uses two launch interfaces of the runner, one for each kind of job in the example:

Runner interfaceInformation or actionIn the example
run_observed_with_start_checkSupervises the managed process. It reports spawn and reap. After spawn, the Ravel CLI restores its own signal mask.Runs fake-agent
run_captured, with CapturedLaunchSupervises a harness run in a new POSIX session. It captures standard output and standard error with a limit, and it installs no signal handler.Runs fake-advisor
StopRequestA flag that the Ravel CLI sets to stop a captured job at a deadline or on cancellationNot set: the run ended by itself
ChannelA fresh completion path. The appearance of its file starts the escalation.One for each fake
EntrySignalsThe signal state that the managed process starts withRecorded when the Ravel CLI starts
EndedThe child status, the cause of its end, whether the file appeared, the group state and the operation reportsTwo values, one for each fake

The runner also has run, run_observed and run_noninteractive. The production code of Ravel does not call them. A test of crates/ravel/src/cancellation.rs calls run_noninteractive.

Why is the runner a separate package? You can test its job mechanics without the Rhai worker or a configuration script. Its only production dependency is libc, which supplies the operating-system calls. A correction to terminal recovery can stay inside the runner while the configuration interface stays the same. Chapter 9 explains the mechanics and their limits.

The Ravel CLI is the runner’s consumer. It supplies the environment rules, the completion channel and the escalation times: a 2-second grace, then up to 5 seconds after TERM. It checks Ended.group before it accepts a result, and it reports the operation reports that Ended carries. The runner can change process-wide signal state, and its return does not restore that state.

Ravel supports no separate component. Its runner serves the Ravel CLI, which supervises one session and then exits. Another program runs the Ravel CLI as a child process and leaves supervision to it. The private packages do not give a contract for a persistent runner.

The result channel carries the value

The result channel adds contents to the runner’s file-appearance mechanism. In the example, Ravel made two channels: one for fake-advisor and one for fake-agent. Each is a SessionChannel. It owns a private temporary directory, the completion path and an open directory handle for the read.

end_session finds the destination in RAVEL_EXIT_FILE. publish_result writes a complete temporary file, then adds the completion name with a hard link. A hard link cannot replace an earlier result. Thus, Ravel keeps the first result.

The runner sees the file appear. The result-channel code reads the contents and rejects invalid files. An empty string is a sent result. An absent file is no result.

A harness run’s result goes to the script, as run.result. In the example, that is advice. The managed process’s result goes to the caller, as the output of start-session. In the example, that is finished.

The table shows why no part alone can decide that a session succeeded:

PartWhat it knowsWhat it cannot decide alone
end-sessionThe destination it inherited and whether the send succeededWhether supervision and cleanup succeeded
RunnerThe file appeared, and the child, group and terminal observationsWhether the file holds a valid Ravel result
SessionChannelThe file contents and the Ended that it receivesWhether that Ended belongs to its own launch
Ravel CLIWhich channel belongs to which launch, and the other cleanup and signal reportsWhat the result means to the caller

The Ravel CLI pairs each channel with its launch. The Rust type of SessionChannel::result_after accepts an Ended from any launch. run.rs gives it the outcome of the launch that received that channel. Then it closes the channel and checks the other cleanup results. A readable file alone does not show success.

Thus, a change to result storage affects the result channel, the runner’s observation and the coordination of the Ravel CLI. It needs no change to a configuration script. The file format stays private, so neither scripts nor callers depend on it. Chapter 8 follows this order and the failure paths.

The program contract stays loose

The configuration script selects a program, Ravel runs it, and a program in the session sends one value with end-session. Ravel expects a harness in normal use. But it needs no model-provider interface from the selected program. The script decides whether the program receives the prompt.

The code shows this separation. config::judge_result in crates/ravel/src/config.rs checks the shape of the selection. Then run.rs makes an Argv from the selected program and argument words. Ravel adds no prompt argument.

In the example, fake-agent received short and no prompt. end_session accepts text and uses the path that it inherited. The final write returns that text unchanged after successful cleanup.

The table shows which decisions can change while the interfaces between the parts stay the same:

ChangePart that owns the decisionBoundary that stays the same
Select a different harness or an ordinary programConfiguration scriptThe runner still receives a program and argument words
Pass the prompt in a different wayConfiguration scriptRavel still gives the prompt to the script in request.prompt
Give a result a new meaningCallerRavel still carries one opaque value

The program still needs to be executable. A successful session still needs a sent result and successful cleanup. A program that exits with status 0 and no result does not complete that contract.

The installed executable supplies both roles

An installation has one executable, bin/ravel. The Ravel CLI finds its own executable to start the Rhai worker. Thus, a symlink to bin/ravel uses the same binary for both roles. Ravel does not look for the worker on PATH.

The hello of the worker holds its build identity. The Ravel CLI compares it with its own before it sends the request. Thus, a worker of another build refuses. The check finds a mismatch. It does not authenticate the installation against its owner. A change to the Rhai worker needs one new build of bin/ravel.

The installation also has share/ravel/examples/, with three example scripts, and share/ravel/notices/THIRD-PARTY-NOTICES.md, the notices of the Rust dependencies. A Homebrew installation adds the installation record share/ravel/installation.json, which names the default skill directory. The installed layout in chapter 6 lists each file, and chapter 11 explains delivery.

To summarise the chapter: the Ravel CLI starts every program, decides every outcome and writes the only result. The Rhai worker only computes, and it asks the Ravel CLI for each effect through the host bridge. The runner supervises each job inside the Ravel CLI. Each job receives its own result channel.

These source boundaries do not give compatibility with every owner helper or a contract for another program that embeds the parts. The test record in chapter 1 separates measured platforms from the others. Chapter 9 gives the process and terminal guarantees within those limits.

Follow a session through the code

Do you want to change what Ravel does, or find the code that made a session fail? Then you need to know which function does each step of a session, and in which order. This chapter follows one session through the code, from main to the last byte on standard output. Then it follows the same session when the configuration script refuses and when a signal cancels it. At the end, you can find where a change belongs.

The session is the ravel-parts example from chapter 7. Its configuration script asks a fake harness, fake-advisor, for advice. The advisor prints short and sends the result advice. Then the script selects the fake agent fake-agent with the argument short. The agent sends finished, and the caller receives finished with status 0.

These are the words of chapter 7, with the value that each word has in this chapter’s example:

WordWhat it namesIn the example
Ravel CLIThe ravel command-line program. It checks the request, runs the programs and decides the outcome.The process ravel start-session --kind demo …
Configuration scriptThe owner’s Rhai file that selects a program or refusesconfig.rhai
Rhai workerA second copy of the ravel executable that evaluates the configuration scriptIt runs select in config.rhai
Host bridgeThe code on both sides of the worker’s private socket. It carries each request of the script to the Ravel CLI and returns the answer.It carries the call ravel::run_harness
Reader threadThe thread of the Ravel CLI that does every file read and every harness run during evaluationIt reads config.rhai and runs fake-advisor
Harness runOne run of a program from the harnesses settings, during evaluation. Its output returns to the script.fake-advisor
Managed processThe program that the script selects. It does the caller’s work.fake-agent
Result channelA private temporary directory with one path for a result file. The path goes to a program in RAVEL_EXIT_FILE.One for fake-advisor, one for fake-agent
RunnerThe Rust library code in the Ravel CLI that launches and supervises a jobIt runs both fakes

This chapter is an orientation to one path. It does not cover the complete source. It omits the load_context exchange of the selection context, the other host calls, platform system calls, test instruments and delivery code. Chapter 7 explains the parts and why each one is separate. Chapter 9 explains the job-control details.

This chapter writes INT, TERM, HUP and KILL without the SIG prefix. Other signal names keep that prefix. Code and diagnostic text keep their literal names. Paths are relative to the root of a checkout.

Trace the example

You saved four files in ~/ravel-parts in chapter 7. The configuration script is the file that decides the path. It is the same file as in chapter 7.

Fragment: config.rhai of the ravel-parts example. This block omits settings.json, the two fakes and the command. Chapter 7 gives them.

export const SCHEMA_VERSION = 4;
export const VERSION = "parts";

fn select(request, context) {
    let run = ravel::run_harness(#{ harness: "advisor", args: [] });
    print(`The advisor run ended: ${run.status}, result ${run.result}.`);
    #{
        status: "selected",
        program: "fake-agent",
        args: [run.stdout],
        provider: "local",
        model: "fake",
        effort: "none",
        reason: "Show each part of Ravel.",
    }
}

The command of chapter 7 gave the kind demo, the script config.rhai and the prompt Show the parts of Ravel.. It ran with a cleared environment and a private home. Standard error showed two lines in this order. The check of chapter 7 claims them as one block, so it also checks their order.

config stdout: The advisor run ended: exited, result advice.
ravel: starting provider local, model fake, effort none for kind "demo"

The first line is the script’s print output. The Ravel CLI wrote it after the worker ended. The second line is the routing notice. The Ravel CLI wrote it just before it launched fake-agent. The file result then held finished, without a newline.

The table gives the whole path before the sections explain each step. Each step needs the step before it. The last column says what the next step receives.

StepWhat happensCode locationWhat the next step receives
1Parse the commandcrates/ravel/src/main.rs, mainRunArgs for run::run
2Refuse inside a harness run, check the entry signal state, install the cancellation handlerscrates/ravel/src/run.rs, runHandlers that keep a signal for later
3Read the settings and inputs, find the script, prepare the skillscrates/ravel/src/choice.rs, preparePrepared
4Spawn the Rhai worker and check its identitychoice::choose_prepared, rhai_worker::front::evaluate, worker::superviseA worker that is the same build
5Send the script to the worker and answer its host callscrates/ravel/src/rhai_worker/serve.rs, serve, with host.rs and harness.rsThe run of fake-advisor, as a map for the script
6Check the reply, reap the worker, find the programworker::supervise, config::judge_result, program::resolveA Choice with the checked command and the executable path
7Reserve standard output, make the result channel, write the routing noticerunA Launch
8Launch and supervise the managed processcrates/keyed-launch/src/run.rsEnded, after reap and terminal recovery
9Read the resultcrates/ravel/src/completion.rs, result_afterSome("finished")
10Complete cleanup and decide the outcomerun, Handlers::finalise, settlefinished, or a failure
11Write the resultrun, then mainUnchanged output, then status 0

The sequence shows the same order. It shows why the sent result reaches the caller only after worker cleanup, managed-job cleanup and the final outcome decision.

Open the full session sequence at full size. The optional region applies when watch sees the result before the child exits. The later appearance check also finds the result when the child exits first. Terminal actions are conditional. The checked example has no terminal.

The session starts other short ravel processes. One is the Rhai worker. Each fake also starts ravel end-session to send its result. None of these processes returns a result to the caller. Only the original Ravel CLI does that.

Prepare the request

main in crates/ravel/src/main.rs first decides which role this process has. The Ravel CLI starts the worker with argv[0] set to ravel-rhai-config-worker. Thus, main compares argv[0] with that name before it reads any argument.

Excerpt from crates/ravel/src/main.rs: the role check comes first in main, after a test-only branch.

#![allow(unused)]
fn main() {
    if std::env::args_os()
        .next()
        .is_some_and(|arg0| arg0 == rhai_worker::ARG0)
    {
        return rhai_worker::role::run();
}

In the example, the name is ravel, so main reads the command line. It uses the clap definitions in crates/ravel/src/cli.rs. For end-session, main calls end_session directly, without a configuration script. For start-session, it first refuses the withdrawn confinement flags with cli::withdrawn. Then it calls run::run.

run in crates/ravel/src/run.rs refuses first when RAVEL_CONFIG_RUN is in its environment. A harness run has that variable, and a start-session inside it cannot select. The example’s caller has no such variable.

Next, signal_state::recorded checks that Ravel saved its entry signal state. An initialiser function saves that state before main runs. Then handlers.begin() installs the cancellation handlers. From here, a signal does not stop the process at once. A handler keeps it, and the code checks it at fixed points.

choice::prepare then reads the settings, reads the inputs and resolves the configuration script path. The order matters: the settings supply defaults that the input checks need, and both come before the worker exists.

Excerpt from crates/ravel/src/choice.rs: these first statements of prepare establish the inputs and the configuration script entry.

#![allow(unused)]
fn main() {
    let home = std::env::var_os("HOME");
    let settings = Settings::read(home.as_deref())?;
    let inputs = Inputs::read(args, &settings)?;
    let entry = authority::resolve(args.config.as_deref(), &inputs.cwd, home.as_deref())?;
}

In the example, Settings::read reads home/.config/ravel/settings.json. It finds the harness entry advisor and the budget of one harness run.

authority::resolve resolves config.rhai relative to the caller’s working directory. It requires a regular file and gives the canonical path. It does not open the file. The reader thread reads it later, inside the selection deadline. authority::resolve does not search for another configuration script when this path fails.

Then prepare resolves the skill sources and prepares the prompt. This step comes before the worker. Thus, a skill refusal starts no worker and makes no result channel.

Excerpt from crates/ravel/src/choice.rs: the call that follows the entry in prepare. config_dir is the directory of the canonical entry.

#![allow(unused)]
fn main() {
    let skills = crate::skills::launch::prepare(
        home.as_deref().map(Path::new),
        &settings,
        config_dir,
        &inputs.prompt.text,
    )?;
}

The example’s skills settings have an empty source array, so delivery is disabled, also in a Homebrew installation. Thus, this call returns None. Prepared holds the checked inputs, the entry and that None. It holds no decision of the configuration script yet.

Back in run, a RunSetup collects three values for each program that Ravel runs later. They are the entry signal state, the --exit-dir directory and the skill descriptor path. A harness run and the managed process both use them. In this trace, the descriptor path is absent.

Start the Rhai worker

choice::choose_prepared calls evaluate_and_resolve_rhai. That function builds the request for the script. In this checkout-build trace, the request holds these fields:

  • kind is demo.
  • originalPrompt and prompt both hold Show the parts of Ravel..
  • cwd is the caller’s working directory.
  • params is empty, and skills is null.

It also makes two checks for later, as closures. The judge closure checks a context that load_context returns. The example has no load_context, so judge never runs. The reply_judge closure calls config::judge_result on the final reply of the worker. Then evaluate_and_resolve_rhai calls rhai_worker::front::evaluate.

evaluate gives the worker to worker::supervise in crates/ravel/src/worker.rs. That function owns the worker process from its start to its reap. It prepares the worker in this way:

  • It makes a private empty directory with mode 0700. The worker starts there.
  • It makes a socket pair. In the child, between fork and exec, the worker’s end moves to file descriptor 3.
  • It clears the environment, so the worker inherits no variable.
  • It sets standard input to /dev/null and pipes standard output and standard error back to the Ravel CLI.
  • In the child, between fork and exec, it marks every descriptor above 3 close-on-exec. Thus, the worker holds only descriptors 0 to 3.

A failure of any of these steps, or of the spawn, refuses as worker_missing or worker_failed, status 5.

The worker therefore has no RAVEL_EXIT_FILE. It cannot send a result for any session. The selection deadline starts at the spawn, so a slow start uses the same time as a slow script. Two threads then read the worker’s standard output and standard error within one size limit.

The worker side is rhai_worker::role::run in crates/ravel/src/rhai_worker/role.rs. It sets its heap limit of 1 GiB before the first byte of script arrives. Then it sends hello with its protocol, package version, build identity and Rhai version.

On the CLI side, converse in rhai_worker/front.rs receives that frame. verify_hello compares each value with the values of the Ravel CLI itself.

Excerpt from crates/ravel/src/rhai_worker/front.rs: verify_hello refuses a worker when one of these four values differs.

#![allow(unused)]
fn main() {
    if protocol != Some(PROTOCOL)
        || text("packageVersion") != PACKAGE_VERSION
        || text("buildId") != BUILD_ID
        || text("rhaiVersion") != RHAI_VERSION
}

A difference gives worker_identity_mismatch, status 5. In the example, the worker is the same file as the Ravel CLI, so each value is equal.

Next, converse starts the reader thread with Reader::start. The thread blocks INT, TERM and HUP for its whole life, so a signal never runs a handler on it. Then converse builds a Tokio runtime on the current thread and runs serve on it. Tokio is the Rust library for asynchronous input and output. The runtime thread itself makes no file call.

Answer the script’s requests

serve in crates/ravel/src/rhai_worker/serve.rs first gives the entry to the reader thread. The worker reads no file, also for its own script. The reader thread reads config.rhai, measures it and returns its source.

Excerpt from crates/ravel/src/rhai_worker/serve.rs: the deadline covers the read of the entry, because the read waits inside watch.until.

#![allow(unused)]
fn main() {
    // The deadline began when the worker started, so it covers this read.
    let source = match watch.until(host.read_entry(entry)).await? {
        Ok(source) => source,
        Err(what) => return Err(Conversation::Refused(unreadable(&what))),
}

watch.until is the pattern of the conversation after the handshake. It waits for one piece of work, and it also watches three conditions: the deadline, the cancellation latch and the output limit. A latch is the saved signal number that a handler stores. The first condition that occurs stops the wait. While a host call is in progress, during_call also watches the socket. A worker that sends a frame or closes its end then breaks the protocol.

Then serve sends the evaluate frame. It holds the entry path, the source exactly as the reader thread read it, the request and the limits.

Excerpt from crates/ravel/src/rhai_worker/serve.rs: the evaluate frame. A selectContext member follows only when the caller supplied a context.

#![allow(unused)]
fn main() {
    let mut evaluate = json!({
        "type": "evaluate",
        "protocol": super::PROTOCOL,
        // Exactly the admitted file.
        "entry": entry,
        "source": source,
        "request": request,
        "bounds": bounds.to_json(),
    });
}

The worker evaluates the source on its own thread with engine::evaluate_with in rhai_worker/engine.rs. It compiles the script and runs its top level. Then it checks the exports SCHEMA_VERSION, VERSION and select. Last, it calls select(request, context).

What does select receive as context in the example? The caller gave no context document, and the script has no load_context. Then select receives request.context, which is null in the request. Rhai shows null as the unit value ().

Excerpt from crates/ravel/src/rhai_worker/engine.rs: the second argument of select, in evaluate_with.

#![allow(unused)]
fn main() {
    let context = match select_context {
        Some(context) if context.is_object() => from_json(context),
        Some(_) => return Err("the selectContext is not an object".to_owned()),
        None => request.get("context").cloned().unwrap_or(Dynamic::UNIT),
    };
}

The refusal example below prints type_of(context), and the output is ().

Follow the harness run through the host bridge

Chapter 7’s host bridge sequence shows how the runtime and reader threads divide this call.

The first statement of select calls ravel::run_harness. The table follows that call. Each row names the thread or process that acts.

OrderWho actsCode locationWhat happens in the example
1The workerrhai_worker/bridge.rsSends a host frame with the call run_harness and the harness advisor, then waits for the answer
2The runtime threadserveReceives the frame and passes it to Host::call in rhai_worker/host.rs
3The runtime threadHost::run_harnessFinds the entry advisor in the settings. The reader thread finds fake-advisor on the caller’s PATH. It checks that the budget allows one more run.
4The reader threadharness::execute in rhai_worker/harness.rsMakes a result channel for the run. Adds RAVEL_CONFIG_RUN=1 to the run’s environment. Calls the runner.
5The reader thread, in the runnerkeyed_launch::run_capturedRuns fake-advisor in a new POSIX session. Captures short from its standard output. Stops its process group after it exits.
6The reader threadharness::executeReads advice from the run’s channel, then removes the channel
7The runtime threadHost::run_harness, then serveSends a reply frame with the run’s map
8The workerrhai_worker/bridge.rsGives the map to the script. run.stdout is short, and run.result is advice.

Excerpt from crates/ravel/src/rhai_worker/harness.rs: the marker that each harness run receives. The skill descriptor path joins it when delivery is on.

#![allow(unused)]
fn main() {
    let mut granted: Vec<(OsString, OsString)> =
        vec![(OsString::from(CONFIG_RUN), OsString::from("1"))];
}

That marker is why chapter 7’s advisor printed RAVEL_CONFIG_RUN is 1. The managed process does not receive it, so it can start a nested session.

While the reader thread runs the harness, the runtime thread still watches the deadline and the cancellation latch. Host::run_harness also limits the run to the time that remains, or to the script’s timeoutMs when that is shorter. At that limit, it sets a StopRequest, and the runner stops the run.

After the reply, select returns its map, and the worker sends a selection frame with VERSION and that map. serve returns. converse then shuts down the runtime with retire and calls reply_judge.

Excerpt from crates/ravel/src/rhai_worker/front.rs: the end of converse. No runtime task remains when reply_judge checks the reply.

#![allow(unused)]
fn main() {
    // Every task is finished here, and the copy of the channel is closed.
    retire(runtime);
    let served = served?;
    reply_judge(&served.reply).map_err(Conversation::Refused)?;
    Ok((identity, served, started.elapsed()))
}

Why does reply_judge check the reply here, before the worker ends? A refusal is part of the conversation. If the later worker teardown fails, the script’s refusal stays the primary failure, and the teardown reports join it.

Check the selection and find the program

converse returns to worker::supervise. The worker sent its last frame, so it exits by itself. supervise waits up to 1 second for the exit, then kills the worker if it still runs. It collects the script’s output and removes the private directory.

Excerpt from crates/ravel/src/worker.rs: supervise accepts a teardown only when the reap, the removal and the stop each succeeded.

#![allow(unused)]
fn main() {
    let admitted = teardown.status.is_ok() && teardown.removed.is_ok() && !teardown.stop_failed;
}

supervise returns success only after confirmed worker reap and successful removal of the directory. A valid command alone does not permit a launch. The runner cannot enforce this order, because it has no worker to inspect. The Ravel CLI enforces it before the runner call.

Back in evaluate_and_resolve_rhai, the reply is a Selection. The function checks the result again with config::judge_result. This check gives the Command with the program, the arguments and the three labels.

Excerpt from crates/ravel/src/choice.rs: the selected command, then a cancellation check before the program lookup.

#![allow(unused)]
fn main() {
    let command = config::judge_result(result, &inputs.kind, &source).map_err(refuse)?;
    cancellation::check(&source).map_err(refuse)?;
}

Then program::resolve searches the caller’s PATH for fake-agent. The example’s bin directory supplies that executable. The Choice keeps both the selected command and the executable path. The labels local, fake and none supply text for the routing notice. Ravel does not use them to talk to a model.

choose_prepared checks the latch one more time. Then it returns the Choice to run, and run writes the worker’s operation reports, if any, on standard error.

Prepare the managed launch

run first calls ResultOutput::reserve. This function checks that standard output is writable, saves its descriptor, then points standard output at standard error. The saved descriptor does not pass through exec to the managed process. Thus, a program that prints cannot write the result of the session.

Next, SessionChannel::allocate makes a fresh temporary directory and channel path under TMPDIR. It keeps an open directory descriptor for the later read. Allocation makes no result file. The channel belongs to this launch alone.

Only now does run write the script’s output and the routing notice.

Excerpt from crates/ravel/src/run.rs: the routing notice. A --json session keeps a notice document instead and writes it after supervision.

#![allow(unused)]
fn main() {
            eprintln!(
                "ravel: starting provider {}, model {}, effort {} for kind {:?}",
                choice.command.provider,
                choice.command.model,
                choice.command.effort,
                choice.inputs.kind
            );
}

Then handlers.try_block_and_check() looks for a signal one last time before the launch. A signal here prevents the runner call.

run builds the argument vector from the checked command. The excerpt shows the difference between the executable path and the original program name, which becomes argument zero.

Excerpt from crates/ravel/src/run.rs: the argument vector uses the resolved path and each argument that the configuration script supplied.

#![allow(unused)]
fn main() {
        let argv = Argv::new(
            OsString::from(&choice.executable.path),
            choice.command.args.iter().map(OsString::from).collect(),
        )
        .with_arg0(OsString::from(&choice.executable.program));
}

In the example, the vector is fake-agent and short. Ravel does no shell expansion or word splitting. The fake’s shebang selects its shell interpreter, as chapter 3 explains.

Excerpt from crates/ravel/src/run.rs: the environment of the managed process. The descriptor path replaces an inherited RAVEL_SKILL_DIRECTORIES, and scrubbed removes that name when delivery is off.

#![allow(unused)]
fn main() {
        let directories = setup.skill_directories.as_ref();
        let environment = skill_environment(directories);
}

The Launch holds the argument vector, the channel, these environment changes, the entry signal state and the escalation times. run calls keyed_launch::run_observed_with_start_check. The runner removes the inherited variables that name a result destination. It applies the environment grants, then sets the new RAVEL_EXIT_FILE last. Thus, a nested session gives its own child a fresh destination. The child otherwise inherits the caller’s environment and working directory.

In crates/keyed-launch/src/run.rs, run_with_output makes the process group and spawns the command. If Ravel owns the foreground of a terminal, the child takes the terminal during spawn, before exec. After spawn, run_with_output calls supervise_started. The Started event records that the spawn succeeded. It does not prove that exec succeeded. The start check of the Ravel CLI restores its entry signal mask before supervision continues.

Send the result

fake-agent calls ravel end-session -- "finished". In this second Ravel process, main calls end_session in crates/ravel/src/completion.rs. That function reads RAVEL_EXIT_FILE and passes the path and the result to publish_result. The function name keeps the source’s own word. Its action is to send one value to the enclosing session.

publish_result checks the byte limit and the parent directory. Then it writes the value into a temporary file in that directory. It adds the completion name only after the write and flush succeed.

Excerpt from crates/ravel/src/completion.rs: the temporary file receives the complete value before the completion name exists.

#![allow(unused)]
fn main() {
    let mut temporary = NamedTempFile::new_in(parent)?;
    temporary.write_all(result.as_bytes())?;
    temporary.flush()?;
}

The next operation, fs::hard_link, adds a name for that file. It does not replace a name that already exists. This behaviour is the first-result rule. Thus, the runner can observe only a complete file, never a partly written value.

Channel stateEffect of end-session
No completion nameAdds the name for the complete temporary file
A valid result already existsChecks the earlier result and leaves it unchanged
Missing or empty inherited path, stale directory, or invalid earlier resultReports failure

The same code ran earlier in the session, when fake-advisor sent advice to its own channel. Only the destination differed.

An empty string is still a sent result. Newlines and Unicode stay in the value. A second call cannot replace the first value, but it races with the shutdown and removal of the channel. It can therefore fail or never run.

The file operation asks for completion. It does not signal the managed group or do the cleanup of the enclosing session. The original Ravel CLI owns those actions.

Finish the managed job

supervise_started calls supervise_reported, which calls watch. These functions are in crates/keyed-launch/src/run.rs.

The runner calls its observer synchronously at successful spawn and at confirmed reap. The observer must return promptly and without a panic. The start-check callback has the same requirement, but it can return an error.

watch checks cancellation, the child’s state and channel appearance. If the child continues after the result appears, watch applies the grace, TERM and KILL stages from chapter 3.

fake-agent can exit before watch observes the file. That path needs no escalation. supervise_reported checks channel appearance again when it makes Ended, after the job cleanup steps. Thus, Ravel does not lose the result when the child exits quickly.

The final group stop comes before the reap on the normal path through watch.

Excerpt from crates/keyed-launch/src/run.rs: after it observes the child’s exit, watch stops the group before it collects the child’s exit status.

#![allow(unused)]
fn main() {
    child.bound(Instant::now() + ERROR_SETTLEMENT);
    child.kill_group();
    let reaped = child.reap();
}

The direct child’s PID keeps the group’s identity until the reap. The runner uses that interval to stop descendants that stay in the group. After watch returns, supervise_reported recovers a lent terminal and confirms whether the group is gone. It returns an Ended with separate fields for the child status, cancellation, channel appearance and group state.

The Ravel CLI needs those separate facts. A later nonzero child status does not invalidate a sent result. A group that stays, or a failed terminal recovery, prevents success. Descendants that leave the group stay outside this cleanup guarantee.

On macOS, standard error can show ravel: stop attempt lines here. The group stop can find only zombie members, and the system then refuses the signal. Chapter 3 explains why these operation reports alone do not decide success.

Read the result

Back in run, allocated.result_after(ending) receives the Ended from this channel’s runner call. The association belongs to run: the Rust type alone does not prove that an arbitrary Ended belongs to this channel.

result_after first checks the group and cancellation, then channel appearance. It reads the contents only after those checks.

ObservationResult of result_after
The group is still presentError
The runner reports cancellationError
The runner observed no completion fileNone
A valid empty completion fileSome(String::new())
A valid completion file that is not emptySome(result)

read_result opens the file relative to the held directory descriptor with openat. A replacement of the directory pathname cannot redirect this read. The open refuses a final symlink and cannot block on a FIFO. read_regular requires a regular file, enforces the byte limit and checks UTF-8. These checks matter because the managed process can change files.

In the example, the value is Some("finished"). run changes None to session_result_missing, even if the child’s exit status is zero. result_after returns candidate text to run, not to the caller. Resource cleanup and the final cancellation decision still follow.

Complete cleanup and write the result

After the runner returns, run writes the runner’s operation reports. Without --json, each report has the form ravel: <report> on standard error. With --json, operationReports belongs to the selection notice, which run writes now.

run then attempts these operations in order:

OrderOperationPurpose
1handlers.restore_entry_mask()Restores the entry mask while the handlers still protect the transition
2channel.close()Removes the result channel’s temporary files
3output.restore()Restores the caller’s standard output
4handlers.finalise(runner_signal)Joins the cancellation observations and completes the signal transitions
5settle(...)Reports failures and decides whether the result can go to the caller

Channel cleanup checks the directory’s recorded identity before removal. It does not remove a replacement directory. This pathname check is not an atomic defence against concurrent changes by code of the same user. An unknown directory identity also prevents removal. An explicit cleanup attempt disables the destructor’s later retry, even on failure. A moved directory can need manual cleanup.

settle decides the end of supervision from the collected result, the cleanup outcomes, the group state and cancellation. It reports failures, gives cancellation priority and rejects unsuccessful cleanup. It does not do those cleanup operations itself. On this successful path it returns finished to run.

Excerpt from crates/ravel/src/run.rs: only a successful settle reaches this final write and flush.

#![allow(unused)]
fn main() {
    io::stdout()
        .write_all(result.as_bytes())
        .and_then(|()| io::stdout().flush())
        .map_err(|error| failed("result_write_failed", error))?;
    Ok(())
}

main changes this success to status 0. Ravel writes the result unchanged and adds no newline. A failed final write can leave only the start of the value on standard output. The invocation then fails, so output bytes alone do not prove success. A signal that arrives after the final cancellation decision can still stop the Ravel CLI during output. The write is not atomic with that decision.

To summarise the successful path: the Ravel CLI starts the worker, answers its one harness call, checks its reply and reaps it. Only then does it make the result channel, write the routing notice and launch fake-agent. After the reap of fake-agent and the cleanup, settle permits the write of finished.

Follow a refusal or launch failure

What changes when the configuration script refuses? Run the same example with one changed file, and see where the path stops. The example directory is ~/ravel-parts-refusal. Make it and copy the three files that stay the same:

Fragment: make the example directory and copy three files of chapter 7. This block omits the new config.rhai. The book checker makes its own directory and does not run this block.

mkdir -p ~/ravel-parts-refusal/bin
cd ~/ravel-parts-refusal
cp ~/ravel-parts/settings.json .
cp ~/ravel-parts/bin/fake-advisor ~/ravel-parts/bin/fake-agent bin/

Then save the new config.rhai. It prints what select received as its context. It runs the advisor as before. Then it refuses with the advisor’s answer in its message. Type the commands of the complete example without their first $ .

Complete example: the configuration script asks the fake harness for advice, then refuses. The managed process does not start.

settings.json

{"harnesses":{"advisor":{"program":"fake-advisor"}},"budgets":{"harnessRuns":1},"skills":{"schemaVersion":1,"sources":[]}}

config.rhai

export const SCHEMA_VERSION = 4;
export const VERSION = "parts-refusal";

fn select(request, context) {
    print(`The context is ${type_of(context)}.`);
    let run = ravel::run_harness(#{ harness: "advisor", args: [] });
    print(`The advisor run ended: ${run.status}, result ${run.result}.`);
    #{
        status: "refused",
        code: "advice_too_short",
        message: `The advisor said only "${run.stdout}".`,
        remedy: "Ask the advisor for a longer answer.",
    }
}

$ env -i HOME="$PWD/home" TMPDIR="$PWD/tmp" PATH="$PWD/bin:$PATH" ravel start-session --kind demo --config config.rhai --prompt 'Show the parts of Ravel.' > result

Standard output
