Command

$ cat advisor.txt && test ! -e agent.txt && echo "fake-agent did not run."
$ test ! -s result && echo "The file result is empty."
$ rmdir tmp && echo "The directory tmp is empty again."

Standard output

advisor: parent is ravel start-session
advisor: RAVEL_CONFIG_RUN is 1
fake-agent did not run.
The file result is empty.
The directory tmp is empty again.

Exit status

0

Here <scratch>/work stands for the example’s directory. In your shell, the line shows the absolute path of your ravel-parts-refusal directory. The first line answers the question of the last section: without a context, select received ().

Follow the refusal through the code. The steps up to the reply are the steps of the successful path. The harness run happened, and its channel was removed. Then the path changes:

  1. The worker sends the refusal map in its selection frame.
  2. reply_judge calls config::judge_result. That function finds status: "refused", checks the three fields and returns config_refused with the script’s own code advice_too_short.
  3. converse returns that refusal as Conversation::Refused. worker::supervise still waits for the worker, reaps it and removes its directory. It returns the refusal together with the script’s output.
  4. choose_prepared returns the failure to run. run never reserves standard output, makes no result channel and writes no routing notice.
  5. The common cleanup and settle run as before. With no cancellation and no cleanup failure, settle keeps the refusal, and main exits with status 3.

Ravel does not try another configuration script or command when selection fails. The Rust type Refusal also carries worker failures and timeouts. Its name alone does not tell you that the script refused. The code does.

The table locates the other forks. Its statuses describe each failure alone, without cancellation. The exit-status table gives their meaning to the caller.

ProblemCode that decidesStatus and point of failure
The configuration script file is missing, is not a regular file, or is the retired policy.tsauthority::resolve3, before the worker starts
The entry cannot be read, is not UTF-8 or passes the entry limitHost::read_entry on the reader thread, then serve3, after the worker starts
A skill source, the skill cache or a skill limit refusesskills::launch::prepare3, or 124 at the skill deadline, or 5 when the random source or a cache cleanup fails. All before the worker starts.
The script does not parse, an import fails, or an export is wrongThe worker’s engine reports it. choice::script_refusal gives the code.3, before the program lookup
load_context refuses, throws, or returns an invalid or oversized contextThe judge closure, with config::loader_refused and context::deliver. Or the worker and serve report it, and choice::script_refusal gives the code.3, before the program lookup
select refuses, abstains or returns a malformed resultconfig::judge_result3, before the program lookup
select throws, or the script passes an engine limit, the heap limit or the harness budgetThe worker reports it. choice::script_refusal gives the code.3, before the program lookup
The script’s output passes its limitworker::supervise, with output_limit3, before the program lookup
The selection deadline passesBefore the hello, receive_for in converse times out. After it, watch.until in serve stops the wait. worker::supervise then uses expired in worker.rs.124, before the program lookup
The worker cannot start, has another identity or breaks the protocolworker::supervise, as worker_missing or worker_failed, then verify_hello and worker::broken_for5, before the managed launch
A harness run cannot be cleaned upharness::execute reports the problem. Host::run_harness or Host::settle makes harness_cleanup_failed.5, before the managed launch
The conversation succeeds, but worker teardown failsworker::supervise5, before the managed launch
The selected program is absentprogram::resolve127, before the runner call
The selected file cannot execute, and no other PATH entry canprogram::resolve126, before the runner call

The deadline covers the worker start, the read of the entry and the whole conversation, harness runs included. A timeout can therefore occur before the worker receives the script. It is not a decision of the script. A valid answer of the script also cannot override an unconfirmed worker reap or a failed directory removal.

When failures combine without cancellation, worker::supervise uses this priority. The table separates the primary failure from the reports that stay in the diagnostic.

First condition that appliesPrimary failureOther reports
The script’s output passes its limitoutput_limit, status 3The conversation failure and the teardown problems stay
The conversation failsThat failure keeps its code, such as 3 for config_refused or 124 for selection_timeoutThe teardown problems stay
The conversation succeeds, but teardown failsworker_failed, status 5The teardown problems stay

The program check does not guarantee that a later spawn succeeds. For example, a script can pass the file checks but name a missing interpreter. run::supervision_failure classifies such a runner error from started and the OS error number. The Started event sets started.

Excerpt from crates/ravel/src/run.rs: supervision_failure chooses a status before it makes the diagnostic.

#![allow(unused)]
fn main() {
    let exit = if started {
        EXIT_WORKER
    } else if errno == Some(libc::ENOENT) {
        127
    } else {
        126
    };
}

EXIT_WORKER is 5. After Started, a supervision error keeps that status. Before Started, ENOENT gives 127 and another launch error gives 126. The conversion also keeps a signal that the runner consumed, for the final cancellation decision.

Each of these failures leaves the main closure in run, then reaches its common cleanup and final decision. An allocator that fails partway through its directory setup attempts its own cleanup before it returns the error. run handles the resources that the allocator returned. Thus, even a failure without an Ended value has cleanup work to complete.

Follow cancellation

A cancellation is an INT, TERM or HUP signal that reaches the Ravel CLI. It can arrive during selection, during supervision or during the final cleanup. The handlers that handlers.begin() installed keep the signal in the latch. Ordinary code then attempts cleanup before the Ravel CLI propagates the signal.

Cancel during selection

During selection, nothing waits on a signal directly. Watch::stopped in serve looks at the latch every 50 milliseconds. When it finds a signal, it stops the current wait with Stop::Signal.

If a harness run is in progress, Host::settle stops it first. It sets the run’s StopRequest and waits up to 8 seconds for the runner to stop the run’s group and reap it. Then serve returns, and converse retires the runtime. worker::supervise sends TERM to the worker, reaps it, collects its output and removes its directory.

The conversation ended without a reply, so supervise first makes a worker_failed refusal. Then it sees the latch, and cancelled_by replaces the primary failure with selection_cancelled. The worker_failed text stays under “original failure:”. run launches nothing. In its final decision, settle writes that report, then returns session_cancelled.

Open the selection cancellation sequence at full size. The figure is the direct-signal example of chapter 5, cancel-selection. In that example, a fake harness sends INT to its parent, the Ravel CLI. The configuration script itself cannot send a signal. The figure does not show keyboard delivery from a terminal.

The next check sits just before the managed launch. run calls handlers.try_block_and_check() after it reserves output and makes the channel. A signal found here prevents the runner call. The common cleanup still closes the channel and restores output.

Cancel during supervision

During supervision, the runner’s own handlers catch INT, TERM and HUP. They write the runner’s latch. watch checks that latch before it accepts even a child that has already exited. It forwards the signal to the managed group, then escalates if needed. It keeps the first signal that it consumes through the wait, the reap and the recovery that follow.

What happens to a result that the program already sent? Run the ravel-parts example with a changed fake-agent to see. The example directory is ~/ravel-parts-cancel. Make it and copy the three files that stay the same:

Fragment: make the example directory and copy three files of chapter 7. This block omits the new bin/fake-agent. The book checker makes its own directory and does not run this block.

mkdir -p ~/ravel-parts-cancel/bin
cd ~/ravel-parts-cancel
cp ~/ravel-parts/settings.json ~/ravel-parts/config.rhai .
cp ~/ravel-parts/bin/fake-advisor bin/

The new fake-agent sends finished, writes a note and sends INT to its parent, the Ravel CLI. Then it waits until Ravel stops it.

Complete example: the managed process sends finished, then sends INT to the Ravel CLI. The caller receives no result.

kill -INT "$PPID"
while :; do sleep 1; done

Command

$ env -i HOME="$PWD/home" TMPDIR="$PWD/tmp" PATH="$PWD/bin:$PATH" ravel start-session --kind demo --config config.rhai --prompt 'Show the parts of Ravel.' > result

Standard output

Standard error includes

ravel: refused (session_result_invalid, stage exec): session was cancelled; no result is returned
  remedy: correct the named process or temporary-directory error and retry start-session
ravel: refused (session_cancelled, stage exec): session selected by <scratch>/work/config.rhai was cancelled by SIGINT
  signal: SIGINT, re-raised once this is reported
  remedy: retry the same command if cancellation was unintended

Command

$ cat agent.txt && test ! -s result && echo "The file result is empty."
$ rmdir tmp && echo "The directory tmp is empty again."

Standard output

agent: sent finished, then INT to ravel start-session
The file result is empty.

0

The note shows that fake-agent sent finished before the signal. Earlier lines of standard error show the routing notice and, on macOS, can show ravel: stop attempt lines. Their process-group IDs change from run to run, so the example does not claim them.

The status is 130, the shell status of INT. Ravel raised INT again, so the shell reports 128 plus the signal number 2.

Follow the two refusals through the code:

  1. watch consumed INT from the runner’s latch, sent INT to the group and stopped the group. Ended carries End::Interrupted, and run keeps the signal as runner_signal.
  2. result_after found the cancellation in Ended and returned an error before it read the file. run made that error session_result_invalid. That is the first refusal.
  3. Handlers::finalise chose INT as the cancellation observation.
  4. settle saw a signal and an original failure. It wrote the original failure, then returned session_cancelled. That is the second refusal.
  5. main wrote the second refusal and called cancellation::reraise.

Two runner outcomes carry cancellation to run:

Runner returnsWhere run gets the signalWhat the value does not establish
Ended with End::InterruptedThe signal field of that endPermission to return a result
LaunchErrorconsumed_signal(), through supervision_failureConfirmed reap, group absence or an Ended value

A result sent before cancellation stays only a candidate. SessionChannel::result_after rejects an interrupted end, even when the file exists. A later signal can also reach the Ravel CLI after that check. Handlers::finalise collects that observation before settle decides the outcome.

Open the supervision cancellation sequence at full size. The figure shows chapter 5’s cancel-session example, whose program sends candidate. The path is the same as for finished in this chapter. Its cleanup arrows are attempts, not confirmation that every operation succeeds.

After the final decision, report_failure in main.rs writes the refusal and calls cancellation::reraise. That function restores the default action of the selected signal, unblocks it and raises it. If propagation fails, it reports the problem and exits with the numeric signal status. The shell statuses are 130 for INT, 143 for TERM and 129 for HUP. Chapter 9 explains entry masks, terminal delivery and the limits of cleanup.

Choose the final outcome

Handlers::finalise and run::settle make different decisions. The first selects the cancellation observation and completes the signal transitions. The second chooses whether cancellation, a failure or a result reaches the caller.

finalise restores the entry mask while the handlers still protect the transition. It then attempts to block the handled signals and to inspect the signals that wait in the queue. Only after that block attempt does it consume the late runner latch. Otherwise a signal could reach that latch between a read and the block, and neither read would find it.

Excerpt from crates/ravel/src/cancellation.rs: Handlers::finalise chooses the first available observation in this expression.

#![allow(unused)]
fn main() {
        let signal = front.or(outcome).or(late).or(received()).or(pending);
}

This expression gives an order of sources. It does not compare the times when the signals arrived. The variables have these meanings:

ExpressionObservation
frontThe latch of the Ravel CLI, before and just after the entry mask returns
outcomeThe signal that the runner’s end or error kept
lateThe runner’s latch, consumed after the final block attempt
received()Another read of the latch of the Ravel CLI
pendingThe result of look(true): the latch of the Ravel CLI first, then an eligible signal in the queue

For signals in the queue, look excludes the signals that the process blocked or ignored at entry. It checks INT, TERM, then HUP. Failures of signal operations become reports. They are not an invented cancellation, and they do not prove that no signal exists. finalise returns the selected signal and those reports to run.

The helper named join_cancellation in run.rs is test-only. It is not the production decision shown above.

In the cancellation example, outcome held INT from End::Interrupted. That observation alone is enough to select INT.

run gives settle the candidate result or the original failure, the optional Ended, all cleanup outcomes and the selected signal. It orders the cleanup outcomes as mask restoration, channel removal, output restoration, then the final signal-operation reports. settle reports every cleanup error and any group that stays. When cancellation overrides an original failure, it reports that failure too.

Open the outcome-precedence activity at full size. The figure separates report order from outcome priority after the required cleanup attempts. Under cancellation, settle reports an original failure first, then cleanup errors, then a group that stays. Its signal step gives the priority of sources, not the order of arrival.

The primary outcome follows this order:

First condition that appliesDecision
A cancellation observation existssession_cancelled, no result, the selected signal. A cancelled skill resolution keeps its own refusal, skill_resolution_cancelled.
The original operation failedKeep that failure and its status
Otherwise, a cleanup failedThe first cleanup error, status 5
Otherwise, an Ended reports a group that staysprocess_group_survived, status 5
None of these conditions appliesReturn the candidate result to run for output

Excerpt from crates/ravel/src/run.rs: after its cancellation branch, settle keeps an original error before it selects a cleanup error.

#![allow(unused)]
fn main() {
    let result = result?;
    if let Some(error) = cleanups.into_iter().find_map(Result::err) {
        return Err(error.into());
    }
}

For example, session_result_missing plus a channel cleanup error keeps status 1 and reports the cleanup problem. If INT also reaches the final decision, cancellation wins and both failures stay as diagnostics. When an Ended reports a group that stays, result_after fails first. Thus, the original failure is already session_result_invalid. The final group branch in settle still prevents success independently.

Only a successful settle reaches the result write. A write or flush failure then gives result_write_failed, status 5. A later signal can still stop the Ravel CLI during output, and a failed write can leave a partial value. The caller accepts a result only after success. The final decision and the output write are not one atomic operation.

Find where a change belongs

You now know the order of the path. Which file do you open for a change? The table names the first file for frequent changes. Each row also names the neighbour that the change can affect.

ChangeStart inCheck also
A new route or a new refusal of the ownerThe configuration scriptNothing in Ravel
A new host capabilityrhai_worker/bridge.rs and rhai_worker/host.rsrhai_worker/convert.rs, the limits and the specification configuration-evaluation.md
The shape that select can returnconfig::judge_result in config.rschoice::script_refusal and the reference in chapter 6
The code or status of a failure of the scriptchoice::script_refusal in choice.rsThe exit-status table in chapter 6
The deadline, the output limit or worker teardownworker::supervise in worker.rsWatch in rhai_worker/serve.rs
A harness runharness::execute in rhai_worker/harness.rsHost::run_harness and Host::settle in rhai_worker/host.rs
The order of failures and cancellationsettle in run.rsHandlers::finalise in cancellation.rs
Process groups, escalation or the terminalcrates/keyed-launch/src/run.rsChapter 9

Suppose that you change how Ravel stores a sent result, and the behaviour of the CLI stays the same. Start with crates/ravel/src/completion.rs. It owns allocation, the first complete value, checked reads and explicit channel removal. A change there also needs checks at its three consumers: the runner, run and harness::execute.

Code to inspectProperty that the change keeps
publish_result and SessionChannel in completion.rsRavel keeps the first complete value, empty differs from absent, and UTF-8 text stays unchanged
Channel::appeared in crates/keyed-launch/src/channel.rsAppearance alone tells the runner when to start completion, without a read of the contents
Launch.channel, CapturedLaunch.channel and Ended in the runnerEach launch uses its own channel and reports job state separately from appearance
run in crates/ravel/src/run.rsA fresh session-result context for each launch, a channel and an Ended from the same launch, explicit cleanup and the final priority
harness::execute in crates/ravel/src/rhai_worker/harness.rsA harness run’s result goes to the script as run.result, and a group that stays gives no result

The completion name now appears only after the complete write. A new mechanism that shows a partly written value would break that agreement with the runner. Empty results also need a positive completion event, because zero bytes do not mean that no result exists.

SessionChannel::result_after accepts an Ended reference. Its type cannot establish that this end belongs to this channel. run and harness::execute each establish the association. Each passes its own channel to the runner, then passes that runner’s end to result_after. A storage change keeps this association as well as the file contents.

For this change, exercise the result channel with empty, multiline and Unicode values, repeated sends, invalid files and nested sessions. Then exercise cancellation after a sent result, as in ravel-parts-cancel. Also exercise a result failure plus a cleanup failure, and cancellation plus a cleanup failure. A status check alone cannot show that the original failure and every cleanup report stayed.

The configuration script’s choice of program does not depend on how Ravel stores a result. Its behaviour can stay unchanged for this change. The code boundaries above tell you where to inspect and test a change. They do not give a supported public component interface.

Jobs, signals and the terminal

What does Ravel do when a program does not stop? Why can a result wait several seconds before it reaches you? And what happens to a slow LLM call when you press Ctrl-C? This chapter answers these questions. It explains how Ravel ends each program that it starts, how it lends the terminal and which signal state each program receives. It also states where these guarantees stop.

In chapter 7 you ran the example ravel-parts. In that session the Ravel CLI started two programs. fake-advisor was a harness run during selection, and fake-agent was the managed process. Both fakes ended by themselves, so the session showed no stop. This chapter changes one fake at a time to make each stop visible.

These are the words that this chapter uses. Each row gives the value that the word has in ravel-parts.

WordWhat it namesIn ravel-parts
Ravel CLIThe ravel command-line program. It checks the request, supervises the session and decides the outcome.The process ravel start-session --kind demo …
RunnerThe Rust library code in the Ravel CLI that starts and supervises a jobIt runs both fakes
Harness runOne run of a program from the harnesses settings, during evaluation. Its output returns to the configuration script.fake-advisor
Managed processThe program that the configuration script selects. It does the caller’s work.fake-agent
JobA program that the runner starts, and the other processes in its process groupOne job for each fake
Result channelA private temporary directory with one path for a result file. The path goes to a program in RAVEL_EXIT_FILE.One for each fake
CancellationAn INT, TERM or HUP signal that reaches the Ravel CLINone: nothing sent a signal

A signal name such as SIGTERM has the prefix SIG. This chapter writes INT, TERM, HUP and KILL without that prefix, as the shell trap command does. Other signals keep their full names.

A result requests the end of a job. It does not prove that the job has ended. The Ravel CLI stays alive to supervise that end before it returns the result to the caller. Chapter 3 introduced a job and its reap through one session. Chapter 8 shows where these operations occur in the code.

Each program runs as a job

The runner starts each program as the leader of a new process group. The group’s ID is the program’s PID. Descendants inherit this group unless they explicitly change it. The program and the members of its group form the job. In ravel-parts, fake-advisor led one job and fake-agent led another.

A signal to a process group can reach its members together. A signal to the program’s PID reaches only that program. If Ravel stopped only the program, a helper could keep a file lock or continue to change the caller’s files. The group gives Ravel a scope for shutdown that includes ordinary descendants.

A POSIX session can contain several process groups and can have a controlling terminal. The two kinds of job differ here. The managed process can need the caller’s terminal, because an interactive harness talks to a human there. A harness run gives its output to the configuration script, so it needs no terminal.

ChoiceUsed forConsequence
A new process group in the caller’s POSIX sessionThe managed processRavel can signal the job together and can lend the caller’s terminal
A new POSIX session through setsidEach harness runThe run starts without a controlling terminal. The terminal’s keyboard signals do not reach it, and /dev/tty names no terminal for it.
Only the program’s PID as the shutdown targetNothingDescendants can survive their parent. This does not meet Ravel’s cleanup contract.

A descendant can leave the job’s group, for example through its own setsid call. Group cleanup does not cover that descendant. Ravel does not promise to track every process descended from the managed process or from a harness run. A new process group also creates no new Ravel session: that term means one start-session invocation. The Rhai worker is no job. It stays in the process group of the Ravel CLI, and the Ravel CLI stops it by its PID.

Run a managed process that stays after its result

What does Ravel do with a program that sends its result and then does not exit? You can see it with one change to ravel-parts. The example directory is ~/ravel-parts-escalation. Make it and copy the three files that stay the same:

Fragment: make the example directory and copy three files of chapter 7. This block omits the new bin/fake-agent. The book checker makes its own directory and does not run this block.

mkdir -p ~/ravel-parts-escalation/bin
cd ~/ravel-parts-escalation
cp ~/ravel-parts/settings.json ~/ravel-parts/config.rhai .
cp ~/ravel-parts/bin/fake-advisor bin/

The new fake-agent sends finished and then stays alive. Its trap line tells the shell to write a note when TERM arrives, and to continue. Thus, Ravel’s escalation must reach KILL to end it. The shell runs the note after the current sleep ends. The TERM to the group also ends that sleep.

Complete example: the managed process sends finished, then stays alive after TERM. The caller still receives finished.

settings.json

{"harnesses":{"advisor":{"program":"fake-advisor"}},"budgets":{"harnessRuns":1},"skills":{"schemaVersion":1,"sources":[]}}

config.rhai

export const SCHEMA_VERSION = 4;
export const VERSION = "parts";

fn select(request, context) {
    let run = ravel::run_harness(#{ harness: "advisor", args: [] });
    print(`The advisor run ended: ${run.status}, result ${run.result}.`);
    #{
        status: "selected",
        program: "fake-agent",
        args: [run.stdout],
        provider: "local",
        model: "fake",
        effort: "none",
        reason: "Show each part of Ravel.",
    }
}

bin/fake-advisor

#!/bin/sh
{
  echo "advisor: parent is $(ps -o args= -p "$PPID" | cut -d ' ' -f 1-2)"
  echo "advisor: RAVEL_CONFIG_RUN is ${RAVEL_CONFIG_RUN-absent}"
} > advisor.txt
printf '%s\n' "$RAVEL_EXIT_FILE" > advisor.path
printf 'short'
ravel end-session -- "advice"

bin/fake-agent

#!/bin/sh
trap 'echo "agent: received TERM and stayed" >> agent.txt' TERM
echo "agent: sent finished" > agent.txt
ravel end-session -- "finished"
while :; do sleep 0.05; done

Command

$ mkdir -p home/.config/ravel tmp && chmod +x bin/* && cp settings.json home/.config/ravel/settings.json
$ env -i HOME="$PWD/home" TMPDIR="$PWD/tmp" PATH="$PWD/bin:$PATH" ravel start-session --kind demo --config config.rhai --prompt 'Show the parts of Ravel.' > result

Standard output

Exit status

0

Command

$ cat agent.txt result && printf '\n'
$ rmdir tmp && echo "The directory tmp is empty again."

Standard output

agent: sent finished
agent: received TERM and stayed
finished
The directory tmp is empty again.

Exit status

0

The session takes at least seven seconds. The note shows that TERM arrived after the result and that the agent stayed. KILL then ended it. The death of fake-agent from KILL does not invalidate its result. finished reaches the caller only after the job ends.

The example check compares the result bytes, the note and the exit status. It does not compare the duration or standard error. macOS can add ravel: stop attempt lines whose process-group IDs change from run to run. The automatic example check has no terminal and uses no real harness. The next section explains each stage of this stop.

Follow the escalation after a result

Why did ravel-parts-escalation take at least seven seconds? An interactive harness can finish its work and then wait for another prompt. The caller therefore tells it to send an opaque result with end-session. The command creates the completion file in the result channel. The runner observes that file’s presence. Ravel checks its contents separately.

Until it sends KILL, the runner checks the program and the channel at regular intervals. For the managed process, the interval is 500 milliseconds. For a harness run, it is 20 milliseconds, because a deadline and an output limit bound the run. The first observation of the file starts the grace period. Thus, the schedule starts at observation, not at the instant the sender calls end-session.

StageConditionAction and wait
GraceThe runner observes the completion file while the program still runsAllow 2 seconds for the program to exit
TERMThe program still runs after the graceSend TERM to the group and the program. Allow up to 5 seconds, the kill grace, for the program to exit
KILLThe program still runs after the kill graceSend KILL to the group and the program. Check for exit at 10-millisecond intervals
Final cleanupThe runner observes the program’s exitStop the other group members before the reap, then recover the terminal and confirm group absence

In ravel-parts-escalation, fake-agent passed through every stage. It sent finished and stayed through the grace. It wrote its note at TERM and stayed through the kill grace. KILL ended it, and final cleanup followed.

The figure separates the grace periods from final cleanup. Both exit paths reach the group stops before reap, then the terminal recovery and group query.

Open the escalation figure at full size.

TERM lets the program handle its own shutdown. A program can ignore TERM. It cannot catch or ignore KILL. If the program exits during either grace period, Ravel proceeds to final cleanup without the later escalation stages. Descendants receive no separate grace period after the program exits.

The 2-second and 5-second values belong to the Ravel CLI, as run::ESCALATION. It gives the same values to the managed process and to each harness run. The runner accepts its caller’s durations. These values are not a seven-second deadline for start-session: selection, observation delays and final cleanup also take time. Without a result, a stop request or cancellation, this escalation places no time limit on the managed process.

After the escalation’s first KILL attempt, the runner uses a two-second bound for its exit checks and reap attempts. It does not restart that bound after an error. If the bound expires, the runner reports unresolved ownership, and Ravel returns no result to the caller. This bound limits retries. It cannot guarantee a wall-clock deadline when the scheduler or a kernel operation stalls.

Stop the group before the reap

A process can exit before its parent collects its status. Until that collection, it remains a zombie: the system retains its identity and exit status, although it executes no more code. To reap the child, the parent collects that status and releases the retained process identity.

Ravel first observes exit without a reap. Its waitid call uses WNOWAIT for that purpose. The unreaped child reserves its PID, which is also the job’s group ID. Ravel uses that reservation for the last group signals.

If Ravel reaped first, the system could reuse the ID for an unrelated group. A later KILL to that number could then reach that unrelated group. The normal cleanup path therefore has this order, for the managed process and for each harness run:

StepOperationWhat it establishes
1Observe the child’s exit with waitid and WNOWAITThe child has exited, but still reserves its identity. A stop is not an exit
2Send KILL to its group twice, with a 20-millisecond pauseAttempt to stop the other members while the identity remains reserved
3Reap the direct child with waitpidConfirm the child’s status and release its identity
4Recover the terminal if Ravel lent itCheck foreground recovery and restore the saved modes. A harness run never receives the terminal, so this step does nothing for it
5Query the group without a signal, for up to one secondConfirm absence only when the system returns ESRCH, meaning no such process group

The second KILL addresses a macOS case where a fork overlaps the first group signal and leaves a new member. Both attempts precede the reap. They do not change the direct child’s status: it has already exited. Ravel reaps its direct child. Other descendants’ parents, or the system, reap those descendants.

A successful signal operation does not prove that the group is gone. The final query uses kill with signal 0, which sends no signal. Success or EPERM means that absence remains unconfirmed. An unrelated group that reuses the ID also prevents confirmation. Ravel does not signal that number after the reap.

On macOS, a group with only zombies can reject KILL with EPERM, or Operation not permitted. Ravel can retain this stop report without a session failure, but only after confirmed reap, successful terminal recovery and an ESRCH absence check. The report alone never confirms cleanup. Ravel writes retained reports as ravel: lines on standard error, or in operationReports with --json.

The same refusal can occur before Ravel observes the exit. The result’s TERM can reach the group while its leader is inside its exit teardown. The leader’s own PID still accepts the signal, but waitid does not report the exit yet. Ravel therefore continues to watch after a group stop answers EPERM. The report then waits for the same three checks. A refused stop to the direct child, or another error, ends the watch at once.

These failures prevent success. Their outcome depends on the kind of job:

FailureFor the managed processFor a harness run
The group still answers the query with success or EPERM after the confirmation wait, or the query failsRavel reports the problem and returns no result to the callerThe evaluation ends as harness_cleanup_failed, status 5. The configuration script receives no map.
An observation or reap errorRavel attempts bounded cleanup, reports what it cannot confirm and returns no resultThe same refusal, harness_cleanup_failed, status 5
Ravel cannot remove the result channelsession_cleanup_failed, and no resultThe same refusal, harness_cleanup_failed, status 5

Once the runner loses or releases the child identity, it stops all numeric signal attempts. After a timeout, it never reports a reap that did not occur.

Stop a harness run

In ravel-parts, fake-advisor printed short, sent advice and exited. What if it does not exit? A real LLM call can wait on a slow model service. You may want such a call to stop at a deadline, so that your configuration script can choose a fallback route.

The Ravel CLI does not send a signal to a harness run itself. It asks the runner instead. Each run has a StopRequest, a flag that the runtime thread of the Ravel CLI sets. The runner also keeps a second flag of its own, for the output limit. It reads both flags at its 20-millisecond checks.

When one flag is set, the runner sends TERM to the run’s group and to the run, without the 2-second grace. If the run still exists after the 5-second kill grace, the runner sends KILL. Then it stops the group before the reap, reaps the run and confirms that the group is gone.

Three causes stop a run. Only the third ends the evaluation:

CauseWho asks for the stopWhat the configuration script sees
The deadline of the run: a timeoutMs in the ravel::run_harness call that is less than the time that remainsThe Ravel CLI, at that deadlineThe map with status timed_out. The script continues.
More than 1 MiB on standard output or standard errorThe runner’s own thread that reads the run’s pipes, through the runner’s second flagThe map with status output_limit. The script continues.
A stop of the whole evaluation: its deadline, a cancellation, the output limit of the script, or a worker that dies or breaks the protocolThe Ravel CLI, in Host::settleNothing. The worker receives no more replies, and Ravel refuses.

Without a timeoutMs, or with one that is not less than the time that remains, the run has no deadline of its own. The evaluation deadline then stops it as the third cause, and the script never sees timed_out.

Chapter 4 shows a configuration script that checks run.status before it uses the answer. The next two sections show the third cause, which also stops the worker.

Stop the evaluation at its deadline

The evaluation has one deadline for all of its work: the worker start, the reads, each harness run and select. The owner sets it as timeoutMs in the settings, and --timeout-ms replaces it for one session. The smallest permitted value is 1,000 milliseconds.

When that deadline expires during a harness run, the Ravel CLI stops the evaluation in this order:

  1. It records the first cause. A later cause does not repeat the stop.
  2. It abandons a file read in progress, and it does not wait for it.
  3. It asks the runner to stop a harness run in progress. The runner escalates from TERM to KILL, clears the group and reaps the run.
  4. It stops the worker with TERM, kills it if it is still there after one second, and reaps it.
  5. It removes the private directories and reports every cleanup failure.
  6. It reads the cancellation latch again, and then it refuses.

The sequence shows why harness cleanup precedes worker teardown, and why a later signal changes the final refusal.

Open the deadline sequence at full size.

The latch is a variable of the Ravel CLI. Its signal handlers keep the first INT, TERM or HUP there.

Only one host call runs at a time, so only one of steps 2 and 3 applies. A stop drops the host call that waits, which abandons a read at once. A run continues after that drop, until Host::settle asks the runner to stop it. The worker only waits for its reply and holds nothing that needs a graceful stop.

Host::settle waits at most 8 seconds for the runner. That is the 5-second kill grace, plus the runner’s bounds for a failed reap and for the group query. A process that survives is a worse outcome than the deadline. Thus, each failure of the run below replaces the deadline with harness_cleanup_failed, status 5:

Failure of the runHow Ravel then stops the worker
The runner does not settle the run in 8 secondsNo TERM. Ravel waits up to one second for the worker to exit, then kills it. The reap of the run is unconfirmed.
The run’s group stays after its reapNo TERM. Ravel waits up to one second for the worker to exit, then kills it.
The reap of the run failsNo TERM. Ravel waits up to one second for the worker to exit, then kills it.

Run this order with one change to ravel-parts. The example directory is ~/ravel-parts-deadline. Make it and copy the three files that stay the same:

Fragment: make the example directory and copy three files of chapter 7. This block omits the new bin/fake-advisor. The book checker makes its own directory and does not run this block.

mkdir -p ~/ravel-parts-deadline/bin
cd ~/ravel-parts-deadline
cp ~/ravel-parts/settings.json ~/ravel-parts/config.rhai .
cp ~/ravel-parts/bin/fake-agent bin/

The new fake-advisor writes its PID to advisor.pid. It never exits, and it writes a note when TERM arrives. The command adds --timeout-ms 1000.

Complete example: the evaluation deadline expires during a harness run that stays after TERM. Ravel stops the run, then the worker, and starts nothing.

The session takes a little more than six seconds: the 1-second deadline, then the 5-second kill grace of the run. Each line of the output shows one step of the stop:

OutputWhat it showsStep
selection_timeout, status 124The deadline was the first cause. No signal reached the Ravel CLI before its final read.1 and 6
advisor: received TERM and stayedThe runner sent TERM to the run at the deadline. The run stayed, so the runner sent KILL after the kill grace.3
The advisor process is gone.kill -0 sends no signal. It fails because the run’s PID no longer exists: Ravel reaped it.3
The agent never started.Ravel started no managed process after the stop6
The directory tmp is empty again.Ravel removed the run’s result channel and its other private directories5

The print line of the script never ran, because ravel::run_harness never returned. fake-agent is the file of chapter 7, and it never runs here.

Let a signal win over the deadline

Step 6 above reads the cancellation latch again. Why? A signal can reach the Ravel CLI while it stops the evaluation for another cause. The completion contract gives cancellation the first priority. Thus, a signal that the Ravel CLI consumed before its final read wins over the deadline.

During evaluation, the runner holds no signal handler. The Ravel CLI installs its own handlers for INT, TERM and HUP at the start of start-session. They keep the first signal in its latch. A signal that was ignored when the Ravel CLI started keeps that state and never reaches the latch.

The runtime thread reads that latch every 50 milliseconds. A harness run is in a POSIX session of its own, so a signal to the caller’s process group does not reach it. The Ravel CLI stops the run through its StopRequest.

Change one line of fake-advisor in ~/ravel-parts-deadline to see the rule. The new trap line also sends TERM to the fake’s parent, the Ravel CLI. That TERM arrives after the deadline and before the kill grace of the run ends.

Complete example: the deadline expires during a harness run. The run’s TERM handler then sends TERM to the Ravel CLI, and the signal wins.

settings.json

{"harnesses":{"advisor":{"program":"fake-advisor"}},"budgets":{"harnessRuns":1},"skills":{"schemaVersion":1,"sources":[]}}

config.rhai

export const SCHEMA_VERSION = 4;
export const VERSION = "parts";

fn select(request, context) {
    let run = ravel::run_harness(#{ harness: "advisor", args: [] });
    print(`The advisor run ended: ${run.status}, result ${run.result}.`);
    #{
        status: "selected",
        program: "fake-agent",
        args: [run.stdout],
        provider: "local",
        model: "fake",
        effort: "none",
        reason: "Show each part of Ravel.",
    }
}

bin/fake-advisor

#!/bin/sh

echo "advisor: started" > advisor.txt
echo "$$" > advisor.pid
while :; do sleep 0.05; done

  echo "agent: argument is $1"

The directory tmp is empty again.
