0

Compare the two runs. The stop was the same: the run received TERM at the deadline, stayed, and ended by KILL after the kill grace. Then the worker stopped, and nothing started. Only the final decision changed:

Deadline aloneDeadline, then TERM to the Ravel CLI
First causeThe deadlineThe deadline
Signal in the latch at the final readNoneTERM
Refusalselection_timeoutselection_cancelled, with selection_timeout under “original failure:”
Exit status124143, which is 128 plus 15, the number of TERM
How the Ravel CLI endsIt exits with status 124It reports, then raises TERM again

The second TERM did not change the run’s stop, and the kill grace did not restart. The runner never reads the latch of the Ravel CLI for a harness run. Host::settle only waits for the stop that it already asked for. Standard error also shows a session_cancelled report after these lines, and /bin/sh reports that TERM ended the command. The example does not claim those lines.

The same final read follows every other refusal of the evaluation. A signal therefore also wins over a breach of a limit and over harness_cleanup_failed. Among signals, the first that the Ravel CLI consumed stays the cause. A later signal does not replace it. The last section of this chapter states the limit for a signal that arrives after the final decision.

Handle cancellation

Cancellation differs from a sent result. INT, TERM or HUP received by the Ravel CLI suppresses the result, even if the managed process already sent it. What Ravel stops depends on where the signal arrives:

Where cancellation occursWho holds the signalProcess actionResult
During selection, with a harness run in progressThe latch of the Ravel CLIStop the harness run through its StopRequest, then stop and reap the Rhai worker. Do not start the managed process.selection_cancelled, then session_cancelled. Return no result to the caller.
During selection, with no harness runThe latch of the Ravel CLIStop and reap the Rhai worker. Do not start the managed process.selection_cancelled, then session_cancelled. Return no result to the caller.
During supervisionThe runner’s handlers, which replace those of the Ravel CLI for the managed launch Forward the signal to the group and the direct child. If the child still runs after the kill grace, send KILL.session_cancelled. Complete cleanup attempts and return no result.

During supervision, the runner checks for cancellation before it accepts an observed child exit. The Ravel CLI also checks signals around supervision and final cleanup. Cancellation skips the result’s initial grace. If the kill grace has already begun, cancellation does not restart it.

The sequence shows why a sent result cannot survive cancellation during supervision. Ravel attempts cleanup before it raises the signal again.

Open the supervision cancellation sequence at full size.

The managed process can itself supervise another process, for example a nested ravel start-session. The forwarded signal and the kill grace let that supervisor attempt its own cleanup. Chapter 5 explains nested sessions.

The two cancellation sequences in chapter 5 show the selection and supervision cases, with complete fake-program examples. Chapter 8 follows the selection case through Host::settle and worker::supervise. Chapter 8 also explains which signal and diagnostic Ravel reports when failures overlap. A cleanup failure stays visible beside cancellation. It does not permit a previously sent result to reach standard output.

Separate the three launch modes

The runner has three launch modes. The Ravel CLI uses two of them. The table compares the three modes:

ModeRunner interfacePOSIX session and inputOutput, completion and signals
Ordinary launch, for the managed processrun_observed_with_start_checkA new process group in the caller’s POSIX session. Inherited input, with terminal access when availableOutput goes to the caller’s standard error. A result can request shutdown. The runner installs cancellation handlers.
Captured launch, for each harness runrun_captured, with CapturedLaunchA new POSIX session, no controlling terminal, no inherited descriptors above 2 after exec. Standard input is the run’s stdin text, or end-of-file (EOF) without itThe runner’s thread reads both streams into buffers of 1 MiB each. A result requests shutdown, and so does a StopRequest. The runner installs no signal handler.
Noninteractive launchrun_noninteractiveA new POSIX session, no controlling terminal, EOF input, no inherited descriptors above 2 after execBoth streams go to a regular file that the caller supplies. No completion channel. The runner installs cancellation handlers.

run_noninteractive has no production caller in Ravel. A test of crates/ravel/src/cancellation.rs calls it.

Why does a captured launch install no signal handler? The Ravel CLI owns its signals during evaluation. Its runtime thread watches the latch, the deadline and the worker together, and it decides when to stop the run. If the runner replaced the handlers of the Ravel CLI, that thread could lose a signal.

A new POSIX session removes the inherited controlling terminal relationship. It does not restrict file access, and a descendant can still move to another group. No mode of the runner restricts file access. These distinctions explain why the runner hides process mechanics behind separate launch interfaces, while the Ravel CLI owns the configuration script and result decisions.

The runner tests check these process operations directly:

Test source under crates/keyed-launch/tests/What it checks
launch.rsGrace and KILL branches, ordinary descendants gone on return, and an unrelated process left untouched
captured.rsSeparate streams, standard input and EOF, and the output bound. A stop request that ends descendants, KILL after the kill grace, a new group without a terminal, and the EPERM cases
internal/wait_events.rsCleanup order, a reported survivor, and cancellation across wait failures through a private process substitute
noninteractive.rsEOF input, descriptor isolation, a new POSIX session, forwarded cancellation, and KILL after the kill grace
job.rsTerminal foreground and mode behaviour through pseudo-terminals

task test:runner passed these tests on aarch64 macOS 27.0.1 on 12 November 2026. The tests check named operations and process shapes. They do not establish containment against every same-user attack or cleanup of a descendant that leaves the job’s group. They also do not qualify a native x86-64 Linux host.

Lend and recover the terminal

A controlling terminal belongs to a POSIX session. It has one foreground process group at a time. That group can read terminal input. With the default terminal modes, Ctrl-C sends INT to that group. A background group’s attempt to read can instead stop it with SIGTTIN.

Start the Ravel CLI in the foreground of an interactive shell. During selection, its group is then the foreground group, and the Rhai worker is in that group too. A harness run is in another POSIX session, so Ctrl-C does not reach it. During supervision, the runner lends the foreground to the managed group, under the rules below.

The runner opens /dev/tty, which names its controlling terminal. Redirected standard input does not remove that relationship. If the open fails, the runner proceeds without terminal job control. Detached modes, such as a harness run, do not open the terminal at all.

An open terminal alone permits no handoff. The runner queries its foreground group with tcgetpgrp and lends it only when the runner’s own group holds it. A Ravel CLI that starts in the background takes no foreground for itself. A shell can later give the foreground to the Ravel CLI’s group, for example with fg. Then a later supervision poll can lend the terminal to the managed group.

Before the first handoff, the runner saves the terminal attributes with tcgetattr. These terminal modes control such behaviour as character echo, line input and keyboard signals. An interactive program can change them to receive individual characters without echo. If KILL ends that program, the program cannot restore the modes itself.

The ordinary foreground launch follows these steps:

StepOwner and operationPurpose
SaveThe runner retains a terminal descriptor, foreground ownership and the current modes in a terminal leaseKeep the recovery information alive through spawn and supervision
RecordThe child enters its new group and writes that group ID to a private pipeTell the parent which group can take the terminal, even if exec fails
LendBefore exec, the child calls tcsetpgrp to make its group foregroundComplete the handoff before the program’s first terminal read
SuperviseThe runner observes the child and performs any required group stops before reapRetain the group identity for those signals
RecoverAfter reap, the runner applies the holder rules below and checks the terminal againRestore saved modes only when the runner’s group owns foreground

The sequence shows why the terminal lease survives the exec attempt. Its recovery branches depend on the child outcome and the current foreground holder.

Open the terminal sequence at full size.

A background group can receive SIGTTOU when it changes terminal foreground. The runner therefore temporarily ignores SIGTTOU for the handback (the return of foreground to the runner’s group) and for mode restoration. Then it restores its previous disposition. It checks both the terminal operation and that restoration. The child also suppresses SIGTTOU for its handoff before it restores its entry signal state.

The lease remembers whether this launch ever held foreground. If it never did, the runner takes no foreground and restores no modes. For a lease that did hold foreground, the child’s outcome changes the rule:

Outcome and current holderForeground and mode action
The runner’s group already holds foregroundRestore the saved modes
Normal child exit, with the child’s group still foregroundTake foreground back, confirm it, then restore the modes
Normal child exit, with another group foregroundPreserve that holder and its modes
Known child death by signalReclaim from a positive foreground group other than the runner’s group or the POSIX session leader’s group. Restore modes only after confirmed ownership
Failed spawn or unknown child status, with the recorded child’s group foregroundAttempt the conditional handback described below, then restore modes after confirmed ownership
Failed spawn or unknown child status, with an unrelated group foregroundPreserve it and report unconfirmed foreground and mode recovery

The wider signal-death rule covers a managed supervisor that gives foreground to another group, then dies without cleanup. Otherwise, that delegated group could retain a raw terminal and continue to read input. The accepted cost is that a nested job-control shell can lose its foreground after such a death. Unknown child status does not permit this wider takeover. The POSIX session leader is the process that created the terminal’s POSIX session.

Exec can fail after the child takes foreground. The parent therefore retains the lease across a spawn error. The private pipe closes across exec and provides an attempted-group record, not proof of exec or a live group. If the record is missing or invalid, recovery is unconfirmed and Ravel reports it. The runner never signals that recorded number after failed spawn.

Failed-spawn handback requires two conditions through recovery: no other process or thread changes foreground, and no unrelated group reuses the recorded ID. These conditions also apply to recovery from unknown status. The foreground query and handback are separate operations. A one-session lifetime of the Ravel CLI does not make them atomic or establish those conditions.

Ravel reports failures of foreground queries, handback, mode restoration and temporary SIGTTOU restoration. After a recovery failure, Ravel returns no result to the caller. After failed spawn, the original spawn error stays primary unless cancellation overrides it. Recovery reports remain visible beside that outcome.

The tests use a pseudo-terminal, or PTY, to observe these operations without a human terminal. A PTY has two ends. The fixture uses one as its controlling terminal, while the test controls input and reads output through the other. Separate fixture processes keep terminal and signal changes out of the test runner itself.

Test sourceObserved distinction
crates/keyed-launch/tests/job.rsA raw-mode child actually changes the modes before KILL. The runner then recovers foreground and saved modes
crates/keyed-launch/tests/job.rsThe same other foreground holder remains after normal exit but loses foreground after signal death
crates/keyed-launch/src/run/terminal_tests.rsFailed exec retains the lease. Unknown status preserves an unrelated holder. Recovery failures remain in the error

These tests check named process arrangements and failure reports. They do not prove exclusive terminal authority in every caller environment. The book’s complete examples have no terminal, so they do not check these handoff branches.

Preserve the entry signal state

A signal’s disposition is the process-wide choice to use its default action, ignore it, or call a handler. A thread’s signal mask names signals whose delivery that thread blocks. A blocked signal can remain queued until the thread permits delivery. An ignored signal and a blocked signal are different states.

Exec replaces the program but preserves the thread’s signal mask. It resets caught handlers to default actions. It preserves ignored dispositions with one exception: portable code cannot assume that an ignored SIGCHLD stays ignored after exec. The POSIX exec specification states that exception. Ravel explicitly restores its recorded ignore/default values before exec on its supported targets.

On its supported targets, Ravel gives the managed process and each harness run the signal state that the Ravel CLI had at its executable entry. That is the state before Rust startup changes it. The table below gives the exclusions. For example, Rust startup ignores SIGPIPE. A capture in main would see that new ignore instead of the state that Ravel inherited.

The initialiser of the Ravel CLI in signal_state.rs records the ignored set and the initial thread’s mask before those runtime changes. It keeps that record separate from later repairs by the Ravel CLI and the runner. If a required query fails, the Ravel CLI refuses the launch. It does not accept a partial record. The runner receives the values through EntrySignals, in Launch.transparent for the managed process and in CapturedLaunch.transparent for each harness run. It does not capture them itself.

StateChild behaviour in a transparent launch
Recorded ignoreSet the signal to ignored before exec
Other recorded dispositionSet the signal to its default action. Do not copy a host handler
Recorded maskApply the mask after the dispositions, so delivery uses the restored actions
KILL and SIGSTOPLeave these unchangeable dispositions alone
Unsupported or runtime-reserved signal numbersExclude the platform’s specified numbers. A failure for another required number remains an error

The Ravel CLI and the runner also need signal state of their own. An ignored SIGCHLD or the SA_NOCLDWAIT flag can let the system discard child status without a parent reap. That would remove both the wait result and the identity reservation needed for group cleanup. The Ravel CLI checks and repairs this state before the Rhai worker starts. The runner checks it before each job starts: each harness run and the managed process. These repairs do not change the immutable child entry values.

For the managed launch, the runner installs handlers for nonignored TERM and HUP. It also handles nonignored INT when no terminal exists or the launch is transparent. Ravel uses a transparent launch, so a nonignored INT sent directly to the Ravel CLI can cancel it even while the managed group owns foreground. The runner leaves ignored cancellation signals ignored. For a harness run, the runner installs nothing, and the handlers of the Ravel CLI stay.

Terminal INT follows foreground ownership. Before handoff, it can reach the group of the Ravel CLI. After handoff, it reaches the managed group. The child’s death from that signal is not itself a cancellation observation by the Ravel CLI. Ravel can still return a result after child signal death if the Ravel CLI observes no cancellation and all required cleanup succeeds.

The runner’s handlers and SIGCHLD repair persist after its call returns, on success or failure. A synchronous return does not restore a host’s prior state. The table gives the restoration responsibility at each boundary:

BoundaryRestoration responsibility
EntrySignalsSupply the child’s ignore/default state and mask. It restores no host state
Runner returnReport the child outcome and recovery failures. Retain installed cancellation handlers and any SIGCHLD repair
Final cleanup of the Ravel CLICheck temporary masks and restore the saved INT/TERM/HUP dispositions under the conditions below
A host that resumes other workNeeds its own signal lifetime, late-cancellation policy and restoration contract. The current runner supplies no such guarantee

The Ravel CLI blocks its handled signals across the spawn transition of the managed process. After spawn reports success, its checked Started transition restores the parent’s entry mask. Failure enters bounded child teardown and terminal recovery. It does not return early with an abandoned child.

Started means spawn-reported success, not confirmed exec. A child can die before exec and still produce that event. Only an actual wait produces Reaped.

Which thread receives a signal? The system can deliver a process signal to any thread that does not block it. Ravel therefore blocks INT, TERM and HUP in each helper thread for its whole life:

ThreadWhat it doesHow it gets the block
config-stdout and config-stderrRead the worker’s standard output and standard errorThe Ravel CLI blocks the signals before it creates them, then restores its own mask
ravel-readerDoes every file read and every harness run of the evaluationThe same, through Shield::block in rhai_worker/reader.rs
keyed-launch-pipesReads the pipes of a harness runThe reader thread creates it, and a new thread inherits the mask of its creator

A block on only the main thread would leave another thread able to consume a signal during handoff. A stop can leave a reader thread alive until the Ravel CLI exits, for example after a bounded output drain or an abandoned file read.

On both success and failure, final cleanup keeps handlers installed while the Ravel CLI restores its entry mask and collects cancellation observations. It then blocks the handled signals, reads queued signal state and consumes the runner’s late signal. Under that block, it restores its saved handled dispositions. A failed block keeps the handlers. Unknown queued signal state never means that cancellation is absent.

Final conditionAction of the Ravel CLI
Known state, successful disposition restoration, no cancellationRestore the entry mask. Other cleanup checks still precede result output
Cancellation selectedReport it and cleanup failures, then check the default action, unblock and raise the selected signal. Use a numeric fallback if propagation cannot succeed
Queued signal query failed while signals remain blocked, with no selected cancellationKeep them blocked through diagnostics. Return failure without a result
Required mask or disposition operation failedRetain the failure report and return no result

An entry-blocked signal does not become cancellation merely because it is queued. The managed child receives that entry block too. The final signal decision of the Ravel CLI follows the priority in chapter 8. Its cleanup restores the saved handled dispositions and temporary masks. It does not undo the SIGCHLD repair or restore every runtime signal change for a host that continues after the call.

Know the guarantees and their limits

Each guarantee below includes the boundary within which it holds. The successful result is evidence of the required checks, not proof that every descendant or hostile same-user process is absent.

GuaranteeLimit
Ravel returns a result only after that launch’s child reap, confirmed group absence and successful required cleanupGroup membership defines the cleanup scope. A descendant that leaves the group lies outside it
Ravel supervises shutdown while the Ravel CLI remains aliveAbrupt death of the Ravel CLI, for example from KILL, can orphan the managed job or a harness run. Such death is not successful completion
Ravel keeps the first complete result through a private inherited channel and rejects invalid result filesThe channel does not authenticate against hostile same-user code. Directory identity checks do not make concurrent pathname changes atomic
Ravel asks the runner to stop each harness run before it stops the worker, and launches nothing after a stopA process that leaves the run’s group lies outside this stop. A run that the runner does not settle in 8 seconds gives harness_cleanup_failed, without a confirmed reap. A stop of an LLM call ends only local work: the model service can still finish a request that it accepted
Ravel verifies and reaps the Rhai worker before the managed launchThe configuration script remains trusted executable code. The worker starts no process. Its harness runs have the group limit above
A signal that the Ravel CLI consumes before its final read wins over the deadline, a breached limit and a cleanup failureThe first consumed signal stays the cause. A later signal does not replace it
Ravel checks terminal recovery under the holder rules aboveFailed-spawn and unknown-status handback require exclusive foreground authority and no unrelated group-ID reuse. Terminal tests cannot establish these conditions for a caller
Ravel retains failure reports and attempts required cleanupA failed operation or expired retry bound can leave cleanup unconfirmed and processes alive. Ravel reports failure and returns no result
Cancellation observed by the final decision suppresses the resultA new signal can arrive after that decision. There is no atomic operation across signal delivery and the final output write
Successful cleanup permits the final result writeThe write can still fail after a prefix reaches standard output. Ravel cannot retract that prefix. A failed write to standard error can also prevent the delivery of diagnostics
The Ravel CLI owns one session and then exitsProcess-wide handlers, signal latches and terminal authority do not support concurrent host calls or a fully restored host that continues work

Native-entry behaviour has measured support on aarch64 macOS and aarch64 GNU/Linux. Each measurement covers its named kernel, libc and compiler, not every combination. The GNU/Linux measurement used Rust 1.85.0. Native x86-64 Linux remains unqualified: builds, lint checks and QEMU user-mode execution do not qualify an x86-64 kernel.

The current runner is a private package within Ravel. Neither that package boundary nor these checks deliver a persistent runner for an arbitrary host.

Ravel supports no separate component. The runner serves the Ravel CLI, which supervises one session and then exits. The runner’s return does not restore process-wide signal state. Another program invokes the Ravel CLI as a child process. Thus, the signal state that the runner changes stays in that child process and ends when it exits.

Develop Ravel

Suppose that you change how Ravel returns a result. You want to be sure that a result with spaces at its ends reaches the caller with those spaces. In this chapter, you prepare a checkout and build Ravel. Then you find the code, choose where a test observes the rule, add the test and run the checks before you hand the change over. The same steps apply to every change to Ravel.

The tests run real processes. They use temporary configuration scripts, Rhai files that select programs, and fake programs that stand in for harnesses. A harness is an agent program, such as Claude Code or Codex, in which an LLM works. The tests need no model account, no personal configuration and no other project checkout.

Chapter 7 explains the parts of Ravel and why each part exists. Chapter 8 follows a session through the code that a change can affect.

Ravel supports no separate component. Its private runner serves the Ravel CLI, which supervises one session and then exits. The runner’s return does not restore process-wide signal state. A program that needs supervised harness sessions invokes the Ravel CLI as a child process. This chapter teaches changes to Ravel itself.

Ravel 1.x evaluated configuration scripts in a TypeScript worker. The checkout built that worker with Bun, typechecked its SDK and installed it beside the CLI. Ravel 2.0.0 removed the worker, its SDK, its build and its typecheck. The Rhai engine is now a Rust dependency inside the one executable, bin/ravel. Bun stays in the checkout only as a tool for two book checks.

Links to files outside the book open repository sources on GitHub. Before a change reaches main, read its evidence and convention files from the checkout.

Prepare a checkout

A checkout needs tools that an installed Ravel does not need. The build uses Rust 1.99.1 and a C linker. On macOS, the Command Line Tools supply the linker. On Linux, the system development tools supply it. The table shows which part of the work needs each tool.

PrerequisitePurpose
rustup, with Rust 1.99.1, rustfmt and ClippyCompile, format and lint the Rust workspace
TaskRun the recipes in Taskfile.yml
cargo-nextestList and run the Rust tests
ShellCheckCheck the shell scripts during task check
Bash, curl, and tar with gzip and xzRun the scripts and unpack the downloaded book tools
shasum or sha256sumCompare each download with its committed SHA-256 pin
GitObtain the checkout, and let scripts read the tracked files

The file rust-toolchain.toml at the root of the checkout selects Rust 1.99.1, with rustfmt, Clippy and the three release target libraries. When rustup’s tools are first on PATH, cargo in the checkout uses that toolchain. rustup toolchain install, run in the checkout, installs that toolchain when rustup does not have it.

  1. Obtain a checkout of the Ravel repository.
  2. Use cd to enter its root directory, where Taskfile.yml is.
  3. Run rustup toolchain install.
  4. Run task setup.
  5. Run task build.

Fragment from the checkout trial for this chapter: these commands omit tool installation and the command output.

rustup toolchain install
task setup
task build

task setup needs network access on its first run. It downloads the book tools into target/tools. It does not install Rust, Task, ShellCheck or nextest. The table gives each download, its pin and the work that needs it.

DownloadPinWork that needs it
Bun1.4.2task docs:ste and task docs:breaks, which run scripts/ste.ts and scripts/breaks.ts
mdBook0.5.4The book build, task docs:book and the release book
Typst0.15.1The figure render, task docs:figures
CeTZ and its dependency oxifmt0.4.2 and 1.0.0The figure render
DejaVu fonts2.37The text of each figure

The scripts compare each archive with its committed SHA-256 digest before use. The figure setup also checks the package trees and the font files. A bad archive in the cache causes another download. A fresh download with the wrong digest stops setup.

Setup has tool pins for macOS arm64, Linux aarch64 and Linux x86_64. Native x86_64 Linux remains unqualified. The checkout trial below covers only macOS arm64.

The Bun of the checkout is a development tool for the book only. No build of Ravel uses it, and no installation or archive holds it. The Ravel CLI and its Rhai worker need no Bun or Node at run time.

Build the executable

task build runs cargo build --locked --workspace. Cargo builds both Rust packages and writes the executable to target/debug/ravel, with the default Cargo target directory. The Rhai worker is not a separate build. The Ravel CLI starts another copy of target/debug/ravel in an internal role to evaluate a configuration script.

Cargo.lock pins every Rust dependency. crates/ravel/Cargo.toml pins the Rhai engine exactly, at version 1.27.0. A first build can need network access for the dependencies that the Cargo cache on the host does not hold.

The build script crates/ravel/build.rs puts a source identity into the executable. That identity is a digest of the Rust sources of both packages, the package manifest, the build script and Cargo.lock. The worker’s hello holds the identity, and the Ravel CLI compares it with its own. Both roles come from one build, so a change to the worker always agrees with its CLI. Chapter 7 explains this check.

TaskUse it whenOutput or check
task buildYou change Rust sourcetarget/debug/ravel and the runner library
task checkYou hand a change overFormat, lint, script checks and all Rust tests
task rhai:upgrade-proofYou change the Rhai pinThe engine tests and the hostile classes through the CLI
task noticesYou change a dependencyLicence notices for the release graph, under target/notices/

task notices installs cargo-about 0.8.4 into the checkout on its first run, through task notices:setup. It refuses a dependency whose licence is not on the approved list. task install and the release builds run it, because each installation carries the notice file.

Find the source for a change

The workspace has two Rust packages. ravel holds the CLI, the Rhai worker role and the result channel. keyed-launch holds the runner. The package boundary does not promise a separately supported component. The table maps each kind of source and test to its directory.

Path from the checkout rootWhat a contributor finds there
crates/ravel/src/CLI arguments, configuration script checks, the result channel and the choice of the final outcome
crates/ravel/src/rhai_worker/The Rhai worker role and the CLI side of the host bridge
crates/ravel/src/skills/Skill source resolution, the default skill directory and the preamble that Ravel adds to the prompt
crates/ravel/examples/The Rhai examples that an installation copies to share/ravel/examples/
crates/ravel/build.rsThe source identity of the executable
crates/keyed-launch/src/Process groups, signals, waits, terminal recovery and the completion channel
crates/ravel/tests/Tests through the real ravel executable, with fake programs
crates/keyed-launch/tests/Runner tests with real processes and pseudo-terminals
scripts/ and Taskfile.ymlReusable checks, book tools and delivery recipes

Chapter 7, “Find each part in the source” gives each part with the process where it runs. In the example of this chapter, the result goes through the result channel. end-session writes the complete result file in one atomic step, and the CLI reads it after cleanup. Tests with real subprocesses check nested session-result context, cancellation, process cleanup, and empty and multiline results. Thus, a change to this boundary needs more than a test of a parser.

Choose a test seam

A test seam is the boundary at which a test supplies inputs and observes behaviour. Choose the nearest seam that can observe the behaviour of your change. For the spaces of a result, a test of the argument parser is too near. It cannot see what end-session writes or what the caller receives. The command boundary can see both.

The table connects each behaviour to a seam and a focused command.

Behaviour to checkSeam and sourceFocused command
A local conversion, parser or decisionRust unit tests beside the CLI codetask test:session -- --bin ravel
A result, refusal or managed launch through the executable crates/ravel/tests/cli.rs, with temporary configuration scripts and fixture processestask test:cli
A configuration script, its settings, helpers, reads or harness runscrates/ravel/tests/rhai_*.rs, through the real ravel start-sessiontask test:session -- --test rhai_config
An LLM call in a configuration scriptcrates/ravel/tests/rhai_llm_calls.rs, with a fake harness that plays the modeltask test:session -- --test rhai_llm_calls
Skill source resolution and deliverycrates/ravel/tests/skills_*.rs, with local source trees and fake programstask test:session -- --test skills_launch
Hostile startup files, module shadows and diagnosticscrates/ravel/tests/hostile.rstask test:session -- --test hostile
Group cleanup, escalation or child exit statusThe runner, with real subprocesses under crates/keyed-launch/tests/task test:runner
Terminal foreground or modesThe linked runner in a fixture process on a private pseudo-terminal, in tests/job.rstask test:runner -- --test job
All tests in the Ravel packageUnit tests and every test file of crates/ravel/tests/task test:session
Both Rust packagesWorkspace tests under nextesttask test

The test tasks build what they need. Cargo builds the ravel executable before the integration tests of its package, and each test starts that executable. Thus, a test always uses the worker of the same build. The tests need no host Bun or Node, no model account and no installed Ravel.

A configuration script can ask a model through a harness run, as chapter 4 teaches. The tests never make a real call. In rhai_llm_calls.rs, a fake harness on the private PATH plays the model. It prints a fixed answer and records its arguments and its standard input. The test then reads which route Ravel launched and what the fake received. Each case has a control: the same script with another answer must give the other route.

A real call is a separate, opt-in check. task harness:smoke:claude and task harness:smoke:codex make one real call through Claude Code or Codex with the owner’s sign-in, and that call spends subscription usage. No default task runs them. Chapter 4 explains them. task check runs only task harness:smoke:test, which tests the smoke script against fake harnesses.

When you change the Rhai pin, run task rhai:upgrade-proof. It prints the pin and checks that Cargo.lock builds the same version. Then it runs each rhai_* test target, in a fixed order. Last, it runs each hostile class of rhai_limits.rs, such as an endless loop, a deep recursion and a full heap. It fails when a rhai_* file is not on its list, or when a target runs no test.

The runner’s terminal tests create a pseudo-terminal, or PTY. This private terminal lets a test observe foreground ownership and modes without a human terminal. The in_pty helper in crates/keyed-launch/tests/job.rs starts the command as the leader of a new POSIX session. It reads the terminal output all the time, so a full terminal buffer cannot stop the leader. At its deadline, it kills the leader’s process group and fails the test.

Tests that change process-wide signal state use a separate process. The job tests run their own binary in a fixture role, a test function marked #[ignore]. A parent test invokes that role with its required environment. The fixture role is not a check when it runs alone. Chapter 9 explains the terminal behaviour that these tests observe.

task test:list lists the cases before a run. The focused tasks pass the arguments after -- to nextest. A package selector chooses code, --test chooses an integration-test binary, and -E chooses test names with a filter expression.

Fragment from the checkout trial: list first, then run the added tests. The fragment omits their source and the nextest output.

task test:list -- -E 'test(book_)'
task test:runner -- -E 'test(book_)'
task test:cli -- -E 'test(book_)'
task test:session -- --test rhai_llm_calls -E 'test(book_)'
task test:session -- --bin ravel -E 'test(book_)'

The book_ cases are the additions in Add a test. They exist only after you add them. A narrow filter can exclude related cases. Read both counts in the nextest summary. The passed count gives the cases that ran and passed. The skipped count includes the cases that an -E filter excluded.

While you edit, run the focused checks. Before handoff, run the full suite.

Run the checks before handoff

task check runs scripts/check.sh. Each stage prints one line with its result. The script stops at the first stage that fails. Its last stage runs the workspace tests with nextest and shows their report. The earlier stages run in this order:

StageWork
formattingcargo fmt --all --check checks the Rust format and changes no file
scriptsShellCheck checks the shell scripts under scripts/
book-bun-integrityOffline fixtures check the book Bun’s download, cache recovery and the refusal of a corrupt download
harness-smoke-fakesThe harness smoke script runs against fake harnesses and makes no real call
installed-recordControls check the script that compares a Homebrew installation record
lintClippy checks the workspace and all targets with the committed lockfile
terminal-recoveryRunner tests produce real-PTY receipts, then a CLI unit test reads them

task test:terminal-recovery runs the last of these checks alone.

The workspace configuration sets Clippy’s all lint group to deny. Thus, a Clippy report from that group fails the lint stage. When a stage fails, scripts/check.sh names its log and shows the last 60 lines.

Each run of task check writes a new target/check.* log. The log holds the output of the earlier stages and the nextest report. The terminal-recovery check creates temporary receipts and removes them when its script exits. The format stage does not change source files.

task check does not build the book or run its example, excerpt, figure or prose checks. It also does not install Ravel or test release archives. Those workflows have their own tasks. The workspace test run uses nextest’s default selection. Thus, a pass does not mean that every ignored fixture or native measurement ran.

Fragment from the checkout trial: run the full check after the focused checks. This fragment omits the output and the log paths, which change between runs.

task check

The checkout trial record gives the source revision, tool versions, commands and results. It describes one macOS machine with cached tools, not a clean-machine installation.

Add a test

The following fragments come from tests added to a scratch copy of the checkout for this chapter. They use the helpers and imports of the named files. All five passed on macOS arm64. They show current behaviour and do not change the product.

For a defect, first add an assertion that fails because of that defect. Then change the code and run the same test again. A test that passes before the code change cannot tell the old behaviour from the new behaviour.

Test the command boundary

This is the test for the example of this chapter. In crates/ravel/tests/cli.rs, Fixture gives the executable an empty environment with a private HOME, TMPDIR and working directory. Its PATH contains /usr/bin:/bin. The selecting helper writes a Rhai configuration script that selects /bin/sh with a script of your choice. The real Rhai worker evaluates that configuration script.

Fragment from the scratch copy of crates/ravel/tests/cli.rs: this test omits the imports and the Fixture definition.

#![allow(unused)]
fn main() {
#[test]
fn book_result_keeps_spaces() {
    let fixture = Fixture::new();
    let config = fixture.selecting(r#""$1" end-session -- "$2""#, "  done  ");
    let output = fixture.start(&config, "Check spaces.");

    assert!(
        output.status.success(),
        "{}",
        String::from_utf8_lossy(&output.stderr)
    );
    assert_eq!(output.stdout, b"  done  ");
    assert_eq!(fs::read_dir(fixture.root.join("tmp")).unwrap().count(), 0);
}
}

The helper gives the shell the built Ravel executable as $1 and the result as $2. The shell sends the result with end-session. The assertions check success, the unchanged spaces and the cleanup of the fixture’s temporary directory. A test of the parser alone cannot check this path through both ravel processes.

Test an LLM call with a fake model

You may want to check that a configuration script launches the route that a model’s answer chooses. In crates/ravel/tests/rhai_llm_calls.rs, SELECTION is a configuration script that asks the harness classifier to classify the prompt. It maps each answer to a route. fake_model writes a fake classifier on the private PATH, and settings names it as a harness.

Fragment from the scratch copy of crates/ravel/tests/rhai_llm_calls.rs: this test omits the file’s imports, constants and helpers.

#![allow(unused)]
fn main() {
#[test]
fn book_fake_model_answer_chooses_the_route() {
    let sandbox = Sandbox::new();
    let recorded = sandbox.recording_agent();
    fake_model(&sandbox, "classifier", &format!("echo {LIGHT}"));
    settings(&sandbox, &["classifier"], 1);
    sandbox.personal_rhai(&rhai_script(SELECTION));

    let notice = notice_of(&start(&sandbox, PROMPT, &[], &[]));

    assert_eq!(recorded.argv().unwrap(), ["routed", "quick-route"]);
    assert_runs(&notice, &[("classifier", "exited")]);
}
}

The fake model prints the light answer, so Ravel launches quick-route. The routing notice lists one harness run of classifier. A copy of the test that expected deep-route failed. Thus, the route comes from the answer and not from a default. The file’s own test of this script also checks the other answer.

Test a real child process

In crates/keyed-launch/tests/launch.rs, the test type Harness writes a shell script in a temporary directory. This type is not an agent harness. The launch helper builds the runner request. support::controls() gives the names of the inherited enclosing-session controls, and the runner removes them from the child. Keep tests separate from live sessions lists these controls.

Fragment from the scratch copy of crates/keyed-launch/tests/launch.rs: this test omits the file’s imports and helper definitions.

#![allow(unused)]
fn main() {
#[test]
fn book_child_exit_keeps_its_status() {
    let harness = Harness::new();
    let script = harness.script("exit 23\n");
    let argv = harness.argv(&script);
    let channel = Channel::allocate(&harness.control()).unwrap();

    let ended = run(launch(&argv, &channel, support::controls(), None)).unwrap();

    assert_eq!(ended.status.code(), Some(23));
    assert_eq!(ended.end, End::Exited);
    assert!(!ended.signalled);
}
}

The child exits with status 23 without a completion signal. The assertions separate the exit status, the reason that supervision ended and the channel state. This test needs no PTY, because it makes no claim about a terminal.

For a claim about a terminal, the helper in job.rs supplies the PTY. The launcher role reports its foreground group before and after the child runs.

Fragment from the scratch copy of crates/keyed-launch/tests/job.rs: this test omits the imports, Scratch, the launcher role and the PTY helper.

#![allow(unused)]
fn main() {
#[test]
fn book_normal_exit_restores_foreground() {
    let scratch = Scratch::new();
    let child = scratch.script("exit 23\n");
    let (status, output) = in_pty(
        role_command(
            "launcher",
            &[("JOB_CHILD", &child), ("JOB_REPORT", &scratch.os("report"))],
        ),
        Duration::from_secs(20),
        |_| {},
    );
    assert!(status.success(), "{status:?}\n{output}");
    let report = report(&scratch.path("report"));
    assert_eq!(report["code"], "23");
    assert_eq!(report["before_foreground"], report["group"]);
    assert_eq!(report["after_foreground"], report["group"]);
}
}

The parent checks that the launcher completed its report. Then it reads the child’s status from that report. The two statuses belong to different processes. The table separates those observations in this example.

ProcessWork and observation in this example
Parent testStarts the launcher role through in_pty. Checks the role’s exit status, then reads the report
Launcher roleRuns the same test binary in a separate process on the private PTY. Its linked runner supervises the child. Reports the foreground values and the child’s status
ChildRuns the shell script that exits with status 23. The report’s code field holds this status

This test checks the final foreground ownership. It does not check recovery after a change of modes. The raw-mode test near it changes the modes and checks that the change occurred before it checks recovery.

Test a local rule

A CLI unit test can check the argument parser without a worker or a child process. The separator -- makes a result such as --help into data.

Fragment from the scratch copy of crates/ravel/src/cli.rs: add this test module at file scope. The fragment omits the argument definitions above it.

#![allow(unused)]
fn main() {
#[cfg(test)]
mod tests {
    use super::*;

    #[test]
    fn book_result_after_separator_is_data() {
        let cli = Cli::try_parse_from(["ravel", "end-session", "--", "--help"]).unwrap();
        let Command::EndSession { result } = cli.command else {
            panic!("expected end-session");
        };
        assert_eq!(result, "--help");
    }
}
}

The parser returns Command::EndSession with the unchanged value. This test does not run end-session or need a result channel. The tests at the command boundary cover the later effect of that command.

Install and test the installed files

task install builds a release executable for the host and generates the licence notices. It copies Ravel’s files into PREFIX, then tests that installation. The default prefix is ~/.local. With an explicit prefix, you can test a build and keep your usual installation.

  1. Choose a disposable prefix for the trial.
  2. Run task install with that prefix.
  3. Read the last line, which names the installed version and the prefix.

Fragment from this chapter’s macOS arm64 trial: these commands omit the output and the removal of the disposable prefix.

task install PREFIX=target/install-trial
task smoke

The installation puts the executable at PREFIX/bin/ravel, the three Rhai examples under PREFIX/share/ravel/examples/ and the notice file under PREFIX/share/ravel/notices/. It removes an old PREFIX/libexec/ravel directory, which held the Ravel 1.x worker and SDK. It does not add the prefix to PATH, and it writes no installation record. Only a Homebrew formula writes share/ravel/installation.json, because only an installation by Homebrew knows the shared skill directory. Chapter 6 describes the installed files. Chapter 11, “Deliver Ravel”, covers release archives, their target checks and the formula.

The table separates the installed files from temporary test files and build outputs.

TaskWhat it builds or checksFiles it leaves
task install PREFIX=…Release executable and notices, then the installed smoke testInstalled files at the chosen prefix, plus build outputs
task smokeThe same installation in a fresh prefix under target/, whose name contains a space and a non-ASCII letterBuild outputs. The script removes its temporary prefix when it exits
task smoke:pair OLD=… NEW=…On macOS, the installed Ravel 1.1 executable OLD and the installation under NEW at the same time. Each has its own temporary home, and a guard denies both runs the real homeNothing. The script removes its temporary homes when it exits

Before task smoke installs, it puts an old libexec/ravel/sdk/ file and an unrelated bin/other-tool into its fresh prefix. After the installation, the old Ravel 1.x payload is gone and the unrelated tool is unchanged. Thus, the check shows what an upgrade over an old installation removes and keeps.

The installed smoke test, scripts/installed-smoke.sh, starts with env -i. It tests both the direct path to the executable and a relative symlink from another prefix, as a package manager links an installation. Its private PATH has selected system utilities and fixture programs. It refuses to run if that PATH finds bun, node or a Grove command. It checks:

  • the version line of the executable.
  • that no libexec/ravel and no installation record exist.
  • the installed examples and the notice file.
  • ordinary, empty, multiline and option-like results, which return unchanged.
  • child output, which goes to diagnostics and not to the result.
  • relative Rhai imports, measured reads, selection context and --param values.
  • the refusal of a policy.ts entry as config_format_retired, with status 3.
  • end-session without session-result context, which fails.
  • the cleanup of temporary session files.

These tasks passed on macOS 27.0.1 arm64 in this chapter’s trial. This local smoke check does not qualify release archives, Linux floors or a different host.

Check the book

Book checks are separate from task check. A source change can make a quoted excerpt stale even when every Rust test passes. A change to the book can break a command example without a change to product code.

task docs:book first builds the book, then serves it at http://127.0.0.1:8775. The server builds the book again after a source change. PORT=… selects another port. task docs:book:build only builds, under target/book. Its script, scripts/book.sh, fails on an mdBook ERROR or WARN diagnostic, even if mdBook exits with status 0. A successful build does not show that every link resolves or that a figure is legible.

The table matches each kind of book change to the check that can find a related defect.

ChangeCheckWhat the check shows
Chapter or book configurationtask docs:book:buildmdBook builds without an ERROR or WARN diagnostic
Figure source or shared figure moduletask docs:figuresFresh SVGs and layout records match the committed files
Complete command exampletask docs:examplesThe built executable runs each complete example and meets its stated output and status
Source excerpt or its source filetask docs:excerptsEach excerpt still occurs as consecutive whole lines in its named tracked file
Prose, table, heading or figure texttask docs:ste -- --strictNo violation of the STE rules that a script can check, or of the shared word decisions
Chapter layouttask docs:breaks -- --strictNo prose run is longer than 450 words under the check’s rules

The figure checks use the pinned Typst, packages and fonts from task setup. They render into a scratch directory. task docs:figures:update instead writes the SVGs and .layout.json files beside their .typ sources. After an intended figure change, run the update task, read the diff and view the built page. Then run the comparison again.

The figure convention describes the shared modules and the visual checks. Equality with a fresh render does not show that a diagram explains the right behaviour.

The example check builds Ravel first. It evaluates real Rhai configuration scripts and runs fake programs with a private home, temporary directory and PATH. Standard input is /dev/null. It compares the declared streams and exit status, with only the scratch path normalised. Every complete example runs on every platform. An <!-- example: ... --> comment is a page error, because the convention has no directive.

Fragments do not run. The example check also does not compare source excerpts. The example convention defines the labels and the output claims.

The excerpt check needs no build. Its comparison keeps spaces and indentation. It refuses untracked sources, symbolic links and generated paths. An exact quotation can still have an incorrect explanation. The excerpt convention describes the label and the comparison.

The STE and prose-break tasks report problems without failure, unless you pass --strict. They run on the book Bun from task setup. By default, the STE check examines all chapter files, SUMMARY.md, CONTEXT.md, README.md and the text of each figure layout record. The prose-break check examines the chapters. A table, figure or code block breaks a prose run. Headings and lists do not.

Neither check can show clear explanations or agreement with every STE rule. A human read still checks meaning, order and the use of active verbs.

The checkers have their own tests. A control gives a checker a known bad input and requires it to refuse that input. A mutant removes a safeguard from a copy of the checker. A control then must fail against that copy. These tests help show that a clean report has a useful meaning. The table names the controls to run after a change to a checker.

TaskControls it runs
docs:figures:testUnchanged figures, and changes to sources, SVGs, records, layout pins, tools and packages
docs:examples:testGood pages, and wrong output, status, labels, build identity, environment and other example rules
docs:examples:mutantsCopies of the example checker with one safeguard removed each
docs:excerpts:testChanged source or quotation, invalid paths and labels, then checker mutants
docs:ste:testPlanted text violations and the default file selection, then rule mutants
docs:breaks:testProse at and above the limit, each kind of break, and rule mutants
book:integrityOffline mdBook download fixtures, cache reuse, recovery from a corrupt cache and refusal of a bad download
book:bun:integrityOffline book Bun download fixtures, cache recovery and refusal of a corrupt download

book:integrity needs the archive that task setup checked. It replaces the download boundary and uses private tool directories. It does not test the availability of GitHub or the book’s content. Run a checker’s controls when you change that checker. Run the related content checks when you change a chapter.

Fragment from this chapter’s final validation: these commands omit setup and the reports. They check the complete book, not only this chapter.

task docs:book:build
task docs:figures
task docs:examples
task docs:excerpts
task docs:ste -- --strict
task docs:breaks -- --strict

Keep tests separate from live sessions

A test can run inside an LLM session that Ravel supervises. If a test inherits that session’s real result path, its end-session call can stop the developer’s session. Thus, each test boundary removes the inherited authority before it creates its own fixture inputs. The table maps each test boundary to its guard and the source that applies it.

BoundaryGuardSource
Cargo commandsForce the listed session controls to empty values.cargo/config.toml
Runner subprocess helpersRemove those names from commands, or pass the names as runner scrub controlscrates/keyed-launch/tests/support/mod.rs
CLI, Rhai, skill and hostile fixturesUse env_clear(), a private HOME and TMPDIR, and a fixture PATHcrates/ravel/tests/cli.rs and crates/ravel/tests/support/
Installed smokeStart with env -i, then supply only fixture inputsscripts/install.sh and scripts/installed-smoke.sh

The Cargo guards cover these names:

  • GROVE_LAUNCH_DIR, GROVE_SIGNAL_FILE and GROVE_RUN_SIGNAL_FILE.
  • HARNESS_DISPATCH_EXIT_FILE, HARNESS_DISPATCH_RUN_ID and HARNESS_DISPATCH_STATE_DIR.
  • RAVEL_EXIT_FILE, RAVEL_RUN_ID and RAVEL_STATE_DIR.

An empty value disables the inherited authority. It does not send the authority to another path. The runner helpers also protect direct subprocess invocations that do not go through Cargo. The CLI fixtures create temporary configuration scripts, so tests do not need the developer’s personal configuration script.

The GROVE_* and HARNESS_DISPATCH_* names stay in these guards on purpose. They protect tests from older enclosing-session controls. They are safety exceptions that remain from the extraction of Ravel, not product dependencies. Paths in source provenance and the retired-project record give historical attribution. Ravel does not load those projects or interpret their task trees. The caller-reference inventory classifies these references in source files and dotfiles.

For a new subprocess test, use the fixture of the chosen seam again. Supply only the environment values that the case needs. If the case needs a result channel, create that channel inside its temporary fixture. Never supply the enclosing session’s real result path.

Read the evidence for a change

An evidence note explains what a particular source read or measured run shows. Evidence notes are outside the book, with their receipts and logs. The tasks produce check output. A contributor writes the note that connects that output to a claim and states its limits. A task does not change a dated evidence note. The table connects each question to a note and the source of its observations.

Question about a changeEvidence to readSource of its observations
Which observations support the skill directory contract and the rule for a package of a default source?Skill discovery design evidenceSource reads, local trials and the checks of each stage of the 2.0.0 line
Did a particular release reach its remote and installed destinations?The release evidence that the release task writes, target/release-evidence/vX.Y.Z.json, and the release and installation recordThe release tasks, download comparisons and installed Homebrew checks
Does an archive contain and run the required files?Archive evidencerelease:smoke, release:test and release:formula, at the revision named in the note
Does the release procedure refuse an unsafe cut or resume the right step?Release lifecycle evidencerelease:lifecycle:test and the archive and formula checks
Which resource and failure paths motivated the later repairs? Result/job source evidenceA source read at the revision named in the note, not a runtime task
Which checks distinguish the completion-failure repairs?Completion failure evidenceFocused runner and CLI tests, test:terminal-recovery and check, with their recorded controls
Why does native entry follow this protocol?Native-entry designSource and design analysis, with later observations linked separately
What did the native-entry instruments measure?Native-entry evidenceThe current native-entry and Linux checks, and archived campaign receipts
What did the TypeScript worker of Ravel 1.x establish?Runtime evidence and component evidenceHistorical runs. Each note says that its worker, SDK and Bun controls are retired
Where are the run records?Evidence directoryThe linked note names each receipt set, its command and its source revision

Start with the note’s source revision and measured platform. Then read the section that matches the claim. Earlier sections can describe work that later sections complete. A design proposal is not an observed run. An old failure does not show that the current source still fails, and an old pass does not show that it still passes.

The current native-entry instruments examine the state of the executable at entry and its cleanup. The archived campaign compared a frozen product from before the repair, the incumbent, with the repaired product. Its drivers and the protected-test comparison, R01, were retired in the change to Rhai. The historical campaign records keep their measured source revisions, plans and receipts. Those observations do not show that the current source has the same behaviour. The table below names the available instruments.

InstrumentTasks, purpose and evidence
Native entrynative-entry:build
native-entry:front

test:native-entry
Build the private fixture. Run the CLI cases, the runner cases and the start_check cases for the runner’s public API. Read the development sections of the native-entry note
Linuxnative-linux:image
test:native-linux
Build the Linux environment and run the current Rust, Rhai and native-entry checks, with unchanged source digests. Earlier target measurements remain in the Linux campaign section

The Linux tasks call scripts/native-linux.sh to stage and check the files of the current checkout without the host’s Git objects. The native-entry tasks build and select Rust tests directly. A development test pass alone does not show the complete campaign or the support of a new platform. The measured support covers aarch64 macOS and aarch64 Linux. x86_64 Linux is built and run only under emulation, and native qualification is still absent.

Three tasks serve editable design views on localhost: task docs:result-job on port 8774, task docs:rhai-configuration on port 8776 and task docs:skills on port 8778. Each accepts PORT=… and needs network access for Mermaid. These design views are separate from the book’s Typst figures and show no runtime result.

The retired-project record names the former actor-daemon revision for historical attribution only.

Find a development task

task --list shows the public task names and short descriptions in the current checkout. The table maps each name at this chapter’s draft to its explanation. The sections above explain the development tasks. Chapter 11, “Deliver Ravel”, owns the release workflow. Its release tasks can create a release version, write remote state and replace an installation. Thus, they are not commands for a chapter check.
